biology.bigb
= Biology
{wiki}

<Biology> studies living systems: how <biological cells> maintain themselves, how <organisms> develop and reproduce, and how <evolution> generates diversity. Its scales connect <molecules>, <genes>, <biological cells>, <organisms> and the interactions studied by <ecology>. These are complementary viewpoints: a <protein>'s chemistry can explain a cellular mechanism, while <evolution> explains why inherited variants of that mechanism persist. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/1-1-the-science-of-biology[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: the science of biology].

Two organizing ideas make this immense subject navigable: shared cellular organization and descent with inherited modification. A <neuron> and a <gliding bacterium> differ dramatically in shape and behavior, yet both depend on a <cell membrane> and <metabolism>. Their differences become intelligible through specialization and <evolution>.

Keep observations separate from the <mathematical models> used to explain them. A measured <membrane potential> is evidence; an idealized <two-state ion-channel gating> equation proposes a mechanism. A <mathematical model> earns trust through predictions and comparisons with measurements. Its assumptions delimit its usefulness, and disagreement can reveal missing biology rather than a faulty measurement.

\Include[biology-author]
\Include[biology-bibliography]

= Intracellular transport
{parent=Biology}

= Diffusion and capture
{parent=Intracellular transport}

A localization mechanism in which nondirected movement brings material to a selectively retaining destination. Localization does not by itself establish motor-driven transport along the entire route.

= Vesicle transport
{parent=Intracellular transport}

= Vesicle fusion
{parent=Vesicle transport}

= Secretory vesicle
{parent=Vesicle transport}

= Population heterogeneity
{parent=Biology}

Variation among <biological cells> in states such as cell-cycle stage, expression level and responsiveness. Population averages can agree even when individual-cell histories differ.

= Biotechnology
{parent=Biology}

Application of biological systems or their components to produce useful materials, measurements or processes.

= Photosynthetic bioproduction
{parent=Biotechnology}

Production of useful molecules in a photosynthetic host by directing fixed carbon, light-derived energy and reducing equivalents into biosynthetic pathways.

= Biophotovoltaic cell
{parent=Photosynthetic bioproduction}

A device harvesting electrical current from reducing equivalents generated or processed by photosynthetic organisms.

= Structural biology
{parent=Biology}

= Protein Data Bank
{c}
{parent=Structural biology}

= Systems biology
{parent=Biology}

A field studying how interactions among biological components generate system-level behavior, using quantitative measurements, perturbations and <mathematical models>.

= Whole-organism modeling
{parent=Systems biology}

Computational integration of an organism’s interacting physiological and developmental processes at a stated resolution and for a stated range of conditions.

= OpenWorm
{c}
{parent=Whole-organism modeling}

= Whole-cell modeling
{parent=Systems biology}

Integration of multiple cellular processes into a computational model of a cell. The label describes the intended scope; it does not mean that every molecular event is known or individually simulated.

= Multiscale modeling
{parent=Systems biology}

Coupling models at different spatial or temporal resolutions while making their interfaces, units and conservation requirements explicit.

= Tissue polarity
{parent=Biology}

= Iron homeostasis
{parent=Biology}

Control of <iron> uptake, storage, utilization and release. <post-transcriptional regulation> can coordinate these processes without requiring all regulated transcripts to change in the same direction.

= Iron regulatory protein
{parent=Iron homeostasis}

= IRP
{synonym}
{c}

A <protein> that binds appropriate <iron-responsive elements> depending on cellular state. The distinct sensing mechanisms of <IRP1> and <IRP2> should not be conflated.

= IRP2
{parent=Iron regulatory protein}
{c}

An iron-responsive <RNA-binding protein> whose abundance is controlled through regulated degradation, including by <FBXL5>. It does not use the <aconitase> switch of <IRP1>.

= IRP1
{parent=Iron regulatory protein}
{c}

A bifunctional <protein> that acts as cytosolic <aconitase> with an appropriate iron–sulfur cluster and as an iron-responsive <RNA-binding protein> when converted to its RNA-binding state.<Cellular regulation of the iron-responsive element binding protein: disassembly of the cubane iron-sulfur cluster results in high-affinity RNA binding by David J. Haile and others>[Biochemical cluster disassembly]{ref}

= Microbiology
{parent=Biology}

= Siderophore
{parent=Microbiology}

A molecule involved in iron acquisition. Secreted forms can generate social effects when other cells can use the bound iron.

= Lipopolysaccharide
{parent=Microbiology}

A component of the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria used in experimental <immune challenges>. It induces host responses without constituting a replicating <infection>.

= Gnotobiotic experiment
{parent=Microbiology}

An experiment using hosts with a defined microbial status. Sterility or known community composition must be verified rather than assumed after treatment.

= Microbial inoculation
{parent=Microbiology}

Introduction of a specified microbial preparation into a recipient or environment. Dose, viability and medium controls affect interpretation.

= Vertical transmission of microorganisms
{parent=Microbiology}

Transfer of microorganisms between parent and offspring. Evidence of transfer does not by itself demonstrate host benefit or genetic determination of the transfer.

= Microbiota
{parent=Microbiology}

The microorganisms occurring in a defined site or environment.

= Microbiome
{parent=Microbiology}

The microbial community and associated genetic or functional information in a specified environment; the exact operational definition should be stated.

= Animal behaviour
{parent=Biology}

= Animal behavior
{synonym}

The actions and responses of <animals>, studied through their mechanisms, development, functions and evolutionary history.

= Scavenging
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Consumption of remains of dead organisms. It can remove, disarticulate and transport material before burial.

= Locomotion
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Movement of an organism through its environment, including walking, swimming and <saltation>.

= Saltation
{parent=Locomotion}

<locomotion> by jumps. Skeletal geometry can constrain jumping capability but does not establish the exclusive behaviour of an extinct organism.

= State-dependent behaviour
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Behaviour whose expression depends on properties such as condition, age, reproductive state or current social position.

= Social decision rule
{parent=Animal behaviour}

A mapping from perceived social information and internal state to action. A stable mapping can generate flexible actions when its inputs change.

= Sleep
{parent=Animal behaviour}

A regulated behavioural and physiological state identified through converging criteria including reversibility, responsiveness and homeostatic regulation. Immobility alone does not establish <sleep>.

= Foraging
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Searching for, acquiring and handling food. Tests distinguish resource-specific actions from general movement or appetite.

= Grooming
{parent=Animal behaviour}

= Sickness behaviour
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Coordinated changes in activity, appetite and social or other motivations associated with immune activation. Context dependence and experimental controls distinguish it from simple incapacity.

= Self-medication in animals
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Use of substances or actions that reduce harm from <parasites> or <disease>. Tests require a health benefit, a relevant behavioural response and controls against ordinary nutritional preference.

= Transgenerational medication
{parent=Self-medication in animals}

Parental use or selection of substances that reduce <disease> in offspring. A benefit to offspring does not imply treatment or cure of the parent.

= Behavioural immune system
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Sensory and behavioural processes reducing exposure to <pathogens>, such as avoidance of contamination. This broader defence usage should be distinguished from cellular and molecular <immunity>.

= Hygienic behaviour
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Removal or avoidance of contaminated material or infected individuals, potentially reducing exposure of an individual or group to <pathogens>. <disease> reduction must be measured rather than inferred from cleaning alone.

= Animal communication
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Transmission of information between animals through signals or cues, assessed through effects on receivers.

= Species recognition
{parent=Animal communication}

Discrimination among potential social or mating partners according to species-associated information. Recognition errors and discrimination costs affect its <evolution>.

= Signal eavesdropping
{parent=Animal communication}

Use of a signal by an unintended receiver, such as a <predator> detecting a mating call. This can impose selection opposed to intended-receiver attraction.

= Biological cue
{parent=Animal communication}

An informative feature not necessarily evolved for the receiver’s response. Distinguishing cues from signals requires an evolutionary argument, not just evidence of information transfer.

= Animal signal
{parent=Animal communication}

A feature or action shaped by selection for its effects on a receiver, with receiver responses also subject to selection. A merely informative incidental feature can instead be a <biological cue>.

= Signal honesty
{parent=Animal signal}

A relation between a signal and a property relevant to its receiver. Honesty can follow aligned interests, physical constraints or differential costs and benefits; high production cost alone does not establish it.

= Costly signalling
{parent=Signal honesty}

Signalling in which costs contribute to the evolutionary incentives governing expression. Reliability depends on differences in net payoffs among signaller types, not merely on a signal being expensive.

= Index signal
{parent=Signal honesty}

A signal whose relation to the advertised property is constrained by its production mechanism. The receiver’s use and the relevant physical constraint should both be tested.

= Multimodal signalling
{parent=Animal signal}

Communication using more than one sensory modality. Components can supply redundant information, different information or context-dependent detection benefits.

= Signal modality
{parent=Animal signal}

The sensory channel through which a signal acts, such as acoustic, visual, chemical, mechanical or electrical stimulation.

= Aposematism
{parent=Animal communication}

Use of a warning signal associated with defence. A conspicuous colour pattern alone does not establish either effective defence or <predator> avoidance.

= Parental care
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Behaviour by a parent affecting offspring survival or development, including provisioning, defence and control of the developmental environment. Its costs and benefits need separate measurement.

= Biparental care
{parent=Parental care}

Care supplied by both parents. It can create shared offspring benefits alongside conflict over which parent bears the costs.

= Parental negotiation
{parent=Biparental care}

Conditional adjustment of care in response to a partner, offspring or the environment. A matching response can reflect information about brood demand rather than absence of conflict.

= Partial compensation in parental care
{parent=Parental negotiation}

An increase in care following a partner’s reduction that replaces less than the entire lost contribution. The predicted response depends on information, costs and the time available to adjust.

= Parental investment
{parent=Parental care}

An allocation to current offspring that carries an opportunity cost for other reproductive opportunities. Time spent near offspring is not automatically an investment of the same value in both sexes.

= Reproductive allocation
{parent=Parental investment}

= Paternity certainty
{parent=Parental investment}

Information concerning the probability that offspring are genetically sired by a male. Its effect on care depends on detection, future mating opportunities and effects on offspring, not only average extra-pair paternity.

= Socially transferred material
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Biological material passed from one individual's body to another and capable of influencing the receiver. Simply carrying external food is not sufficient for the narrower framework; donor-processed or donor-produced components matter.

= Maternal provisioning
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Maternal behaviour supplying offspring with resources and potentially bioactive transferred material. Resource quantity and material composition can affect offspring differently.

= Brood ball
{parent=Maternal provisioning}

A provisioned dung mass surrounding the developmental site of an individual dung-beetle offspring. Its food quantity and maternal contributions can be manipulated separately.

= Faecal pedestal
{parent=Brood ball}

Maternal faecal material associated with the egg chamber in certain dung beetles. Ingested material can provide an indirect route of maternal microbial transfer.

= Mirror self-recognition
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Use of mirror information in recognizing one's own body. A particular test outcome does not establish all forms of self-representation or theory of mind.

= Mirror-mark test
{parent=Mirror self-recognition}

An assay asking whether an animal uses reflected information to investigate an otherwise unseen mark on its own body. Visible and sham marks, absence of the mirror and independent detection or action controls constrain the interpretation.

= Metacognition
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Monitoring or control of one's own cognitive processing, assessed through measures such as information seeking or uncertainty responses. Success need not imply introspective awareness.

= Food caching
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Storage of food for future use, which creates opportunities for both memory and competition over stored resources.

= Anticipatory looking
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Looking towards a predicted future action or event before it occurs. Gaze measures require prespecified regions, timing and exclusions.

= Mental representation
{parent=Animal behaviour}

An internal state or process used in representing information about the world, self or others. Behavioural tests should distinguish its proposed content from simpler predictive rules.

= Self-representation
{parent=Mental representation}

Representation of aspects of one's own body, knowledge or actions. These components need not entail an autobiographical or linguistically expressed concept of self.

= Self-awareness
{parent=Self-representation}

Awareness involving oneself as an object or subject of representation. Its proposed forms must be operationally distinguished; <bodily self-recognition> does not alone establish reflective or autobiographical awareness.

= Body schema
{parent=Self-representation}

An action-related representation of bodily configuration and capacities. Its functional use need not entail a verbal or autobiographical concept of self.

= Bodily self-recognition
{parent=Self-representation}

Identification of one’s own body or body-related sensory information. It is narrower than reflective knowledge of one’s identity and does not alone demonstrate <theory of mind>.

= Theory of mind
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Attribution or representation of mental states in oneself or others to explain or predict behaviour. Component abilities need not form an all-or-none package.

= Goal attribution
{parent=Theory of mind}

Interpreting another agent's action in relation to an outcome it is pursuing. It can be narrower than representing the agent's beliefs or intentions in a human-like way.

= False-belief understanding
{parent=Theory of mind}

Representing an agent's belief as differing from reality and predicting action accordingly. False belief, ignorance and a correct remembered location require different controls.

= Knowledge attribution
{parent=Theory of mind}

Tracking another individual's informational access or knowledge. Success does not automatically imply understanding false belief.

= Visual perspective taking
{parent=Theory of mind}

Using differences in visual access between individuals. Knowing what another can see differs from representing exactly how an object appears to them.

= Judgement bias
{parent=Animal behaviour}

A change in responses to ambiguous stimuli relative to trained reference stimuli. Motivation, general activity and perceptual discrimination require control.

= Anthropomorphism
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Attribution of human-like properties to non-human animals. Its evidential risk depends on whether the attributed mechanism is tested rather than assumed.

= Animal consciousness
{parent=Animal behaviour}

The study of conscious experience in non-human animals. Behavioural and neural evidence can constrain hypotheses without directly providing another individual's standpoint.

= Learning
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Change in performance or behaviour through experience, distinguished from immediate sensory responses, fatigue and maturation.

= Predator learning
{parent=Learning}

Changes in predatory responses through experience, including association of <prey> signals with unprofitability. Experiments must distinguish <learning> from immediate avoidance and from loss of appetite.

= Social learning
{parent=Learning}

Learning influenced by other individuals or their products. Its mechanism can range from attention to a location to acquisition of more detailed behavioural information.

= Cultural inheritance
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Transmission of acquired information or behavioural patterns between individuals or generations through social processes. It need not follow genetic parentage.

= Habitat preference
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Differential use or selection of habitats, which can affect exposure to resources, mates and selection.

= Host fidelity
{parent=Habitat preference}

Repeated use of a host type across life stages or generations. Host-associated mating can translate host fidelity into partial reproductive isolation.

= Ethology
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Biological study of <animal behaviour>, combining observation, experiments and evolutionary explanation.

= Tinbergen's four questions
{parent=Ethology}
{c}

Complementary questions concerning mechanism, development, adaptive function and evolutionary history.

= Dominance hierarchy
{parent=Animal behaviour}

A pattern of asymmetric competitive outcomes among group members. Rank can constrain access and participation without implying efficient allocation or identical interests.

= Winner-loser effect
{parent=Dominance hierarchy}

Dependence of subsequent contest behaviour or outcomes on previous wins or losses. Repeated outcomes require controls for pre-existing ability and opponent differences.

= Division of labour
{parent=Animal behaviour}

= Division of labor
{synonym}

Distribution of different tasks among members of a group. Specialization, individual efficiency and collective productivity are separate quantities requiring separate measurements.

= Age polyethism
{parent=Division of labour}

Age-associated changes in the tasks performed by social-group members. Social and physiological regulation can make the sequence plastic rather than strictly chronological.

= Behavioural response threshold
{parent=Animal behaviour}

The stimulus level at which an individual becomes likely to perform an action. Differences in thresholds can generate task allocation when actions change the stimuli confronting other group members.

= Social role
{parent=Animal behaviour}

A recurring contribution or position occupied by a group member in a particular social setting. A stable role can arise through intrinsic differences, state, partner responses or their interaction.

= Social niche specialization
{parent=Social role}

Persistent differentiation of social roles through interactions between an individual’s behaviour and the responses of its partners. Such differentiation need not be genetically fixed.

= Animal personality
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Repeatable differences in behaviour among individuals, assessed after accounting for relevant contexts and states. Repeatability does not by itself establish heritability or an adaptive function.

= Behavioural repeatability
{parent=Animal personality}

The fraction of appropriately modelled phenotypic variance associated with among-individual differences across repeated observations. Estimates depend on the timescale, measured setting and covariates.

= Behavioural syndrome
{parent=Animal personality}

An association among behavioural tendencies across different contexts, distinct from repeatability of one measured behaviour.

= Sensory exploitation
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Use of an existing sensory response to obtain a benefit, including responses maintained by functions outside the exploiting interaction.

= Behavioural manipulation
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Alteration of another organism’s behaviour in a way potentially favouring the manipulator. Demonstrating an adaptive manipulation requires distinguishing its fitness consequences from pathology, host defence and other effects.

= Deceptive signalling
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Signalling that exploits a receiver response in circumstances where that response does not provide its normally associated benefit, while benefiting the signaller. Conscious intent is not required by the evolutionary definition.

= Aggressive mimicry
{parent=Deceptive signalling}

Mimicry by an exploiter that attracts or otherwise misdirects a potential victim, for example by resembling a resource or reproductive opportunity.

= Coordinated behaviour
{parent=Animal behaviour}

Adjustment of the timing, location or type of actions in response to other individuals. Coordination can supply immediate benefits without requiring evolutionary <altruism>.

= Quorum response
{parent=Coordinated behaviour}

A nonlinear increase in commitment to an action as the number of other individuals performing it increases. The effective threshold can depend on group size, competing actions and environmental risk.

= Animal leadership
{parent=Coordinated behaviour}

Consistent influence on the initiation or direction of group action. Leadership may arise from differences in motivation and information together with followers’ responses.

= Mass photometry
{parent=Biology}

Measurement of single-particle mass through its contribution to an optical scattering signal. Unlike <size-exclusion chromatography>, its principal size readout is mass rather than <hydrodynamic size>.

= Structural bioinformatics
{parent=Bioinformatics}

= Protein structure prediction
{parent=Structural bioinformatics}

= Predicted local distance difference test
{parent=Protein structure prediction}
{title2=$\mathrm{pLDDT}\in[0,100]$}

= pLDDT
{c}
{synonym}

An <AlphaFold> confidence score for local structural accuracy, usually reported on a 0–100 scale. It is not a <probability> that a complete structure is correct.

= AlphaFold
{parent=Protein structure prediction}
{c}
{wiki}

= AlphaFold 2
{c}
{parent=AlphaFold}

= Predicted aligned error
{parent=AlphaFold}
{title2=PAE}

Expected positional error at one residue after predicted and true structures are aligned on another residue. Low within-domain values with high between-domain values support more confident internal geometry than relative domain placement. PAE is not a physical residue–residue distance and need not be symmetric.<PAE: A measure of global confidence in AlphaFold2 predictions by EMBL-EBI>[Formal definition and interpretation of inter-domain confidence]{ref}

= Biomineralization
{parent=Biology}

Biologically controlled or influenced production of mineral material. Its evolutionary distribution affects mechanical function and the probability that a body part enters the <fossil record>.

= Biological anthropology
{parent=Biology}

= Biological anthropologist
{parent=Biological anthropology}

{parent=Biological anthropologist}

= Katerina Harvati
{parent=List of biological anthropologists}
{c}

= Israel Hershkovitz
{parent=List of biological anthropologists}
{c}

= Hypoxia
{parent=Biology}

Oxygen availability low relative to cellular or tissue requirements. It can alter metabolism, signalling and treatment responses.

= Senescence
{parent=Biology}

Age-associated deterioration in survival or reproductive performance. <antagonistic pleiotropy> can favor an <allele> with an early reproductive benefit despite a later cost; this does not require deterioration itself to be beneficial.

= Botany
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Plant physiology
{parent=Botany}

Study of the functions of <plants>, including <photosynthesis>, transport, growth and <signal transduction>.

= Flavonoid
{parent=Plant physiology}

A class of plant metabolites. Selected root-exuded flavonoids help induce rhizobial symbiotic signaling; this is one role among many.

= Phototropin
{parent=Plant physiology}

A blue-light-activated receptor kinase family controlling responses including chloroplast relocation, phototropism and stomatal opening.

= NRT1.1
{parent=Plant physiology}
{c}

An Arabidopsis nitrate transporter and sensor, also called CHL1 or NPF6.3. Its nitrate-regulated auxin transport connects local nutrient conditions to lateral-root development.

= Cryptochrome
{parent=Plant physiology}

A blue-light photoreceptor family with roles including plant development, circadian entrainment and photoperiodic responses.

= Photoperiodism
{parent=Plant physiology}

Dependence of a biological response on the daily pattern of light and darkness, often mediated by coincidence between light and an internally timed sensitive phase.

= CONSTANS
{parent=Photoperiodism}
{c}

An Arabidopsis transcriptional regulator whose timed expression and light-dependent protein regulation contribute to photoperiodic activation of <FLOWERING LOCUS T>.

= External coincidence model
{parent=Photoperiodism}

A model in which a light-sensitive internal phase produces a response when it coincides with the external light interval. Equal total light need not produce equal responses if timing differs.

= Guard cell
{parent=Plant physiology}

One of a pair of specialized epidermal cells controlling a <stoma>. Changes in solute content and <turgor pressure> alter pore aperture.

= Plant immunity
{parent=Botany}

= Growth-defense trade-off
{parent=Plant immunity}

Allocation and regulatory conflicts between growth and defence; the sign and magnitude of the relationship depend on environment and <genotype>.

= PAD4
{c}
{parent=Plant immunity}

A component of a <plant> immune signaling pathway whose evolutionary conservation and loss are coupled to other pathway members.

= EDS1
{c}
{parent=Plant immunity}

A component of a <plant> immune signaling pathway whose evolutionary conservation and loss are coupled to other pathway members.

= Hybrid necrosis
{parent=Plant immunity}

A deleterious hybrid <phenotype> that can arise from incompatible immune-gene interactions and inappropriate defence activation.

= Gene-for-gene interaction
{parent=Plant immunity}

A host resistance and pathogen avirulence specificity relationship; it is not the complete architecture of all <plant> disease resistance.

= Nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat receptor
{parent=Plant immunity}

= NLR
{c}
{synonym}

An intracellular immune receptor class in <plants>, commonly shortened to <NLR>.

= Pan-NLRome
{parent=Nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat receptor}

The species-wide collection of <NLR> genes and alleles across accessions, rather than the inventory in a single reference genome.

= NLR gene cluster
{c}
{parent=Nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat receptor}

A genomic group of related receptor genes shaped by duplication, deletion and sequence exchange; not every <NLR> is clustered.

= Integrated decoy
{parent=Nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat receptor}

A domain incorporated into an immune receptor that mimics an effector target and links its perturbation to immune recognition.

= Paired NLR
{parent=Nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat receptor}

Functionally coupled receptors whose sensor and signaling activities may be partitioned between two genes. Physical linkage can help preserve their compatible inheritance.

= RRS1-RPS4 receptor pair
{c}
{parent=Paired NLR}

An <Arabidopsis thaliana> paired immune-receptor example associated with a WRKY integrated domain in RRS1.

= Pattern recognition receptor
{parent=Plant immunity}

= PRR
{c}
{synonym}

A receptor recognizing conserved microbial or damage-associated molecular patterns.

= Resistance gene
{parent=Plant immunity}

A host gene contributing to reduced disease; not all <resistance genes> encode <NLR> receptors.

= Crop
{parent=Botany}

= Plant genetics
{parent=Botany}

= FLOWERING LOCUS T
{c}
{parent=Plant genetics}

= FT protein
{synonym}
{c}

A flowering regulator that can also link maternal temperature history in fruit tissues to progeny dormancy.

= Ph1 locus
{c}
{parent=Plant genetics}

A wheat locus promoting meiotic stability and limiting crossover between homoeologous chromosomes.

= Plant breeding
{parent=Plant genetics}

= Seed priming
{parent=Plant breeding}

A controlled preparatory treatment that changes subsequent germination or stress responses; benefits and storage costs depend on the <crop> and treatment.

= Linkage drag
{parent=Plant breeding}

Retention of unfavorable linked material during introgression of a desired allele.

= Somaclonal variation
{parent=Plant breeding}

Variation among <plants> regenerated from cultured somatic cells; it can have genetic or epigenetic causes.

= Karma epiallele
{c}
{parent=Somaclonal variation}

Methylation variation at an intronic <retrotransposon> associated with normal versus mantled oil-palm development; loss of methylation is associated with altered splicing and reduced yield.

= Plant epigenetics
{parent=Plant genetics}

= FWA
{c}
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

An <Arabidopsis thaliana> gene whose ectopic expression and flowering <phenotype> can be associated with a stably unmethylated promoter state.

= VTE3(1)
{c}
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

A <tomato> vitamin E biosynthetic gene whose expression can be influenced by methylation of a nearby promoter <retrotransposon>.

= Stress priming
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

A prior stimulus changes the response to a subsequent challenge. Persistence may involve metabolites, proteins, regulatory feedback or <chromatin>; priming alone does not establish <epigenetic inheritance>.

= HSFA2
{c}
{parent=Stress priming}

A heat-shock <transcription> factor involved in sustained transcriptional stress memory in <Arabidopsis thaliana>.

= Somatic stress memory
{parent=Stress priming}

Persistence of a response state within an individual after the initial stimulus has ended, distinguished from inheritance through reproduction.

= Vernalization
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

Acquisition of competence to flower after prolonged cold; the molecular implementation and persistence vary among <plant> lineages.

= PEP1
{c}
{parent=Vernalization}

The <Arabis alpina> <FLC> orthologue, whose seasonal reactivation contributes to perennial flowering behavior.

= FLOWERING LOCUS C
{c}
{parent=Vernalization}

= FLC
{c}
{synonym}

An <Arabidopsis thaliana> floral repressor whose stable silencing contributes to memory of prolonged cold.

= Nucleolar dominance
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

Epigenetic silencing of one parental set of ribosomal RNA genes in a hybrid; it is not simply the deletion of those genes.

= RNA-directed DNA methylation
{c}
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

= RdDM
{c}
{synonym}

A <plant> silencing pathway in which small RNAs guide methylation at homologous <chromatin>. Canonical <RdDM> involves <Pol IV>, <RDR2>, <DCL3>, Argonaute and <Pol V> scaffold <transcription>.

= Epigenome editing
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

Targeted alteration of an <epigenetic state>, distinguished from cutting or rewriting the target DNA sequence. Persistence after removal of the targeting factor is locus dependent.

= Epigenome-wide association study
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

= EWAS
{c}
{synonym}

An association analysis relating epigenomic measurements to a <phenotype>. Association alone does not establish causal direction or independence from genetic variation.

= Epigenetic recombinant inbred line
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

= epiRIL
{c}
{synonym}

A line from a mapping population segregating experimentally induced epigenetic differences in a comparatively uniform genetic background. New transposon insertions and residual sequence differences still need to be assessed.

= Epiallele
{parent=Plant epigenetics}

An alternative regulatory state of a locus associated with an epigenetic difference. Its stability and independence from DNA sequence variation must be tested.

= Epimutation
{parent=Epiallele}

A gain or loss of an inherited <epigenetic state> rather than a change in nucleotide sequence.

= Botanist
{parent=Botany}

A <biologist> specializing in <botany>.

= Centrifugation
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Centrifugation> separates suspended material by its motion relative to a rotating liquid. A <centrifuge rotor> creates an outward <centrifugal acceleration> in the <rotating reference frame>; material denser than its surroundings tends to move outward, opposed by <drag force>. Collected material can form a <centrifugation pellet>, leaving a <supernatant>, or remain in separated zones within a <density gradient>. The useful question is which physical difference the separation exploits: a difference in <sedimentation coefficient> or a difference in <buoyant density>.

\Image[centrifuge-rotor-and-separated-fractions.png]
{title=<Centrifuge rotor> geometry and the <centrifugation pellet>–<supernatant> distinction}
{description=Original schematic of one swung-out tube during rotation and a conceptual pelleting comparison. <Rotor radius> increases toward the tube's outer end. Colors and particle counts illustrate incomplete separation and trapping; they are not measured recovery fractions. The tube remains horizontal in this depiction of the rotating geometry.}

<Centrifugation> makes otherwise slow settling useful for studying <biological cells>, <organelles>, <proteins> and <DNA>. Separation and identification are different steps: a <centrifugation pellet> is a collected fraction, not proof that it contains only one kind of <organelle>. The <lysozyme wall-removal experiment> illustrates a complementary readout: testing material released into the separated liquid can be more informative than inspecting the retained solids.

= Analytical ultracentrifugation
{parent=Centrifugation}
{wiki}
{title2=AUC}

Analytical ultracentrifugation observes concentration profiles during rotation, rather than only recovering a separated pellet. Sector-shaped sample cells rotate past optical detectors. Absorbance scans measure attenuation at selected radii. A <Rayleigh interferometer> compares a sample sector with a solvent reference, converting concentration-dependent refractive-index changes into fringe shifts. Repeating scans follows a sedimentation boundary or the approach to a stationary distribution.<Optical Systems Used for AUC by Beckman Coulter Life Sciences>[Radial concentration scans, synchronized absorbance detection and reference-sector interference]{ref}

For a dilute solute of buoyant mass $m_b$ and translational friction $f_h$, radial force balance gives $s=m_b/f_h$, where $s$ is the <sedimentation coefficient>. <Diffusion> supplies $D=k_BT/f_h$. A velocity experiment resolves boundary motion and broadening; a <sedimentation equilibrium> experiment instead measures $d\ln c/d(r^2)=m_b\omega^2/(2k_BT)$. This follows from the <Boltzmann distribution> in centrifugal potential $-m_b\omega^2r^2/2$. The two approaches separate frictional size information from buoyant mass information; shape, hydration and solvent properties must be specified to infer a unique geometric dimension.

= Molar mass from sedimentation and diffusion
{parent=Analytical ultracentrifugation}
{title2=$M=R_{\rm gas}Ts/[D(1-\bar v\rho_s)]$}

Let $M$ be molar mass in $\mathrm{kg\,mol^{-1}}$, $\bar v$ partial specific volume, $\rho_s$ solvent density, and $N_A$ the <Avogadro constant>. The buoyant molecular mass is $m_b=M(1-\bar v\rho_s)/N_A$. Combining <sedimentation coefficient> $s=m_b/f_h$ and the thermal diffusion-friction relation $D=k_BT/f_h$ eliminates the unknown friction and proves the displayed formula, with $R_{\rm gas}=N_Ak_B$. Once friction is known, $R_h=f_h/(6\pi\eta)$ defines a <hydrodynamic radius> using <Stokes's law>. Mass and radius are different inferences: an extended molecule need not occupy the volume of that friction-equivalent sphere.

= Microcentrifuge
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <microcentrifuge> performs <centrifugation> of small sample tubes, as used to concentrate <biological cells> and separate debris in the <PV92 teaching experiment>.

= Centrifuge rotor
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <centrifuge rotor> holds sample vessels around the rotation axis. A fixed-angle <centrifuge rotor> holds tubes at a fixed inclination; a swinging-bucket <centrifuge rotor> lets them swing outward during rotation. Material moves away from the axis, so a <centrifugation pellet> can first collect against an outer tube wall rather than travel along the entire tube axis. Geometry sets both the <rotor radius> and the travel distance.<Basics: Centrifugal Force & Centrifuge Type by Eppendorf>[Basics: Centrifugal Force & Centrifuge Type, Fixed-angle or swing-bucket rotors? and Fixed-angle rotor: tube orientation and pellet position]{ref}

= Rotor radius
{parent=Centrifuge rotor}
{title2=$r$}

The <rotor radius> $r$ is the perpendicular distance from the rotation axis to a specified sample position, not the tube's length. A filled tube spans a range $r_{\min}\leq r\leq r_{\max}$. Consequently, one rotational speed produces different local <centrifugal accelerations> along that range. A reported <relative centrifugal force> needs a stated reference position, such as the outermost sample radius.

= Relative centrifugal force
{parent=Centrifuge rotor}
{title2=$\mathrm{RCF}=a/g_0$}

<Relative centrifugal force> is the <dimensionless> ratio of the local <centrifugal acceleration> magnitude $a$ to the conventional gravitational reference $g_0=9.80665\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. Despite its name, it is an acceleration ratio: the actual <force> on a specified <mass> $m$ is $ma$. With <angular speed> $\omega$ and <rotor radius> $r$, $a=\omega^2r$. If $n$ is the numerical rotational speed in revolutions per minute and $r_{\mathrm{cm}}$ the numerical <rotor radius> in centimetres, unit conversion gives
$$
\omega=\frac{2\pi n}{60}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}},\qquad
\mathrm{RCF}=\frac{(r_{\mathrm{cm}}/100)(2\pi n/60)^2}{9.80665}
\simeq1.118\times10^{-5}r_{\mathrm{cm}}n^2.
$$
Thus rpm alone does not specify the <centrifugal acceleration>.<Basics: Centrifugal Force & Centrifuge Type by Eppendorf>[Important definitions, Rcm vs rpm, and How to calculate relative centrifugal force: radius dependence and acceleration ratio]{ref}

\Image[centrifuge-radius-speed-relative-acceleration.png]
{title=<Relative centrifugal force> for stipulated <rotor radii> and rotational speeds}
{description=Original theoretical curves calculated from $a=\omega^2r$ with $g_0=9.80665\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. The <rotor radii> of $5$, $10$ and $15\,\mathrm{cm}$ are chosen examples, not specifications of real <centrifuge rotors>. The plotted range is not an instrument operating limit or a recommendation.}

For a stipulated $3000\,\mathrm{rpm}$, <rotor radii> of $5$ and $10\,\mathrm{cm}$ give approximately $503$ and $1006$ times $g_0$. Doubling the <rotor radius> doubles <relative centrifugal force>; doubling rpm quadruples it. Even equal <relative centrifugal forces> do not guarantee equal separation: travel distance, elapsed time, <viscosity>, sample <density> and particle shape still matter. These are comparisons of a <mathematical model>, not instructions for running an instrument.

= Centrifugation pellet
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <centrifugation pellet> is material accumulated against the vessel wall by <centrifugation>. Its existence establishes collection, not purity; other suspended material can be trapped or co-sediment.

= Supernatant
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <supernatant> is the liquid remaining above settled or pelleted material. It can contain dissolved <molecules> and particles that have not reached the <centrifugation pellet>; a visually clear <supernatant> need not be chemically empty.

= Sedimentation coefficient
{parent=Centrifugation}
{title2=$s=v/(\omega^2r)$}

The <sedimentation coefficient> $s$ is radial drift <velocity> divided by local <centrifugal acceleration>. It has units of time and combines particle properties with those of the surrounding liquid. In a dilute, deterministic <mathematical model> with negligible particle <inertia>, an effective displaced volume $V$, particle <mass> $m$, local liquid <density> $\rho_f$ and <linear friction coefficient> $f>0$ give
$$
f v=(m-\rho_f V)\omega^2r,\qquad s=\frac{v}{\omega^2r}=\frac{m-\rho_f V}{f}.
$$
The subtraction represents <buoyancy>. Equal <mass> does not imply equal $s$: changing shape changes <drag force>, and changing the medium changes both <buoyancy> and <viscosity>.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/ultracentrifugation/[An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation, The Physics Behind the Technology: mass, size, shape, medium density and viscosity]{ref}

For an isolated <sphere> of radius $b$ and uniform <density> $\rho_p$, <Stokes's law> supplies $f=6\pi\eta b$, where $\eta$ is dynamic <viscosity>, and $m=(4\pi/3)b^3\rho_p$. Therefore
$$
s=\frac{2b^2(\rho_p-\rho_f)}{9\eta}.
$$
This original force-balance check predicts fourfold $s$ on doubling $b$ with everything else fixed, and zero drift when $\rho_p=\rho_f$. It applies to dilute isolated <spheres> in <Stokes flow>, not arbitrary folded <proteins> or crowded suspensions. If $s$ and $\omega$ remain constant in a uniform medium, the <ordinary differential equation> $dr/dt=s\omega^2r$ has the <exponential function> solution $r(t)=r(0)e^{s\omega^2t}$. Arrival at an outer boundary therefore depends on starting position as well as $s$; a separation has no universal size cutoff.

= Differential centrifugation
{parent=Centrifugation}
{wiki}

<Differential centrifugation> successively separates <centrifugation pellets> from <supernatants> by differences in settling rate. A first <centrifugation pellet> can enrich faster-settling material; a later <centrifugation pellet> from its <supernatant> can enrich slower-settling material. The fractions overlap because particles have different starting positions and can become trapped. <Differential centrifugation> is consequently an enrichment method, not automatic identification or complete purification.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/principles/particle-separation/[Particle Separation: differential pelleting, smaller particles initially near the tube bottom, and incomplete separation]{ref}

= Density-gradient centrifugation
{parent=Centrifugation}

<Density-gradient centrifugation> uses a liquid whose <density> varies along the separation direction. A band does not by itself establish which mechanism produced it: <rate-zonal centrifugation> separates by migration rate before equilibrium, whereas <isopycnic centrifugation> uses equilibrium positions set by <buoyant density>.

The distinction is between a moving clock and a restoring balance. In <rate-zonal centrifugation>, two species may occupy different positions because their <sedimentation coefficients> differ, even when their <buoyant densities> agree. In <isopycnic centrifugation>, two differently sized species can converge to the same band because their <buoyant densities> agree. Conversely, equal <mass> does not ensure equal migration <velocity>: shape affects <drag force>, and medium <density> affects <buoyancy>.<An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation by Beckman Coulter Life Sciences>[The Physics Behind the Technology; Rate Zonal Centrifugation; Isopycnic Centrifugation: migration properties versus matching density]{ref}

The worked <finite-time rate-zonal separation> and <linear-gradient isopycnic focusing> examples isolate these mechanisms in chosen <mathematical models>. The actual <DNA> experiment described under <isopycnic centrifugation> illustrates the second mechanism; its initial collection of <biological cells> into <centrifugation pellets> is a different operation from the later molecular banding. Collecting liquid fractions from a tube can recover material at different positions, but the position alone does not identify its <molecules> or establish purity.

= Density-gradient flotation
{parent=Density-gradient centrifugation}

Separation in which a particle's buoyant properties cause it to move toward a lower-density part of a gradient. A <liposome> fraction can thereby be separated from unassociated soluble <proteins>; copurification alone does not demonstrate correct <membrane topology>.

= Isopycnic centrifugation
{parent=Density-gradient centrifugation}

= Equilibrium density-gradient centrifugation
{synonym}

In <isopycnic centrifugation>, a species concentrates near the position where the liquid's <density> matches its <buoyant density>. In a <density gradient> increasing outward, material displaced inward encounters less-dense liquid and tends to move outward; material displaced outward encounters denser liquid and tends to move inward. <Diffusion> broadens the resulting band. If the <density gradient> never reaches the species' <buoyant density>, a matching equilibrium position need not exist within the vessel.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/ultracentrifugation/[An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation, Isopycnic Centrifugation: matching buoyant density and diffusion in equilibrium gradients]{ref}

The 1958 paper <The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl> (pp. 672–676, Figures 1–4) makes the distinction concrete. Its separation apparatus was a Spinco Model E ultracentrifuge with an <Escherichia coli> lysate in a <cesium chloride density gradient>. Ultraviolet <wave absorption> photographs of the rotating sample were scanned with a recording microdensitometer: peak position reported <buoyant density>, while signal above baseline reported <DNA> <concentration>.<The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl>[p. 676, Experimental: Spinco Model E and recording microdensitometer; p. 675, Figure 4: peak position and baseline]{ref}

Cultures received labeled <nitrogen>; chilled <biological cells> were collected as <centrifugation pellets> and disrupted before molecular banding.<The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl>[pp. 673–674, Experimental: labeled growth medium, chilled collection and detergent disruption]{ref}

Mixed lysates labeled fully with the <nitrogen> <isotope> $^{15}\mathrm{N}$ and unlabeled lysates supplied reference bands; a mixture including intermediate material checked its position relative to those references. Figure 1 followed band formation over time, helping distinguish a migrating distribution from an approximately stationary one. These controls address resolution, calibration and approach to equilibrium. They do not make every ultraviolet-absorbing component identifiable as <DNA>, or turn a band into a direct image of individual strands. The <DNA replication> article develops the copying inference; here the key result is a separation by <buoyant density>, not a claim that heavier-labeled <DNA> is physically larger.<The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl>[pp. 672–673, Figures 1–2; p. 675, Figure 4 density references and microdensitometer baseline; p. 676, Experimental: Spinco Model E and ultraviolet photographs]{ref}

= Linear-gradient isopycnic focusing
{parent=Isopycnic centrifugation}
{title2=$z_*=(\rho_b-\rho_0)/G$}

Why does an <isopycnic centrifugation> band return after displacement? Choose a dilute <mathematical model> with outward coordinate $z$, constant local <centrifugal acceleration> $a>0$, fixed displaced <volume> $V>0$, <linear friction coefficient> $f>0$, and an imposed liquid <density> $\rho_f(z)=\rho_0+Gz$ with $G>0$. Treat the species' <buoyant density> $\rho_b$ as constant in this medium. The <sedimentation coefficient> force balance gives the <ordinary differential equation>
$$
\frac{dz}{dt}=\frac{Va}{f}\bigl(\rho_b-\rho_f(z)\bigr)=-k(z-z_*),\qquad k=\frac{VaG}{f},\qquad z_*=\frac{\rho_b-\rho_0}{G}.
$$
Its <exponential function> solution is $z(t)=z_*+(z(0)-z_*)e^{-kt}$. The sign of the <velocity> reverses across $z_*$, proving <stable equilibrium> when $z_*$ lies inside the liquid. Changing $V/f$ changes the approach time $1/k$, while $z_*$ depends on <buoyant density> and the imposed <density gradient>. A real <centrifuge rotor> has radially varying <centrifugal acceleration>; the constant-$a$ approximation describes a short radial interval. Here the <density gradient> is held fixed, rather than modeling its formation.

For an original <dimensionless> example, let $u=z/L$ and $\tau=t/T$, where $L$ is column length and $T$ a chosen time scale. Write $q=z_*/L$ and $\lambda=kT$, so $u(\tau)=q+(u(0)-q)e^{-\lambda\tau}$. Species A and B have the same $q=0.45$ but different friction-dependent rates: $(\lambda_A,u_A(0))=(0.4,0.1)$ and $(\lambda_B,u_B(0))=(1.6,0.95)$. A moves outward and B inward, both toward $u=0.45$. Species C has $(q_C,\lambda_C,u_C(0))=(0.8,0.8,0.1)$ and approaches a separate band at $u=0.8$. At $\tau=4$, their positions are approximately $0.3793$, $0.4508$ and $0.7715$: a finite-time photograph would still show A short of its limiting position.

\Image[isopycnic-bands-and-restoring-trajectories.png]
{title=<Isopycnic centrifugation>: different approach rates can lead to the same <buoyant density> band}
{description=Original schematic tubes and deterministic trajectories for the stipulated <linear-gradient isopycnic focusing> model. A and B share a matching <density> position; C has another. Tube shading represents an imposed increasing <density gradient>. Band thickness is schematic; <diffusion> is omitted from the plotted trajectories. The final tube shows the mathematical limit while rotation continues, not a measured result or a stopped tube.}

An equilibrium band has finite width because <diffusion> opposes focusing. In the same local model, let $c(z)$ be <concentration> and let the constant <diffusion coefficient> be $D>0$. Combining the restoring drift with <Fick's laws>, zero net <flux> requires
$$
J=-k(z-z_*)c-D\frac{dc}{dz}=0,\qquad c(z)=c(z_*)\exp\!\left[-\frac{k(z-z_*)^2}{2D}\right].
$$
Dividing by $c$ and integrating proves the <Gaussian function> profile, whose <standard deviation> is $\sigma_z=\sqrt{D/k}$. This local result assumes that the band is narrow relative to its distance from the boundaries and that $D$, $k$ and the <density gradient> vary negligibly across it. Equal <buoyant densities> therefore imply coincident centers in this model, not identical widths. A band outside the available <density> range has no such interior restoring point.

= Buoyant density
{parent=Isopycnic centrifugation}
{title2=$\rho_b$}

<Buoyant density> is the surrounding liquid <density> at which a species has no net centrifugal drift under the stated conditions. It depends on interactions with the medium, including associated solvent, rather than necessarily equaling the dry substance's <density>. Equal <buoyant densities> can give overlapping bands even for differently sized <proteins> or <DNA> fragments.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/ultracentrifugation/[An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation, Isopycnic Centrifugation: buoyant density depends on the surrounding medium]{ref}

= Cesium chloride density gradient
{parent=Isopycnic centrifugation}

A <cesium chloride density gradient> forms when the <salt> $\mathrm{CsCl}$ redistributes in a rotating <chemical solution>. It can supply matching-density positions for <DNA> during <isopycnic centrifugation>.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/density-gradient-centrifugation/[Types of Density Gradients and Types of Gradient Material: self-forming CsCl gradients and nucleic-acid separation]{ref}

= Rate-zonal centrifugation
{parent=Density-gradient centrifugation}

<Rate-zonal centrifugation> resolves an initially narrow sample zone by differences in <sedimentation coefficient> during a finite interval. Its bands remain transient: extending the interval can carry material into a <centrifugation pellet>. Thus a <density gradient> need not imply an equilibrium <buoyant density> measurement.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/density-gradient-centrifugation/[Types of Density Gradients, Rate Zonal Centrifugation and Table 1: migration rates before equilibrium or complete sedimentation]{ref}

A typical preparation layers a narrow sample above a preformed <density gradient>, often containing <sucrose>. The initial zone must be narrow enough that differences in starting position do not overwhelm the later separation. The run ends while useful bands remain inside the liquid. In the simple case where all particles are denser than every part of the column, none has an interior <isopycnic centrifugation> position.<An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation by Beckman Coulter Life Sciences>[Rate Zonal Centrifugation: top loading, preformed gradient, starting-zone width and stopping before pelleting]{ref}<Density Gradient Centrifugation by Beckman Coulter Life Sciences>[Types of Density Gradients: rate-zonal preformed sucrose gradients]{ref}

The <sedimentation coefficient> $s=(m-\rho_fV)/f$ explains why separation is not an ordering by <mass> alone. At fixed local <centrifugal acceleration>, increasing displaced <volume> can increase <buoyancy>, while a less compact shape can increase the <linear friction coefficient> and reduce <velocity>. Even for a <sphere>, <Stokes's law> gives $s=2b^2(\rho_p-\rho_f)/(9\eta)$, so both the <density> difference and the dynamic <viscosity> matter. The resulting <sedimentation coefficient> can change along a <density gradient>.

= Finite-time rate-zonal separation
{parent=Rate-zonal centrifugation}

Consider two dilute populations of <spheres> with the same <buoyant density> and radii $b$ and $2b$. Adopt the fixed linear <density gradient> and constant local <centrifugal acceleration> of <linear-gradient isopycnic focusing>, with constant dynamic <viscosity> and negligible <diffusion>. <Stokes's law> makes $V/f$ proportional to $b^2$, so doubling radius multiplies the migration rate parameter by four, although the <mass> increases eightfold.

Use $u=z/L$, $\tau=t/T$ and $q=(\rho_b-\rho_0)/(GL)=2$. The liquid occupies $0\leq u\leq1$, so the matching <density> position lies beyond its outer boundary. Both populations begin at $u(0)=0.1$; choose $\lambda_A=0.15$ and $\lambda_B=0.6$. Their band-center trajectories are
$$
u_A(\tau)=2-1.9e^{-0.15\tau},\qquad u_B(\tau)=2-1.9e^{-0.6\tau}.
$$
At $\tau=0.5$, they lie at approximately $0.2373$ and $0.5924$; at $\tau=1$, at $0.3647$ and $0.9573$. Equal <buoyant density> has not prevented a useful finite-time separation. Solving $u_B(\tau)=1$ using the <natural logarithm> gives first boundary arrival at $\tau=\ln(1.9)/0.6\simeq1.0698$. The corresponding arrival for A is $\ln(1.9)/0.15\simeq4.2790$. Trajectories describe migration only until contact with the boundary; subsequent collection into a <centrifugation pellet> is not represented by extending the curve beyond $u=1$.

\Image[rate-zonal-bands-before-boundary-arrival.png]
{title=<Rate-zonal centrifugation>: equal <buoyant density>, different finite-time migration}
{description=Original schematic tubes and trajectories for the stipulated <finite-time rate-zonal separation> model. B represents <spheres> with twice A's radius. Both matching <density> positions lie outside the column. Tube shading shows an imposed increasing <density gradient>; band thickness is schematic. The marked boundary-arrival time is calculated from this model, not an experimental run time or an operating instruction.}

This is the useful contrast with <isopycnic centrifugation>: stopping time helps determine the recovered positions in <rate-zonal centrifugation>, whereas a stationary matching <density> position sets the limiting band center in <isopycnic centrifugation>. Real resolution also depends on initial zone width, <diffusion> and variations in particle properties; distinct calculated centers alone do not prove that fractions will be pure.

= Biophysics
{parent=Biology}

= Biomolecular condensate
{parent=Biophysics}

A compositionally enriched biomolecular assembly. Calling an assembly a condensate does not alone establish equilibrium liquid–liquid phase separation or a particular biological function.

= Stress granule
{parent=Biomolecular condensate}

A stress-associated RNA–protein assembly whose formation and composition depend on context. <RNA> enrichment does not establish obligatory translational silence.<Single-Molecule Imaging Reveals Translation of mRNAs Localized to Stress Granules by Daniel Mateju and others>[Translation of granule-associated mRNAs]{ref}
