= Work by Robert K. Nakamoto
{c}
{parent=Robert K. Nakamoto}

= Molecular motor
{wiki}
{parent=Biophysics}

A <molecular motor> couples an <energy> supply to directed mechanical motion. <ATP synthase> provides a rotary example.

= Dynein
{parent=Molecular motor}

A <microtubule>-associated <molecular motor> that generally transports cargo toward minus ends.

= Kinesin
{parent=Molecular motor}

A <microtubule>-associated <molecular motor> family. Many transport cargos toward plus ends, although direction and function vary among family members.

= Biophysical measurement
{parent=Biology}

= DNA fluorescence in situ hybridization
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

Detection of selected genomic regions with complementary fluorescent probes, supporting spatial-distance and nuclear-position measurements. Fixation, denaturation and probe design can affect the measurement.

= DamID
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

Mapping <DNA> near a <protein> by tethering a <DNA> adenine methyltransferase and detecting its deposited mark. Expression level and exposure duration affect spatial and temporal interpretation.

= CUT&RUN
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

Antibody-targeted nuclease cleavage and release of <DNA> near a chosen chromatin-associated <protein> or mark. Background, <antibody> specificity and cleavage conditions still need controls.

= SunTag
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

A repeated peptide-epitope system detectable by fluorescent binders. Placing tags on nascent chains can make a <biological translation> site visible separately from completed <proteins>.

= Isotope labelling
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

Introducing selected <isotopes> into molecules to support detection or assignment. In <nuclear magnetic resonance>, labelling can add observable spins or suppress unwanted relaxation pathways.

= Selective methyl labelling
{parent=Isotope labelling}

Isotopic labelling of selected methyl groups to provide sensitive <NMR> probes in a relatively sparse spin network.

= Deuteration
{parent=Isotope labelling}

Replacement of hydrogen with deuterium. In <protein> <NMR>, suitable <deuteration> reduces proton dipolar relaxation but also removes directly observable proton signals unless selected sites are retained or reintroduced.

= Traction force microscopy
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<traction force microscopy> estimates forces that cells exert on a deformable substrate from measured substrate displacement and a mechanical model. Calibration, boundary conditions and material properties affect the inferred forces.

= Electron cryotomography
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<Electron cryotomography> reconstructs three-dimensional electron-scattering density from a tilt series of a vitrified specimen. It can reveal native cellular assemblies such as receptor arrays, subject to dose, sampling and resolution limits.

= Selection assay
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

A <selection assay> preferentially retains, amplifies or permits survival of candidates satisfying a criterion. It differs from directly measuring every candidate and can fail if the selectable phenotype becomes detached from the intended biochemical function.

= Screening assay
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

A <screening assay> measures candidate variants individually or in distinguishable compartments. In protein engineering, expression, stability and intrinsic activity must be separated when interpreting an increased signal.

= Surface plasmon resonance
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<Surface plasmon resonance> can monitor binding near an optical sensor surface in real time through refractive-index changes. Concentration-dependent association and dissociation can constrain kinetics, subject to controls for nonspecific binding, transport limitation and multivalency.

= Localized surface plasmon resonance
{parent=Surface plasmon resonance}

= LSPR
{synonym}
{c}

A resonant collective electronic response confined to a finite conducting nanostructure. Its near field depends on geometry, environment and loss.

= Isothermal titration calorimetry
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<Isothermal titration calorimetry> measures heat accompanying additions of one binding partner to another at controlled temperature. With suitable concentration ranges, stoichiometry and a binding model, it can estimate affinity and binding enthalpy; dilution and buffer effects need controls.

= Dynamic light scattering
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<Dynamic light scattering> analyses fluctuations in scattered light to infer diffusion and hydrodynamic size under a suitable model. Large particles can dominate the intensity, making it sensitive to aggregation but limiting simple interpretation as a number distribution.

= Toeprinting
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<Toeprinting> maps a reverse-transcription stop caused by an assembled ribosome or initiation complex on RNA. It can report occupancy near a translation start site, but controls are needed for stops caused by RNA structure or other bound proteins.

= Radiation damage
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<radiation damage> is a radiation-induced change in a specimen. In biological <electron microscopy>, accumulated exposure can alter chemical groups and remove high-resolution information, so recording more electrons has competing signal and damage effects.

= Knock-on displacement
{parent=Radiation damage}

Displacement of an atom when a collision transfers enough momentum to exceed its displacement threshold. Its energy dependence differs from <radiolysis>.

= Radiolysis
{parent=Radiation damage}

Chemical damage initiated by ionization and electronic excitation from radiation. It is distinct from direct momentum-transfer <knock-on displacement>; the useful image information per damaging event depends on beam energy and specimen.

= Water radiolysis
{parent=Radiolysis}

Chemical transformation of water initiated by ionizing radiation. Both reducing and oxidizing products occur; observed net redox chemistry depends on the solution and reactions of primary products.

= Double electron-electron resonance
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<double electron-electron resonance> is a pulsed <electron paramagnetic resonance> method that uses spin–spin coupling to constrain distances between labels. Label conformation, distance distributions and background correction affect the structural interpretation.

= Colocalization
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<colocalization> is spatial coincidence of measured distributions within the resolution of an imaging method. Shared pixels can suggest a common compartment without demonstrating direct molecular contact.

= Single-particle tracking
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<single-particle tracking> estimates successive positions of individual labeled objects. Displacements can constrain <diffusion>, confinement or directed movement, provided localization error, missed detections and label effects are accounted for.

= TurboID
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

<TurboID> is an engineered promiscuous <biotin> ligase used for <proximity labeling>. A fusion can label nearby <proteins> during a short pulse, while localization-matched and no-added-biotin controls help distinguish the intended neighbourhood from background.<Efficient proximity labeling in living cells and organisms with TurboID by Tess C. Branon and others>[Fig. 2, short-pulse compartment labeling and specificity comparisons]{ref}

= Proximity labeling
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<proximity labeling> uses a localized reactive system to mark nearby <molecules> before extraction. The recovered label integrates access, distance, labeling rate and residence time; proximity alone does not demonstrate a direct <protein-protein interaction>.

= Co-immunoprecipitation
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

<co-immunoprecipitation> captures a <protein> using an <antibody> and tests which other components remain associated. Reciprocal capture, input abundance and negative controls help interpret the result; coprecipitation need not imply direct contact.

= Affinity purification-mass spectrometry
{parent=Biophysical measurement}

In <affinity purification-mass spectrometry>, a bait <protein> is isolated with associated material, and <mass spectrometry> identifies candidate partners. Enrichment relative to matched controls supports association; it does not alone establish a direct <protein-protein interaction> or the number of copies per assembly.

= Förster resonance energy transfer
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

= FRET
{synonym}
{c}

<Förster resonance energy transfer> is a nonradiative transfer of <energy> from an <excited state> of a <FRET donor> to a nearby <FRET acceptor>, through coupling of their <electric dipoles>. The transfer step does not emit a <photon> that the <FRET acceptor> subsequently absorbs. It competes with the <FRET donor>'s own <fluorescence> and other routes out of its <excited state>. A fluorescent <FRET acceptor> can then emit <fluorescence>; an absorbing but nonfluorescent <FRET acceptor> can still receive the transferred <energy>.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1: nonradiative dipole coupling and a potentially nonfluorescent acceptor]{ref}

The striking use of <FRET> is to turn a change in molecular proximity into an optical readout. <Proteins> carrying a <FRET donor> and a <FRET acceptor> may change the readout when they associate, as in the <chemotaxis FRET assay>; two labels on one <protein> may report a change in its shape. Interpretation depends on the <FRET spectral overlap>, <FRET dipole orientation factor> and labeling geometry, as well as separation. A change in <FRET> is therefore evidence about proximity under specified conditions, rather than a direct measurement of an arbitrary distance.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1 and §3.3: dependence on distance and orientation; inter- and intramolecular applications]{ref}

For a single pair with fixed <Förster distance> $R_0$ and label separation $r$, the <FRET efficiency> is
$$
E=\frac{1}{1+(r/R_0)^6}.
$$
At $r=R_0$, half the <FRET donor> excitations transfer <energy>. At $r=R_0/2$, the model gives $E=64/65\approx98.46\%$; at $r=2R_0$, it gives $E=1/65\approx1.54\%$. The sixth power makes <FRET> especially useful near the <Förster distance>, with little sensitivity to further separation once transfer is already negligible. These are model calculations, not measurements of a particular pair.

\Image[forster-distance-transfer-efficiency.png]
{title=<FRET efficiency> versus separation relative to the <Förster distance>}
{description=Original single-pair <mathematical model> with fixed <Förster distance>. The shaded interval is $0.2\leq E\leq0.8$. The curve does not establish a universal working range or justify the <electric dipole> approximation for overlapping labels.}

= Single-molecule Förster resonance energy transfer
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}

<single-molecule Förster resonance energy transfer> records energy-transfer signals from individual donor–acceptor pairs. State occupancies and dwell times can constrain structural dynamics, provided dye photophysics, labeling geometry and time resolution are controlled.

= FRET donor
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

A <FRET donor> supplies the initial <excited state> <energy>. Its <fluorescence> provides both an emission channel and a reference <FRET donor lifetime> when no <FRET acceptor> is present. Calling a label a <FRET donor> describes its role in a chosen pair, rather than a permanent classification of the <molecule>.

= FRET acceptor
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

A <FRET acceptor> receives the <excited state> <energy>. Its absorption must support <FRET spectral overlap> with the <FRET donor>. <FRET sensitized emission> requires a fluorescent <FRET acceptor>; measuring a shortened <FRET donor lifetime> does not.

= FRET efficiency
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}
{title2=$E$}

<FRET efficiency> is the <probability> that an excitation of the <FRET donor> ends in transfer to the <FRET acceptor>. Write $k_D=k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr}$ for the total decay rate without a <FRET acceptor>, including radiative <fluorescence> and other decay, and $k_T$ for the transfer rate. For constant competing rates,
$$
k_T=k_D\left(\frac{R_0}{r}\right)^6,\qquad E=\frac{k_T}{k_D+k_T}=\int_0^\infty k_T e^{-(k_D+k_T)t}\,dt.
$$
The <integral> counts transfer events among all excitations in a population with <exponential decay>. Substitution gives the separation law above. This short rate argument assumes one <FRET donor>, one <FRET acceptor>, weak coupling, an available <FRET acceptor>, and fixed geometry during the decay; it is not a model for arbitrary interacting labels.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1, equations 14–16 and discussion before §3.3: single-pair assumptions and transfer efficiency]{ref}

= Förster distance
{parent=FRET efficiency}
{c}
{title2=$R_0$}

= Förster radius
{synonym}
{c}

The <Förster distance> is the separation giving <FRET efficiency> $E=1/2$ for the specified pair and conditions. It is a property of the optical pair and its environment, rather than a universal length. In the usual pair model,
$$
R_0^6\propto \kappa^2 Q_D J n^{-4},
$$
where $\kappa^2$ is the <FRET dipole orientation factor>, $Q_D$ the <FRET donor quantum yield>, $J$ the <FRET spectral overlap> <integral>, and $n$ the medium's <refractive index>. The omitted proportionality constant depends on the unit convention for $J$.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1, equations 16–18: factors controlling Förster distance]{ref}

If <FRET efficiency> is known and those assumptions hold, inversion gives
$$
r=R_0\left(E^{-1}-1\right)^{1/6},\qquad \frac{d\log r}{dE}=-\frac{1}{6E(1-E)}.
$$
The <derivative> explains why errors become troublesome near $E=0$ or $E=1$. An illustrative pair with $R_0=4$ nanometres and another with $R_0=6$ nanometres both give $E=1/2$ at their different <Förster distances>: knowing <FRET efficiency> alone does not determine $r$.

A heterogeneous population adds another ambiguity. In a synthetic example with equal weights per <FRET donor> excitation, efficiencies $0.1$ and $0.9$ average to $0.5$, but their separations are $9^{1/6}R_0$ and $9^{-1/6}R_0$. Their average separation is approximately $1.068R_0$, whereas inverting the average <FRET efficiency> gives $R_0$. A measured emission <ratio> has additional detection weights; it need not equal even this excitation-weighted average. The transferable lesson is that an average readout does not uniquely specify a distribution of molecular separations.

= FRET spectral overlap
{parent=Förster distance}
{c}
{title2=$J$}

<FRET spectral overlap> quantifies overlap between the <FRET donor>'s emission and the <FRET acceptor>'s absorption as functions of <wavelength> $\lambda$. With normalized donor emission $f_D$ and acceptor molar absorption coefficient $\varepsilon_A$, one convention is
$$
\int_0^\infty f_D(\lambda)\,d\lambda=1,\qquad J=\int_0^\infty f_D(\lambda)\varepsilon_A(\lambda)\lambda^4\,d\lambda.
$$
Matching two emission colors is insufficient: the receiving absorption matters.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1, equation 17 and Figure 19: normalized donor emission and acceptor absorption overlap]{ref}

= FRET dipole orientation factor
{parent=Förster distance}
{c}
{title2=$\kappa^2$}

The <FRET dipole orientation factor> describes the geometry of the two transition <electric dipoles> relative to their separation. Let $\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_D$, $\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_A$ and $\widehat{\mathbf r}$ be their <unit vectors>. Then
$$
\kappa^2=\left[\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_D\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_A-3(\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_D\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r})(\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_A\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r})\right]^2,\qquad 0\leq\kappa^2\leq4.
$$
For a separation along $z$, parallel <electric dipoles> along $z$ give $\kappa^2=4$, parallel ones along $x$ give $1$, and one along $x$ with the other along $y$ gives $0$. Thus rotating a pair can change <FRET efficiency> without changing its separation.

The familiar value $\kappa^2=2/3$ requires suitable rapid, independent orientation averaging during the <FRET donor lifetime>. Random orientations across a population of slowly rotating fluorescent <proteins> do not establish that dynamic condition. <Quantitative time domain analysis of lifetime-based Förster resonant energy transfer measurements with fluorescent proteins: Static random isotropic fluorophore orientation distributions by Yuriy Alexandrov and others>{ref}

= FRET donor quantum yield
{parent=Förster distance}
{c}
{title2=$Q_D$}

The <FRET donor quantum yield> is the emitted <fluorescence> <photon> fraction per absorbed excitation <photon>, measured without a <FRET acceptor>. In the constant-rate model used for <FRET efficiency>, $Q_D=k_{\rm r}/(k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr})$. Changing the <FRET donor quantum yield> changes the <Förster distance>, even if label positions remain fixed.

= FRET donor lifetime
{parent=FRET efficiency}
{c}
{title2=$\tau_D,\tau_{DA}$}

The <FRET donor lifetime> is the mean time spent in the <excited state>. For the constant-rate model of <FRET efficiency>,
$$
\tau_D=\frac1{k_D},\qquad \tau_{DA}=\frac1{k_D+k_T},\qquad E=1-\frac{\tau_{DA}}{\tau_D}.
$$
After a sufficiently brief excitation pulse, the <FRET donor>'s <fluorescence> follows <exponential decay>, $I(t)=I(0)e^{-t/\tau}$. With the illustrative values $\tau_D=4$ nanoseconds and $\tau_{DA}=2$ nanoseconds, the inferred <FRET efficiency> is $1/2$. Multiplying $I(0)$ changes brightness without changing this decay time.

The lifetime comparison requires the same nontransfer decay rates in the reference and paired samples. Changes in the <FRET donor>'s environment or a different nonradiative loss can also shorten the <FRET donor lifetime>. Heterogeneous <FRET efficiencies> or slowly varying <FRET dipole orientation factors> need an appropriate decay model; substituting any fitted average lifetime into the single-pair formula can mislead. <Quantitative time domain analysis of lifetime-based Förster resonant energy transfer measurements with fluorescent proteins: Static random isotropic fluorophore orientation distributions by Yuriy Alexandrov and others>{ref}

\Image[fret-fluorescence-lifetime-readouts.png]
{title=<FRET sensitized emission>, optical controls and the <FRET donor lifetime>}
{description=Original schematic and synthetic curves. The spectral panel assumes equal <fluorescence> yields and collection efficiencies for the two labels; its amplitudes are illustrative, not a calibration or measured cyan/yellow spectra. The lifetime panel uses $\tau_D=4$ and $\tau_{DA}=2$ nanoseconds. Dashed optical paths require <FRET optical crosstalk> controls; they do not represent the nonradiative transfer mechanism.}

= FRET sensitized emission
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

<FRET sensitized emission> is <FRET acceptor> <fluorescence> caused by transfer from an excited <FRET donor>. Excite predominantly the <FRET donor> and collect both emission bands. Reduced <FRET donor> emission and increased <FRET acceptor> emission are useful together, but an acceptor-to-donor emission <ratio> is not automatically the <FRET efficiency>: brightness, label abundance and channel collection also enter.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.4.2: sensitized emission and dependence on optical correction and label concentrations]{ref}

= FRET optical crosstalk
{parent=FRET sensitized emission}
{c}

<FRET optical crosstalk> includes <FRET donor> emission leaking into the <FRET acceptor> detection band and direct excitation of the <FRET acceptor> by the nominal <FRET donor> excitation light. Measure donor-only, acceptor-only and unlabeled preparations with the same <fluorescence microscope> settings to separate these contributions and background.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.4.2, samples i–iii and equations 20–21: spectral correction controls]{ref}

An explicit linear measurement model makes the controls' purpose clear. Let $D$ be background-subtracted donor-band counts under donor excitation, $A$ acceptor-band counts under acceptor excitation, and $F$ acceptor-band counts under donor excitation. Donor-only and acceptor-only preparations determine leakage coefficients $a$ and $b$. Then
$$
S=F-aD-bA
$$
is a corrected <FRET sensitized emission> signal under the assumed linear, fixed optical response. In a synthetic example $D=100$, $A=80$, $a=0.10$, $b=0.05$ and $F=34$ give $S=20$. Even a nontransferring sample would give $F=14$ from these two contaminants. Correcting that signal still does not convert $S$ into <FRET efficiency> without a calibration of excitation, yields and detection.

A biological comparison also needs a pairing-negative preparation and a known transferring positive preparation. Match label abundance and localization where possible; changing <concentration> can change encounter frequency as well as brightness. Check that attaching the labels preserves the <protein> function being investigated. Otherwise the measurement can faithfully report a perturbation introduced by the labels themselves.

= FRET photobleaching controls
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

In <FRET photobleaching controls>, selectively destroy the <FRET acceptor>'s ability to absorb and test whether <FRET donor> <fluorescence> increases. Under unchanged excitation and detection, complete loss of transfer, and an intact <FRET donor>,
$$
E=1-\frac{I_{D,\rm before}}{I_{D,\rm after}}.
$$
This is a destructive comparison. Monitor acceptor loss, donor-only samples exposed to the bleaching illumination, and an unbleached reference region or time trace. Incomplete bleaching, donor damage, movement or label replenishment violate the comparison. During ordinary recordings, gradual unequal bleaching can instead produce an apparent change of <FRET> without a biological change.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.4.1.1–3.4.1.2: donor recovery, sample damage, acceptor replenishment and photoconversion artifacts]{ref}

= FRET proximity readout experiment
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

In <Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>, <Victor Sourjik> and <Howard C. Berg> used a cyan fluorescent tag on <CheZ> as <FRET donor> and a yellow tag on <CheY> as <FRET acceptor>. <Escherichia coli> <biological cells> adhered to a polylysine-coated coverslip in a continuously perfused chamber. A <fluorescence microscope> excited them with a $442$-nanometre <laser>; split emission bands fed two <photon>-counting detectors. The yellow/cyan emission <ratio> fell when attractant was added and rose when it was removed.<Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>[Introduction, Materials and Methods: Preparation of Cells and Data Acquisition; Results and Figure 1]{ref}

Saturating attractant increased cyan emission by an amount matching selective bleaching of the yellow tag: removing the <FRET acceptor> supported relief of <FRET donor> transfer. Strains carrying the tagged pair but lacking other <chemotaxis> machinery showed no stimulus-dependent emission <ratio> change. Weak illumination reduced bleaching drift.<Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>[Data Analysis and Results: acceptor bleaching comparison, low illumination, and flhC negative control]{ref}

The inference is a change in the population of nearby tagged <CheY>/<CheZ> pairs associated with <CheY> <phosphorylation>. It neither measures each pair's separation nor directly observes motor motion. The readout also averages many <biological cells>, and perfusion takes finite time. The pathway interpretation belongs to the <chemotaxis FRET assay>; the general measurement lesson is that molecular association can alter <FRET> while the geometry of an individual associated pair stays the same.

= Model organism
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <model organism> is an <organism> used to investigate biological processes through accessible, repeatable experiments. Its value combines <experimental tractability> with a carefully delimited relationship to other <organisms>. For example, a <gene deletion> in <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> can test whether a <gene> is required for growth under specified conditions; adding another <gene> can test <functional complementation>. Such interventions support <causal inference> more directly than merely noticing that a <gene> and a <phenotype> occur together. <Budding Yeast for Budding Geneticists: A Primer on the Saccharomyces cerevisiae Model System by Andrea A. Duina and others>[Duina, Miller and Keeney (2014), introduction and Experimental Toolkit and Related Resources: manipulation of genes and phenotypic effects]{ref}

The striking opportunity is to ask two experimentally separate questions: what does this component do here, and which part of that function survives a change of <species> or conditions? The <yeast cross-species complementation experiment> makes both questions concrete. <Model organism evidence transfer> requires specifying the function being compared; a useful <model organism> need not reproduce every feature of another <organism>.

= Arabidopsis thaliana
{parent=Model organism}
{c}

A flowering plant used to investigate <genetics>, <chloroplast> function and development.

= Experimental tractability
{parent=Model organism}

<Experimental tractability> is the practical ability to cultivate a system, manipulate the variables of interest, and obtain an interpretable readout with appropriate <scientific controls>. In <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>, inexpensive cultivation, accessible <haploid> and <diploid> states, and tools for introducing <DNA> make controlled changes of <genotype> feasible. A <haploid> <biological strain> exposes a changed <allele> without a second copy at that <genetic locus>, while crosses and <meiosis> permit tests of inheritance. <Budding Yeast for Budding Geneticists: A Primer on the Saccharomyces cerevisiae Model System by Andrea A. Duina and others>[Duina, Miller and Keeney (2014), introduction, Figure 2 and Experimental Toolkit and Related Resources]{ref}

An accessible readout is not necessarily a direct measurement of the mechanism. Growth of a <biological colony> integrates many cellular functions. Failure to grow could reflect the intended <gene deletion>, unsuitable conditions, failed <genetic transformation>, or another <mutation>. Matching the <genetic background> and using <scientific controls> narrows these alternatives. Ease of doing an experiment and strength of its inference are distinct advantages.

= Yeast cross-species complementation experiment
{parent=Model organism}

Can a <gene> from <Schizosaccharomyces pombe> replace a missing function in <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>? A modern laboratory project tested <functional complementation> of <methionine> synthesis. For each tested <gene deletion>, the investigators compared <plasmids> carrying the corresponding <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> <gene>, a candidate <Schizosaccharomyces pombe> <gene>, and an unrelated <gene>. The first was a positive <scientific control>; the last was a negative <scientific control>. The paper describes this design rather than publishing a complete set of individual growth measurements. <Pathways over Time: Functional Genomics Research in an Introductory Laboratory Course by Todd D. Reeves and others>[Reeves, Warner, Ludlow and O’Connor (2018), Course Activities, pp. 5–6 and Table 1: three plasmids, replica plating and protein-expression checks]{ref}

The recipient <biological strains> also lacked the ability to make <uracil>. The pYES2.1/V5-His <plasmids> carried URA3, a <gene> restoring that ability, and used the GAL1 <gene promoter> to control the inserted <gene>. Initial plates lacked <uracil> but supplied <methionine>: this selected <biological colonies> containing <plasmids> without requiring successful <methionine> synthesis. Subsequent plates lacking <methionine> tested the missing function, comparing <galactose induction> with <glucose> conditions. Thus selecting for a <plasmid> and testing its functional contribution were separate operations. <Pathways Over Time Plasmids Engage Students in Functional Genomics Research by Clare O’Connor>[O’Connor (2018), Plasmid features enable multiple student experiments and Complementation reveals conservation of function]{ref}

The apparatus can be simple and informative. In the associated protocol, <replica plating> uses a block covered with velveteen to transfer small samples of <biological colonies> from a master plate onto plates with different nutrient conditions. Orientation marks preserve correspondence between locations; gentle contact limits the transferred material. The specified replicas omit <uracil> and <methionine> with either inducing <sugar> or <glucose>, and include a fresh <glucose> plate supplying <methionine>. The readout is growth at corresponding locations, not a direct measurement of <enzyme> activity. <Investigations in Molecular Cell Biology by Clare M. O’Connor/12.6: Exercise 2 - Replica plating and complementation>[O’Connor, Investigations in Molecular Cell Biology, §12.6, introductory paragraphs and steps 1–3: velveteen block, orientation marks and three replica media]{ref}

\Image[yeast-gene-complementation-experimental-design.png]
{title=Separating <plasmid> selection from a <yeast> <functional complementation> test}
{description=Original schematic of the reported experimental design, not copied growth data. All three constructs enter the same <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> <gene deletion> background. Plate labels identify distinct comparisons; arrows show sample transfer, not observed rescue. The candidate <Schizosaccharomyces pombe> outcome must be measured.}

The <scientific controls> answer different objections. Growth on the <methionine>-supplied replica checks survival and transfer. Rescue by the native <gene> checks that this <biological strain> and assay can reveal <functional complementation>. The unrelated <gene> tests whether carrying a <plasmid> alone is sufficient. Comparing induction conditions checks dependence on <gene expression>, although changing the <sugar> also changes <metabolism>: it is not an isolated change of <gene expression> in every other respect.

If the candidate <gene> restores growth while those comparisons behave as intended, it supplies a function sufficient for the tested growth requirement in this recipient. Failure to rescue is less decisive: the candidate <protein> might not be produced, might be altered by its attached tag, or might not work in this cellular context. The investigators therefore also used <Western blots> to examine <protein> production. A detected <protein> still does not establish correct <protein folding> or catalytic activity. <Pathways over Time: Functional Genomics Research in an Introductory Laboratory Course by Todd D. Reeves and others>[Reeves et al. (2018), Course Activities, pp. 5–6: functional conservation, tag interference and Western-blot follow-up]{ref}

= Model organism evidence transfer
{parent=Model organism}

Evidence transfers at a stated level. <Sequence alignment> can suggest a candidate relationship between <genes>; <functional complementation> tests whether a candidate can supply a particular function in a chosen recipient. Neither alone establishes identical native <gene expression>, identical <signal transduction>, or identical consequences in every <species>. The <yeast cross-species complementation experiment> is especially useful because it tests a conserved function by intervention rather than assuming it from resemblance. <Pathways over Time: Functional Genomics Research in an Introductory Laboratory Course by Todd D. Reeves and others>[Reeves et al. (2018), project design, pp. 3–4, and Course Activities, pp. 5–6]{ref}

\Image[model-organism-evidence-transfer-boundaries.png]
{title=What a <model organism> experiment establishes, and what needs another test}
{description=Original evidence map using the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment>. Solid arrows indicate an experimental question that can be addressed in the recipient; dashed arrows mark additional claims requiring evidence. This is a schematic of inference boundaries, not a <phylogenetic tree>, a measured pathway, or a claim about medical outcomes.}

Even successful <functional complementation> occurs within a specific <genetic background>, nutrient supply and imposed <gene expression>. The <enzyme> may work with the recipient’s available <substrates> and partner <proteins> at an experimentally imposed abundance. Establishing the same role in the donor <species> calls for experiments there; establishing behavior under another condition calls for experiments under that condition. A conserved component can therefore coexist with context-dependent behavior of an entire pathway. This separation makes <model organisms> powerful: an accessible experiment supplies a precise mechanistic foothold and a better question for the next system.

\Include[organism]{parent=Biology}

= Taxonomy
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Taxonomy
{synonym}

<Taxonomy> names and classifies <organisms>. Classification can organize known diversity and make comparisons useful, but ranks and names are not themselves evidence of ancestry. <Phylogenetics> infers that ancestry; a <clade> includes an ancestor and all its descendants. A shared feature such as lacking a <cell nucleus> need not identify an exclusive <clade>. The <organism> branch here organizes cell types for reading and is not a proposed <phylogenetic tree>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/20-1-organizing-life-on-earth[OpenStax Biology 2e, 20.1: classification and evolutionary history].

= Domain
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Taxonomy (biology)}
{wiki}

A biological <domain (biology)> is a high-level rank used to classify <organisms> in the <three-domain system>. Distinguish a taxonomic rank from a primary branch in an inferred <phylogenetic tree>: retaining familiar names does not settle the branching relationships.

= Three-domain system
{parent=Domain (biology)}
{wiki}

The <three-domain system> recognizes <bacteria>, <archaea> and <eukaryotes> as three domains. In 1990, Woese, Kandler and Wheelis proposed the domain rank and the names Bacteria, Archaea and Eucarya, using molecular comparisons to argue that the older <prokaryote>/<eukaryote> division hid important differences. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2112744/[Woese, Kandler and Wheelis, 1990: Towards a natural system of organisms].

The associated three-domain ancestry model places the nuclear lineage of <eukaryotes> alongside <archaea>, rather than within them. This historically influential interpretation should be distinguished from the <two-domain system>; listing three familiar groups does not establish three independent primary branches.

= Two-domain system
{parent=Domain (biology)}
{wiki}

The <two-domain system> recognizes <bacteria> and <archaea> as the two primary lineages, placing the nuclear ancestry of <eukaryotes> within <archaea>. In this model, <archaea> as a complete <clade> includes that <eukaryotic> lineage; restricting the name to <prokaryotic> members describes a different grouping. Williams and colleagues' study, published online in 2019 and in a 2020 journal issue, found support for this model and a close relationship to <Asgard archaea>. See https://doi.org/10.1038/s41559-019-1040-x[Williams et al., 2020: Phylogenomics provides robust support for a two-domains tree of life].

This updates the interpretation of the 1977 and 1990 classifications rather than undoing their discovery of deep differences between <bacteria> and <archaea>. Deep <phylogenetic trees> depend on the sampled <genes>, <organisms> and inference model; the precise closest archaeal relatives and early sequence of events require further evidence. Bacterial ancestry of <mitochondria> through <endosymbiosis> is a separate part of <eukaryotic> history.

= Species
{parent=Taxonomy (biology)}
{wiki}

A <species> is a named unit of biological classification. The useful criteria vary among groups: reproductive compatibility can inform classification in many <animals>, while microbial comparisons also use <genomes> and other characters. <Escherichia coli> is a <species>; K-12 MG1655 identifies a particular strain within it. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/18-2-formation-of-new-species[OpenStax Biology 2e, 18.2: species concepts].

= Species concept
{parent=Species}

A <species concept> states what should make <organisms> members of the same <species>; an operational criterion states what observations will test that claim. <Reproductive isolation>, distinguishable <morphology> and ancestry inferred through <phylogenetics> answer different questions. In a lineage-based interpretation, they are evidence for separation of evolving <biological populations>, and need not appear together. <Species Concepts and Species Delimitation by Kevin de Queiroz>[pp. 880–883, Table 1, Figure 1 and “Conceptualization versus Delimitation”: distinguish defining properties from operational evidence]{ref}

= Biological species concept
{parent=Species concept}

The <biological species concept> groups naturally interbreeding <biological populations> with viable, fertile offspring, distinguished from other such groups by <reproductive isolation>. An operational test examines mating, <fertilization>, offspring survival and offspring <reproduction>, rather than only whether two adults can be paired. <Sexual reproduction> is its scope: <fossils> and <organisms> studied only through <asexual reproduction> cannot supply the same mating evidence. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[§18.2, “Species and the Ability to Reproduce”, “Speciation” and “Reproductive Isolation”]{ref}

For an original hypothetical example, imagine two separated <biological populations> that reproduce successfully after contact. The separation alone did not demonstrate <reproductive isolation>. Conversely, failure to mate in one unfamiliar chamber could reflect the assay conditions. Field observations of <gene flow> and tests under suitable conditions address the biological question more directly. A successful laboratory pairing does not establish frequent interbreeding in nature.

= Morphological species concept
{parent=Species concept}

A <morphological species concept> distinguishes <species> using <morphology>: measurable differences in form or structure. It can be applied to preserved specimens and <fossils> when mating cannot be observed. Similar appearance need not imply the same <species>, and different appearance need not imply different <species>. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[§18.2, “Species and the Ability to Reproduce”, paragraphs accompanying Figures 18.9–18.10: appearance can mislead]{ref}

An original operational example is to compare a chosen structure in specimens of comparable age, sex and rearing conditions. Suppose two apparent groups differ in size, but one group contains only juveniles. The observed difference does not yet diagnose two <species>. Specify the measured <phenotype>, variation within each sample, and whether the distinction persists across comparable specimens. A single striking specimen is a poor substitute for that comparison.

= Phylogenetic species concept
{parent=Species concept}

<Phylogenetic species concepts> use ancestry and diagnostic inherited differences; versions differ over requiring a <clade>, an exclusive ancestry pattern at <genetic loci>, or a distinguishable character combination. State which criterion is intended. <Species Concepts and Species Delimitation by Kevin de Queiroz>[p. 880, Table 1: distinct monophyletic, genealogical and diagnosable versions of phylogenetic criteria]{ref}

For an original operational example, suppose a <phylogenetic tree> inferred from one <genetic locus> separates two sampled <biological populations>. Compare further <genetic loci> and additional local samples before interpreting that branch as a <species> boundary. Otherwise the test may merely identify the particular ancestry of the sampled <alleles>. Adding more sites from the same <genetic locus> improves that locus's evidence without creating an independent ancestry test.

= Species delimitation
{parent=Species}

<Species delimitation> infers the boundaries and number of <species> from evidence. No single arbitrary percentage of <nucleotide sequence> difference is a universal boundary. Divergence within an old, widespread <species> can exceed divergence between recently separated <species>; geography and multiple kinds of evidence matter. <Species Concepts and Species Delimitation by Kevin de Queiroz>[pp. 884–885, “Alternatives to the Traditional Properties”: divergence cutoffs and geographic differentiation]{ref}

An original counterexample to a universal percentage rule: an assay could examine a strongly conserved <gene> in one comparison and a rapidly changing <nucleotide sequence> in another. Equal percentages then answer different historical questions. Even the denominator needs specifying: all sites, aligned sites, or only a filtered region? A threshold is an operational rule for a stated assay and group of <organisms>, whose errors need evaluating. It is not a definition that automatically transfers to every <species>.

\Image[species-evidence-criteria.png]
{title=Contrasting operational evidence for <species delimitation>}
{description=Original hypothetical comparison of <morphology>, mating and ancestry evidence. Each observation tests a different claim; the combinations are thought experiments, not a dataset or verdicts about named <species>. “Not tested” does not mean absent.}

The diagram's hypothetical pair B has similar <morphology> but reduced mating and distinct ancestry evidence. A <morphological species concept> could miss that distinction. Pair C instead has different <morphology> but compatible mating and ancestry that fails to separate the samples. That combination calls for checking variation in <phenotype> within a <species>. Pair A has only a structural difference observed: the other criteria remain untested. These examples show why missing evidence, disagreeing evidence and agreeing evidence require different interpretations.

= Species, populations and strains
{parent=Species}

A <species> can encompass many <biological populations>. A <biological population> specifies a set of <organisms> in a place and time; a <biological strain> identifies a maintained variant or lineage, often in a laboratory. A <species> name, a local census and a <biological strain> designation therefore describe different things. Multiple independently maintained cultures can also share the same <biological strain> designation.

For example, a culture identified as <Escherichia coli> K-12 MG1655 specifies more than the <species> alone. Two flasks inoculated from that culture are separate experimental <biological populations>; the separate containers do not establish two <species> or two different <biological strains>. This is an original experimental bookkeeping example, not a claim that every <biological strain> name encodes a complete <genotype>.

= Morphology
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Morphology
{synonym}

<Morphology> concerns biological form and structure; a structural difference is one aspect of <phenotype>, not automatically a <species> boundary.

= Allometry
{parent=Morphology (biology)}

Scaling of a measured trait with body size or another dimension. Size correction can change apparent ecological differences.

= Morphospace
{parent=Morphology (biology)}

A space defined by measured or theoretical shape variables. Distance in this space is not automatically ecological niche distance.

= Functional morphology
{parent=Morphology (biology)}

Study of relationships between anatomical construction and mechanical or physiological function.

= Ecomorphology
{parent=Morphology (biology)}

Study of relationships between organismal form and ecological performance or resource use.

= Body size
{parent=Morphology (biology)}

The size of an organism, operationalized through a measure such as mass or a specified linear dimension. Comparative measurements require consistent definitions and allowance for growth.

= Developmental biology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Maternal-to-zygotic transition
{parent=Developmental biology}

The transition from predominant reliance on maternally supplied products to zygotic <gene expression>, accompanied by regulated turnover or redeployment of maternal molecules.

= Implantation
{parent=Developmental biology}

Attachment and embedding of a mammalian conceptus in maternal tissue. Embryonic and extraembryonic tissues undergo coordinated <morphogenesis> during this transition.

= Plant development
{parent=Developmental biology}

Formation and differentiation of plant tissues and organs, continuously influenced by internal state and the environment.

= Root nodule
{parent=Plant development}

A specialized plant organ hosting symbiotic nitrogen fixation in compatible associations. Nodule initiation, bacterial infection and effective nitrogen fixation are separable outcomes.

= Nodulation
{parent=Root nodule}

Formation of root nodules. More nodules do not necessarily imply more effective nitrogen fixation or greater host benefit.

= Autoregulation of nodulation
{parent=Nodulation}

Systemic feedback restricting additional nodulation after symbiotic signaling has begun. Root-to-shoot and shoot-to-root communication coordinates local initiation with whole-plant status.

= TML
{parent=Autoregulation of nodulation}
{c}

TOO MUCH LOVE, a root-localized negative regulator of symbiotic susceptibility targeted by <miR2111> in Lotus japonicus.

= HAR1
{parent=Autoregulation of nodulation}
{c}

HYPERNODULATION ABERRANT ROOT FORMATION1, a Lotus japonicus receptor kinase with a shoot role in systemic restriction of nodulation.

= Heart development
{parent=Developmental biology}

= Myocardium
{parent=Heart development}

The muscular tissue of the heart. Regional myocardial differentiation and growth are distinct from endocardial or supporting-cell development.

= Atrioventricular canal
{parent=Heart development}

The region connecting atrial and ventricular compartments during cardiac development. Its regulatory programme differs from that of rapidly expanding chamber <myocardium>.

= Chamber myocardium
{parent=Heart development}

<myocardium> acquiring chamber-associated growth, structural and electrophysiological properties, distinct from specialized non-chamber territories.

= Cardiac looping
{parent=Heart development}

Three-dimensional bending and torsion of the early <heart tube>. Geometry, left–right patterning, tissue growth and regional <cell differentiation> contribute distinct aspects.

= Heart tube
{parent=Heart development}

An early tubular cardiac structure whose construction includes tissue convergence, differentiation and continued addition of cells at its poles.

= Venous pole of the heart
{parent=Heart tube}

The inflow end of the developing heart, with progenitors contributing to inflow and atrial regions under species- and stage-specific conditions.

= Arterial pole of the heart
{parent=Heart tube}

The outflow end of the developing heart, receiving progenitor contributions on a stage-specific schedule.

= Cardiac crescent
{parent=Heart development}

An early arrangement of cardiogenic tissue before formation of the linear <heart tube> in the mammalian embryo.

= First heart field
{parent=Heart development}

An early cardiac progenitor domain that differentiates into much of the initial <myocardium>, with major contributions to the left ventricle in mouse. Its boundaries and contributions require stage- and species-specific <fate mapping>.

= Homeotic transformation
{parent=Developmental biology}

Replacement of one position-specific developmental identity by another. Demonstrating it requires positive evidence for the alternative identity as well as loss of the original features.

= Developmental timing
{parent=Developmental biology}

The timing and ordering of specification, differentiation, tissue movement and maturation. Equal chronological age need not imply equal developmental stage after perturbation.

= Lung development
{parent=Developmental biology}

= Fetal breathing movement
{parent=Lung development}

Movement before air breathing that contributes to fluid-related mechanical stimulation of the developing lung. Physical loading and mature respiratory function are distinct.

= Alveolar epithelium
{parent=Lung development}

The epithelial lining of pulmonary gas-exchange units, containing differently specialized cell types.

= Type II alveolar cell
{parent=Alveolar epithelium}

An alveolar <epithelial cell> with secretory functions including surfactant production and context-dependent progenitor activity.

= Type I alveolar cell
{parent=Alveolar epithelium}

A thin, extended <epithelial cell> contributing to the gas-exchange surface. Flattening and molecular differentiation should be measured separately.

= Skin development
{parent=Developmental biology}

= Feather placode
{parent=Skin development}

A localized epithelial primordium of a feather. Its molecular identity and underlying dermal aggregation can be coupled through mechanical and chemical signals.

= Developmental competence
{parent=Developmental biology}

The ability of a cell or tissue to respond to an instructive signal at a particular stage and location. The same signal can have different effects after competence changes.

= Neural development
{parent=Developmental biology}

Formation and organization of nervous-system cells and their connections.

= Neural progenitor cell
{parent=Neural development}

A precursor able to produce one or more neural <cell types>. <multipotency> is a property of an individual progenitor under tested conditions, not merely of a mixed population.

= Radial glial cell
{parent=Neural progenitor cell}

A neural progenitor with radial processes and context-dependent neuronal or glial outputs. <cell> <morphology>, molecular state and traced descendants provide complementary evidence for its role.

= Neuronal differentiation
{parent=Neural development}

Acquisition of neuronal identity and function. Commitment, subtype specification, maturation and survival should be measured separately rather than inferred from one neuronal marker.

= Axon guidance
{parent=Neural development}

Processes directing growing axons to appropriate regions or targets.

= Epicardium
{parent=Developmental biology}

The outer tissue layer covering the heart, with developmental contributions to supporting cells and signals affecting myocardial and coronary development.

= Endocardium
{parent=Developmental biology}

The inner endothelial lining of the heart, which also provides developmental signals to neighbouring myocardium.

= Second heart field
{parent=Developmental biology}

A population of cardiac progenitors that contributes cells to the developing heart after initial heart-tube formation. Retaining a proliferative reservoir allows progressive addition of differentiated myocardium.

= Regeneration
{parent=Developmental biology}

Replacement of lost biological structures through organized growth and differentiation. Recovery of mass alone need not demonstrate restoration of the original pattern or function.

= Blastema
{parent=Regeneration}

A regeneration-associated accumulation of progenitor cells. A blastema need not be a homogeneous pluripotent population; contributors can retain tissue and positional restrictions.

= Positional information
{parent=Developmental biology}

Information allowing a cell to respond according to its location within a developing or regenerating structure. Establishing a positional value and interpreting it as a differentiated outcome are distinct steps.

= Positional memory
{parent=Positional information}

Persistence of a position-associated cellular identity after the original spatial context or instructive signal changes. It is distinct from simply retaining a differentiated tissue identity.

= Intestinal development
{parent=Developmental biology}

Formation, regionalization and maturation of the <intestine>, including its epithelial surface, supporting tissues and renewing cell populations.

= Villus morphogenesis
{parent=Intestinal development}

Formation of epithelial villi through interactions among growth, tissue constraints and local signalling. The ordering of these events differs between species.

= Mesenchyme
{parent=Developmental biology}

A tissue organization with cells dispersed through <extracellular matrix>, rather than arranged as a continuous polarized <epithelium>. Mesenchymal populations differ in origin and developmental potential.
