= Fossil
{parent=Paleontology}
{wiki}

\Include[ecology]{parent=Biology}
\Include[protein]{parent=Biology}

= Transitional fossil
{parent=Fossil}

A <fossil> with a combination of ancestral and derived characters informative about an evolutionary transition. It need not be the direct ancestor of a named living group.

= Missing link
{parent=Transitional fossil}

A historical metaphor for a predicted but unsampled evolutionary connection. Modern phylogenetic hypotheses concern branching relationships and character changes rather than one obligatory intermediate.

= Trace fossil
{parent=Fossil}

Preserved evidence of biological activity, such as a track or burrow. It can reveal movement or feeding even without body remains, but usually does not uniquely identify the producer.

= Rhynie chert
{parent=Fossil}
{c}

A fossil deposit preserving early land-plant anatomy, including reproductive structures interpreted as <gametophytes> and <sporophytes>.<The Gametophyte Generation of Some Early Devonian Land Plants by Winfried Remy and others>[Anatomy and proposed generation associations]{ref}

= Morphogen
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <morphogen> is a signal whose spatial <concentration> helps specify different <biological cell> fates or behaviors during <biological development>. A <morphogen gradient> provides a positional cue: <biological cells> at different distances from a source experience different local <concentrations> and can follow different developmental pathways. A spatial <concentration> variation alone does not establish that a substance is a <morphogen>; its graded developmental effects matter. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26825/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition (2002): morphogens as graded developmental signals].

The beautiful idea is that one distributed signal can help organize several regions of a developing body. A continuous <morphogen gradient> can supply the input for distinct domains of <gene expression>, rather than requiring a separate signal for every position. A <morphogen response threshold> is a useful idealization of that interpretation: high, intermediate and low <concentrations> are assigned different outcomes. The <morphogen reaction-diffusion equation> describes one way to generate a <morphogen gradient>; the equation is a model of transport and reaction, while the <morphogen> is the signal being transported.

Detection and interpretation also depend on the receiving <biological cell>. For an extracellular <morphogen>, <receptors> and <signal transduction> connect the local <concentration> to a response; <receptor binding occupancy> is only one step in that connection. The same <morphogen gradient> can be interpreted differently in different tissues because their developmental context differs. Thus a <morphogen response threshold> summarizes a particular response in a particular context, rather than being a universal property of the signal. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9968/[Scott F. Gilbert, Developmental Biology, 6th edition (2000): positional information and context-dependent interpretation].

An important experimental example came in 1988. Wolfgang Driever and Christiane Nüsslein-Volhard used antibodies to examine the bicoid <protein> in early fruit-fly embryos. They reported a <concentration> profile approximately described by an <exponential function>, highest at the front, with the <protein> concentrated in <cell nuclei>. Their abstract proposed <diffusion> from localized <messenger RNA> and dispersed degradation as a likely origin of the <morphogen gradient>, rather than proving a unique transport mechanism. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3383244/[Driever and Nüsslein-Volhard, A gradient of bicoid protein in Drosophila embryos, Cell 54 (1988), 83–93: primary abstract]{ref}

In a companion study, Driever and Nüsslein-Volhard genetically changed the amount and distribution of bicoid <messenger RNA>, measured the resulting <protein> <morphogen gradient>, and compared developmental maps. Increased local bicoid <protein> levels shifted prospective anterior structures toward the rear; decreased levels shifted them toward the front. This linked a measured <concentration> profile to positional effects in <biological development>. The toy calculations below illustrate that logic with arbitrary numbers, not measured bicoid parameters. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3383245/[Driever and Nüsslein-Volhard, The bicoid protein determines position in the Drosophila embryo in a concentration-dependent manner, Cell 54 (1988), 95–104: primary abstract]{ref}

= Retinoic acid
{parent=Morphogen}

A signalling derivative of vitamin A involved in stage- and tissue-dependent patterning.

= Sonic hedgehog
{parent=Morphogen}
{c}

A signalling protein important in <vertebrate> pattern formation, including the <limb>. Signal duration, position and responding tissue affect interpretation of digit-patterning experiments.

= ZRS enhancer
{parent=Sonic hedgehog}
{c}

A long-range regulatory element contributing to limb expression of Sonic hedgehog. Evolutionary changes in its activity provide a testable example of altered developmental deployment.

= Morphogen gradient
{parent=Morphogen}
{title2=$c(x)$}

A <morphogen gradient> is a spatially varying <concentration> profile of a <morphogen>. The profile $c(x)$ specifies the local amount; its mathematical <gradient>, $c'(x)$ in one dimension, specifies how rapidly that amount changes with position. In the simplest positional interpretation, a <biological cell> responds to its local <concentration>, without having to measure the <gradient> across itself. Production, transport and removal together determine a <morphogen gradient>.

For a worked example, take a stationary one-dimensional tissue occupying $x\geq0$, constant <diffusion coefficient> $D>0$, and uniform <first-order reaction> removal at rate $k>0$. At <steady state>, this specialization of the <morphogen reaction-diffusion equation> gives the <ordinary differential equation> and <boundary conditions>
$$
D\frac{d^2c}{dx^2}-kc=0,\qquad c(0)=c_0,\qquad \lim_{x\to\infty}c(x)=0.
$$
Here $D$ has units of length squared per time, $k$ has units of inverse time, and $c_0$ is a prescribed source <concentration>. The <Dirichlet boundary condition> fixes the <concentration> at $x=0$. It does not prescribe a source <flux>; any required <flux> follows from the solution. These assumptions define a toy <morphogen gradient>, not a universal account of transport during <biological development>.

Define the <diffusion length> $\ell=\sqrt{D/k}$. Substitution into the <ordinary differential equation> shows that its general solution is $A e^{x/\ell}+B e^{-x/\ell}$. The vanishing <boundary condition> at infinity forces $A=0$, and the source <Dirichlet boundary condition> forces $B=c_0$. Hence
$$
c(x)=c_0e^{-x/\ell},\qquad \ell=\sqrt{D/k}.
$$
Over every additional <diffusion length>, the <concentration> falls by the same factor $e^{-1}$. Faster <diffusion> increases $\ell$; faster removal decreases $\ell$. This makes the <diffusion length> a useful bridge between microscopic transport and the spatial reach of a <morphogen gradient>.

Choose $c_0=100$ arbitrary <concentration> units and $\ell=100\,\mu\mathrm m$, where $1\,\mu\mathrm m=10^{-6}\,\mathrm m$. Then $c(x)=100e^{-x/(100\,\mu\mathrm m)}$: at $x=0$, $100\,\mu\mathrm m$ and $200\,\mu\mathrm m$, the <concentrations> are $100$, approximately $36.79$ and approximately $13.53$. The following diagram combines this <morphogen gradient> with the idealized responses calculated under <morphogen response threshold>.

\Image[morphogen-gradient-and-thresholds.png]
{title=A toy <morphogen gradient> and three idealized developmental bands}
{description=The <steady state> profile uses $c_0=100$ arbitrary <concentration> units and <diffusion length> $100\,\mu\mathrm m$. <Morphogen response thresholds> at $50$ and $20$ place the boundaries at approximately $69.31$ and $160.94\,\mu\mathrm m$. The lower strip shows assigned fates A, B and C, not measured tissues. The plotted domain ends at $350\,\mu\mathrm m$; the mathematical domain continues to infinity.}

= Morphogen source-diffusion-degradation model
{parent=Morphogen gradient}
{title2=$\lambda=\sqrt{D/k}$}

An idealized model balancing localized production, <diffusion> and degradation. In one dimension at <steady state>, away from the source, $Dc^{\prime\prime}-kc=0$ gives $c(x)=c_0e^{-x/\lambda}$ with $\lambda=\sqrt{D/k}$. Tissue geometry and time-dependent source distributions can invalidate a simple point-source interpretation.

= Morphogen response threshold
{parent=Morphogen}
{title2=$\theta$}

A <morphogen response threshold> is a <concentration> level used to distinguish developmental responses to a <morphogen>. In an idealized threshold model, otherwise equivalent <biological cells> exposed to $c\geq\theta$ take one response and those exposed to $c<\theta$ take another. Two <morphogen response thresholds> can assign three bands: fate A for $c\geq50$, fate B for $20\leq c<50$, and fate C for $c<20$, all in the toy arbitrary <concentration> units. The A/B/C labels specify a response rule, not named cell types or a claim that every <morphogen> produces sharp boundaries. See the positional-information model in Gilbert's reference under <morphogen>.

For the decreasing <morphogen gradient> $c(x)=c_0e^{-x/\ell}$ and $0<\theta<c_0$, solve $c(x_\theta)=\theta$ using the <natural logarithm>:
$$
x_\theta=\ell\ln\!\left(\frac{c_0}{\theta}\right).
$$
With $c_0=100$ and <diffusion length> $\ell=100\,\mu\mathrm m$, the <morphogen response thresholds> $50$ and $20$ give
$$
x_{50}=100\ln2\,\mu\mathrm m\approx69.3147\,\mu\mathrm m,\qquad x_{20}=100\ln5\,\mu\mathrm m\approx160.9438\,\mu\mathrm m.
$$
Thus fate A occupies $0\leq x\leq x_{50}$, fate B occupies $x_{50}<x\leq x_{20}$, and fate C occupies $x>x_{20}$ in this idealization. The equality convention belongs to the response rule. If $\theta=c_0$, the crossing is at the source; if $\theta>c_0$, that <morphogen response threshold> is never reached anywhere in the tissue.

Now double the prescribed source <concentration> from $c_0$ to $2c_0$, keeping $D$, $k$, <diffusion length> $\ell$ and a fixed <morphogen response threshold> $0<\theta<c_0$ unchanged. The whole <morphogen gradient> doubles, but the boundary moves by an additive distance:
$$
\Delta x_\theta=\ell\ln\!\left(\frac{2c_0}{\theta}\right)-\ell\ln\!\left(\frac{c_0}{\theta}\right)=\ell\ln2.
$$
For $\theta=20$, the crossing moves from $160.9438\,\mu\mathrm m$ to $100\ln10\,\mu\mathrm m\approx230.2585\,\mu\mathrm m$, a shift of $69.3147\,\mu\mathrm m$. Every fixed <morphogen response threshold> below the original source <concentration> shifts by this same distance in the toy model. The example compares two <Dirichlet boundary conditions>, $c(0)=100$ and $c(0)=200$; doubling an experimentally supplied source <flux> is a different boundary specification.

\Image[morphogen-source-strength-boundary-shift.png]
{title=Doubling source <concentration> shifts a toy <morphogen response threshold> boundary}
{description=Profiles with source <concentrations> $100$ and $200$ share <diffusion length> $100\,\mu\mathrm m$. The same <morphogen response threshold> $20$ is crossed at approximately $160.94$ and $230.26\,\mu\mathrm m$. The arrow marks the predicted $69.31\,\mu\mathrm m$ shift. Source strength here means prescribed boundary <concentration>, with all numbers arbitrary and illustrative.}

These calculations are useful because they separate changes in source <concentration>, <diffusion length> and response rule. Their limits are equally concrete: growing tissues change the geometry, a <transient response> can precede <steady state>, molecular fluctuations can blur a boundary, and <signaling feedback> or altered transport can change the <morphogen gradient> or its interpretation. A local instantaneous <morphogen response threshold> need not capture exposure duration, <signaling adaptation> or the receiving <biological cell>'s state. Experiments on cultured human <biological cells> found different responses to the level and rate of change of developmental signals, illustrating why signal dynamics can matter. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6398983/[Heemskerk et al., Rapid changes in morphogen concentration control self-organized patterning in human embryonic stem cells, eLife 8 (2019), e40526: primary experiments]{ref} Quantitative measurements of bicoid <protein> and its downstream <gene expression> also make precision a question for experiment, rather than something guaranteed by drawing threshold lines. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2253670/[Gregor et al., Probing the limits to positional information, Cell 130 (2007), 153–164: primary measurements]{ref} <Morphogen gradients> provide one mechanism for spatial patterning in <biological development>, alongside other signals and interactions.

= Twin
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Twins are two offspring from the same gestation. <Monozygotic twins> arise from one fertilized egg, while <dizygotic twins> arise from two. The distinction supplies the biological premise for <twin studies>.

= Biosignature
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <biosignature> is observable chemical, morphological or other evidence attributable to biological activity after assessing plausible abiotic explanations. It can concern present or past life on Earth or elsewhere. An <exoplanet biosignature> is the remote planetary specialization, requiring atmospheric and environmental context.

= Mortality
{parent=Biology}

Mortality describes deaths in a defined population during a specified observation window. A cumulative death <probability> is distinct from a death rate per person-time. Comparing treatments requires the same endpoint definition, time origin and ascertainment in both arms.

\Include[neuroscience]{parent=Biology}
\Include[enzyme]{parent=Biology}

= Flow cytometry
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Flow cytometry> records signals from suspended particles passing an illuminated interrogation region. For a <flow cytometric DNA-content assay>, a <laser> excites a <DNA>-binding dye and a detector records its <fluorescence>. An event is a recorded passage, not a guarantee that exactly one intact <biological cell> was measured.

= Flow cytometric DNA-content assay
{parent=Flow cytometry}

Under controlled staining and detector settings, integrated <fluorescence> can be approximately proportional to nuclear <DNA> amount. A <histogram> of accepted single-cell events then separates a $2C$ region, an intermediate region and a $4C$ region. These are ordinarily assigned to <G0 phase>/<G1 phase>, <S phase>, and <G2 phase>/<M phase> respectively in the stated <diploid> model.

\Image[dna-content-assay-illustrative-peaks.png]
{title=Illustrative <DNA>-content assay peaks: equal amount need not mean equal <cell cycle> stage}
{description=Original synthetic curves, not measured data or a fit to an experiment. The horizontal axis is a proportional <fluorescence> scale expressed in $C$ units for a reference <diploid> population. Component shapes and weights are arbitrary. The $2C$ peak combines <G0 phase> and <G1 phase>; the $4C$ peak combines <G2 phase> and <M phase>. The plotted curve cannot itself distinguish these paired states.}

A Cornell protocol fixes a single-cell suspension by adding cold 70% <ethanol> dropwise while mixing, reducing clumping. After washing, its staining solution contains propidium iodide at $50\,\mathrm{\mu g/ml}$; <ribonuclease A> removes interfering <RNA>. It specifies excitation at $488\,\mathrm{nm}$ and emission around $600\,\mathrm{nm}$. The resulting readout is <fluorescence> per event, not a direct count of <chromosomes>. <DNA Cell Cycle Analysis with PI>[DNA Cell Cycle Analysis with PI, p. 1: dye properties, staining steps 1–7 and reagent recipe]{ref}

Useful controls include an unstained sample for background and a reference population with known nuclear <DNA> amount, processed with identical settings. Two attached $2C$ <biological cells> can mimic one $4C$ event. Comparing signal integral with pulse height or width helps exclude such doublets; a gate selecting intact single particles also limits debris. These are proposed assay checks, not measurements underlying the illustration.

A $4C$ peak cannot distinguish <G2 phase> from <mitosis>; a $2C$ peak cannot distinguish <G1 phase> from <G0 phase>. Mixed <ploidy>, uneven staining and aggregates can invalidate phase assignments. Peak areas describe the accepted sample at collection, and do not by themselves establish phase durations, division rates or a particular arrest mechanism. A treatment comparison needs matched handling and another readout appropriate to the claimed mechanism.

\Include[biological-cell]{parent=Biology}

= Metabolism
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

The network of <chemical reactions> that maintains a living system, supplies energy and builds or processes its material. Gas emissions can arise as <primary metabolic byproducts> or <secondary metabolic byproducts>, with different implications for an <atmospheric biosignature gas>.

<Enzymes> make this network operate on biologically useful timescales. Following material through successive <substrates> and <reaction products> explains why a single fast <enzyme> need not make the entire network fast: another step may constrain the flow. Changing <enzyme> amounts or activity therefore changes which routes can carry material, connecting molecular <chemical kinetics> to the maintenance and growth of a <biological cell>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: multienzyme complexes and cell metabolism].

= Biosynthesis
{parent=Metabolism}

Formation of biological molecules through <enzyme>-catalysed metabolic reactions.

= Phenazine biosynthesis
{parent=Biosynthesis}

Formation and modification of phenazine metabolites. In Pseudomonas aeruginosa, two core phz loci generate precursors modified by additional enzymes.

= Basal metabolic rate
{parent=Metabolism}

A standardized minimum metabolic rate measured under stated resting, postabsorptive and thermal conditions; it is not interchangeable with maximum aerobic performance.

= Glycerol phosphate shuttle
{parent=Metabolism}

A route transferring cytosolic reducing equivalents through glycerol-phosphate dehydrogenases to mitochondrial electron transport. Its entry point differs from matrix NADH oxidation.

= Malate-aspartate shuttle
{parent=Metabolism}

A coupled reaction and transport system transferring reducing equivalents between cytosol and mitochondrial matrix without transporting NADH itself across the inner membrane.

= Diauxic growth
{parent=Metabolism}

A growth pattern with successive utilization of substrates separated by an adaptation interval. Glucose followed by lactose is a classic example, but timing depends on culture conditions and transport regulation.

= Endothermy
{parent=Metabolism}

Production of metabolic heat sufficient to contribute substantially to body-temperature regulation. It is not synonymous with constant <temperature>, and similar thermophysiology does not by itself identify a <clade>.

= Metabolic control analysis
{parent=Metabolism}

Quantitative analysis of how local changes in reaction capacities alter steady-state metabolic fluxes and concentrations. Its sensitivities depend on the operating condition rather than identifying one invariant limiting step.

= Flux summation theorem
{parent=Metabolic control analysis}

Under the scaling assumptions of <metabolic control analysis>, the complete set of independently scaled reaction-capacity <flux control coefficients> for one steady-state flux sums to one. Uniformly scaling all capacities scales the flux, and differentiation with respect to the scale factor gives the sum.

= Flux control coefficient
{parent=Metabolic control analysis}
{title2=$C_i^J$}

A local logarithmic sensitivity of a steady-state flux to a specified reaction capacity: $C_i^J=\partial\ln J/\partial\ln E_i$. It is not the fraction of flux physically passing through that step; a zero value does not make an essential step dispensable.

= Sulfate activation
{parent=Metabolism}

Conversion of <sulfate> to activated derivatives used in subsequent metabolic reactions. Its compartmentalization in <Entamoeba histolytica> <mitosomes> demonstrates lineage-specific retention of organellar function.

= Ethanol
{parent=Metabolism}
{tag=Chemical compound}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{C}_2\mathrm{H}_5\mathrm{OH}$}

<Ethanol> is the <chemical compound> produced by the <alcoholic fermentation> described under <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>. Its two <carbon> <chemical atoms> explain the product bookkeeping there. See https://webbook.nist.gov/cgi/cbook.cgi?ID=C64175&Mask=4[NIST Chemistry WebBook: ethanol formula and identity]; the Biology 2e section cited under <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> identifies <ethanol> as the alcohol product.

= Fermentation
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

In its biochemical sense, <fermentation> is <metabolism> that obtains <ATP> from breakdown of organic <substrates> without a respiratory electron transport chain. The alcoholic route is one example; not every <fermentation> produces <ethanol>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/7-5-metabolism-without-oxygen[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 7.5: fermentation versus anaerobic respiration].

= Hindgut fermentation
{parent=Fermentation}

Microbial digestion in enlarged caecal or colonic regions after the principal small-intestinal digestive region. Intake, particle sorting and retention affect its consequences for nutrition.

= Cecotrophy
{parent=Hindgut fermentation}

Reingestion of selected caecal material, allowing a later digestive passage to recover nutrients including microbial products. It is more specific than indiscriminate faeces consumption.

= Foregut fermentation
{parent=Fermentation}

Microbial digestion in an enlarged compartment before the principal acid and enzymatic digestive regions. Independent specializations occur in <ruminants>, <colobine monkeys> and <macropods>.

= Rumination
{parent=Foregut fermentation}

Return of previously swallowed material to the mouth for further chewing, followed by reswallowing. It helps expose plant material to microbial attack.

= Ethanol fermentation
{parent=Fermentation}
{wiki}

= Alcoholic fermentation
{synonym}

<Alcoholic fermentation> produces <ethanol> and <carbon dioxide> from <sugars> through an <enzyme>-catalyzed pathway. See <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> for its ideal <glucose> balance and <yeast> for the distinction between living cells and active extracts.

= Glycogen
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Glycogen> is a highly branched <polysaccharide> of <glucose> units, serving as a storage form of <glucose> in animals. Liver and muscle provide familiar examples. The classical <second messenger> experiments measured regulation of an <enzyme> involved in <glycogen> breakdown, <glycogen phosphorylase>. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-2-carbohydrates[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 3.2: glycogen]{ref}

= Glycogenesis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Glycogenesis> builds <glycogen>; <glycogenolysis> breaks it down. Their opposing directions matter in the <insulin> and <glucagon> circuit.

= Glycogenolysis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

= Gluconeogenesis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Gluconeogenesis> synthesizes <glucose> from non-<carbohydrate> precursors. It is distinct from <glycogenolysis>, which mobilizes an existing <glycogen> store.

= Lipolysis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

= Photosynthesis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Photosynthesis> captures <light> <energy> and transforms it into chemical forms that a <biological cell> can use. In <plants>, <algae> and <cyanobacteria>, this supports <carbon fixation>: incorporation of <carbon> from <carbon dioxide> into <chemical compounds> used to build living material. Capturing <light> and incorporating <carbon> are distinct processes coupled through <metabolism>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-1-overview-of-photosynthesis[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.1: overview of photosynthesis].

For <oxygenic photosynthesis>, a simplified net equation writes a six-<carbon> <sugar>, <glucose>, as the representative product:
$$
6\mathrm{CO}_2+6\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}+\text{light}\longrightarrow\mathrm{C}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}\mathrm{O}_6+6\mathrm{O}_2.
$$
This is net <stoichiometry>, not a one-step mechanism. A worked bookkeeping check counts six <carbon> <chemical atoms>, twelve <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>, and eighteen <oxygen> <chemical atoms> on each side: the product side has six <oxygen> <chemical atoms> in the <sugar> and twelve in <molecular oxygen>. The <light> label supplies <energy>, not additional <chemical atoms>.

Crucially, the released <molecular oxygen> comes from <water>, not <carbon dioxide>. Cancelling <water> made and consumed in different steps hides this distinction in the net equation. The <Calvin cycle> supplies a net three-<carbon> <triose phosphate>, such as <glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate>, for subsequent <sugar> synthesis; it does not directly export <glucose>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-2-the-light-dependent-reactions-of-photosynthesis[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.2: light-dependent reactions] and https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: using light energy to make organic molecules].

\Image[photosynthesis-energy-and-matter.png]
{title=<Photosynthesis>: <water> supplies released <molecular oxygen>; <carbon dioxide> supplies <sugar> <carbon>}
{description=Original schematic of <oxygenic photosynthesis>. Solid blue arrows trace selected material transfers; dashed orange arrows show <energy> transfer and the coupling through <ATP> and <NADPH>. These carriers are also <molecules>, not pure <energy>. Arrows omit intermediate <chemical reactions> and do not specify rates or yields.}

The useful achievement is connecting incoming <light> to both usable <energy> and new biological material. <ATP> is an immediate <energy> currency; <NADPH> supplies <electrons> for synthesis; <carbon dioxide> supplies the <carbon> incorporated into <carbohydrates>. These roles are complementary. <Conservation of energy> still holds: <photosynthesis> transforms <energy>, never creates it, and some absorbed <energy> is dissipated as <heat>. A <plant> also carries out <cellular respiration>, including in <biological cells> that perform <photosynthesis>. Gas exchange reflects both processes. <Oxygenic photosynthesis> remains central to interpreting terrestrial <molecular oxygen> as an <atmospheric biosignature gas>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: photosynthesis and respiration].

= Photosynthetic acclimation
{parent=Photosynthesis}

Adjustment of the composition, capacity or organization of photosynthetic machinery to prevailing conditions. It acts alongside faster regulation of existing components.

= Photoinhibition
{parent=Photosynthesis}

A light-associated reduction in photosynthetic capacity, often involving damage and recovery processes. It should be distinguished from rapidly reversible protective energy dissipation.

= Photoprotection
{parent=Photosynthesis}

Processes limiting light-induced damage when absorbed excitation exceeds safe photochemical use. Energy dissipation, regulated electron flow and repair have complementary roles.

= Chloroplast relocation
{parent=Photoprotection}

Light-dependent movement of chloroplasts within plant cells. Accumulation can increase interception under weak light, whereas avoidance can reduce exposure under strong light.

= Photosynthetic reaction centre
{parent=Photosynthesis}

The pigment–protein machinery converting excitation into separated redox equivalents. Productive charge separation competes with decay of the excited state.

= Light-harvesting complex
{parent=Photosynthesis}

A pigment–protein assembly collecting light and transferring excitation towards a photosynthetic reaction centre. Excitation transfer between pigments differs from electron transfer after charge separation.

= Stomatal conductance
{parent=Photosynthesis}
{title2=$g_s$}

Conductance for gas exchange through <stomata>, influencing carbon uptake and water loss. Responses to carbon dioxide and atmospheric dryness can affect growth and water-use efficiency.

= Gross primary production
{parent=Photosynthesis}
{title2=$\mathrm{GPP}$}

Total photosynthetic carbon uptake over the stated area and interval, before subtraction of producer respiration.

= Chlorophyll fluorescence
{parent=Photosynthesis}

A measurable signal used to investigate the state of the photosynthetic machinery. Recovery after drying can be informative, but should be complemented by measurements of growth and reproductive success.

= Photosynthetic state transition
{parent=Photosynthesis}

A <photosynthetic state transition> redistributes excitation between photosystems in response to their relative activity. In plants, regulated phosphorylation of a mobile LHCII fraction helps alter its functional association with the two photosystems.

= Photorespiration
{parent=Photosynthesis}

<Photorespiration> is the metabolic route processing products of Rubisco oxygenation and recovering part of their carbon. It consumes energy and releases some carbon dioxide; under particular conditions it also provides a sink for energy and reducing power.

= Cyclic electron flow around photosystem I
{parent=Photosynthesis}

<Cyclic electron flow around photosystem I> returns photosystem-I-derived electrons to the intersystem electron-transport chain. It can support proton-gradient formation without net NADPH production, helping adjust ATP supply and photoprotection.

= Photosynthetic control
{parent=Photosynthesis}

<Photosynthetic control> refers to regulation of electron flow, especially lumen-pH-dependent restriction at the cytochrome b6f complex. Slowing electron delivery can protect downstream acceptors from excessive reduction.

= Xanthophyll cycle
{parent=Photosynthesis}

The <xanthophyll cycle> reversibly changes antenna-associated xanthophyll composition with light conditions. Lumen-acidification-dependent de-epoxidation towards zeaxanthin can support photoprotective quenching in plants.

= Non-photochemical quenching
{parent=Photosynthesis}

<Non-photochemical quenching> describes pathways that reduce chlorophyll fluorescence through routes other than photochemical charge separation. Rapid protective components can dissipate excitation as heat, but a fluorescence decrease alone does not uniquely identify the pathway.

= Oxygenic photosynthesis
{parent=Photosynthesis}

<Oxygenic photosynthesis> uses <water> as an <electron> source and releases <molecular oxygen>. <Plants>, <algae> and <cyanobacteria> provide examples. The defining distinction from <anoxygenic photosynthesis> is <molecular oxygen> release, not merely capture of <light>.

= Anoxygenic photosynthesis
{parent=Photosynthesis}
{wiki}

<Anoxygenic photosynthesis> captures <light> without producing <molecular oxygen>. It occurs in some <bacteria> and uses <electron> sources other than <water>. Whether <carbon fixation> accompanies this <light> capture depends on the <organism> and conditions; <photosynthesis> does not imply one universal <carbon>-assimilation pathway. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26819/[Alberts et al.: chloroplasts and photosynthetic bacteria].

= Light-dependent reactions
{parent=Photosynthesis}
{wiki}

In a <chloroplast>, <light-dependent reactions> take place at <thylakoid> membranes. <Chlorophyll> captures <light>; <electron> transfer helps establish an <electrochemical gradient> across the <thylakoid> membrane, enabling formation of <ATP>. The <oxygenic photosynthesis> pathway also forms <NADPH> and releases <molecular oxygen> from <water>. <ATP> and <NADPH> become available in the <chloroplast stroma> for the <Calvin cycle>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26819/[Alberts et al.: chloroplast light reactions].

\Image[chloroplast-photosynthesis-compartments.png]
{title=<Thylakoid> membranes and <chloroplast stroma> couple two stages of <photosynthesis>}
{description=Original <chloroplast> schematic, not an experimental photograph or a scale drawing. <Light-dependent reactions> supply <ATP> and <NADPH> to the stromal <Calvin cycle>; depleted carriers return for reuse. The <molecular oxygen> arrow leaves the <water>-using stage, while <carbon dioxide> enters <carbon fixation>.}

= Photosystem
{parent=Light-dependent reactions}

An organized light-harvesting and reaction-center assembly supporting photosynthetic energy conversion.

= Photosystem I
{parent=Photosystem}

<Photosystem I> is a light-driven reaction-centre complex in oxygenic photosynthesis. Excitation supports electron transfer to acceptors including ferredoxin; the balance between linear and cyclic routes affects reductant and ATP supply.

= Photosystem II
{parent=Photosystem}

A <photosystem> involved in water oxidation during <oxygenic photosynthesis>. Its reaction-center components include <D1 protein>.

= Oxygen-evolving complex
{parent=Photosystem II}

The manganese–calcium-containing catalytic machinery that extracts electrons from water in oxygenic photosynthesis. Four oxidizing equivalents permit formation of one oxygen molecule from two water molecules.

= LHCII
{c}
{parent=Photosystem II}

<LHCII> is a major light-harvesting antenna complex in plants. A regulated mobile fraction participates in photosynthetic state transitions; antenna redistribution is distinct from simply dissipating excitation.

= PsbS
{c}
{parent=Photosystem II}

<PsbS> participates in rapid proton-dependent regulation of photosynthetic antenna quenching in plants. Loss of PsbS-dependent quenching can be separated experimentally from loss of light-harvesting capacity.

= Photosystem II repair
{parent=Photosystem II}

Processes replacing damaged <photosystem II> components and restoring functional complexes. Regulated <D1 protein> synthesis is one part of this cycle.

= D1 protein
{parent=Photosystem II}
{c}

A reaction-center <protein> of <photosystem II>, encoded by <psbA messenger RNA> in chloroplast expression systems. Damage creates demand for replacement during <photosystem II repair>.

= Chlorophyll
{parent=Light-dependent reactions}
{wiki}

<Chlorophyll> is a family of <molecules> that absorb <light> for <photosynthesis>. In <chloroplasts>, <chlorophyll> works with <proteins> in <thylakoid> membranes; absorbing <light> initiates <energy> capture rather than directly constructing a <sugar>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-2-the-light-dependent-reactions-of-photosynthesis[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.2: photosynthetic pigments].

= Carbon fixation
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Carbon fixation> incorporates <carbon> from <carbon dioxide> into organic <chemical compounds>. It identifies a material transformation, rather than a particular source of <energy>: <carbon fixation> can be supported by <photosynthesis> or by other <metabolism>. The <Calvin cycle> is one pathway, not the definition of all <carbon fixation>.

= Calvin cycle
{c}
{parent=Carbon fixation}
{wiki}

= Calvin-Benson cycle
{c}
{synonym}

The <Calvin cycle> couples <carbon fixation> to use of <ATP> and <NADPH>. In a <chloroplast> it takes place in the <chloroplast stroma>. Its <chemical reactions> incorporate <carbon dioxide>, use <NADPH>-supplied <electrons>, and regenerate the <molecules> that accept further <carbon dioxide>.

In the standard <Calvin cycle> bookkeeping, three <carbon dioxide> <molecules> require nine <ATP> and six <NADPH> for one net three-<carbon> <triose phosphate>. A six-<carbon> <sugar> equivalent therefore corresponds to six <carbon dioxide>, eighteen <ATP> and twelve <NADPH>; subsequent <sugar> synthesis is separate. These are pathway requirements, not a universal measured <photosynthesis> yield. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: Calvin cycle bookkeeping].

Calling the <Calvin cycle> light-independent means its individual <chemical reactions> do not directly capture <light>; it does not mean the <Calvin cycle> runs only at night or independently of the supplies from <light-dependent reactions>.

= RuBisCO
{parent=Calvin cycle}
{c}

Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate carboxylase/oxygenase, the enzyme catalysing carbon-dioxide addition to ribulose bisphosphate in the Calvin cycle. Its competing oxygenase reaction links carbon assimilation to photorespiration.

= Cellular respiration
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Cellular respiration> transfers <energy> from chemical fuels into usable forms including <ATP>. <Oxidation> of the fuel is coupled to <reduction> of an <electron acceptor>; the <energy> transfer can support <ATP> synthesis. <Plants> carry out <cellular respiration> as well as <photosynthesis>. Their <biological cells> need usable <energy> in darkness too, and a <seed> can draw on stored fuel during <germination>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.1, Electrons and Energy and ATP in Living Systems: redox and energy carriers]{ref}<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §8.3, The Energy Flow, Figures 8.21–8.22: plant respiration and energy transfer]{ref}

Follow three different accounts: <carbon> moving from fuel to products, <electrons> moving to an <electron acceptor>, and <energy> becoming available for cellular work. They are connected, but an <oxygen> uptake measurement alone does not identify the fuel or count the <ATP> made. The <pea seed respirometry> experiment makes this distinction visible with simple apparatus.

= Heterotrophic respiration
{parent=Cellular respiration}
{title2=$R_h$}

Respiratory carbon loss by organisms using organic matter produced by others. It includes important decomposer fluxes and must be distinguished from <autotrophic respiration>.

= Autotrophic respiration
{parent=Cellular respiration}
{title2=$R_a$}

Respiratory carbon loss by primary producers. Subtracting it from <gross primary production> gives <net primary production>.

= Aerobic respiration
{parent=Cellular respiration}

<Aerobic respiration> uses <molecular oxygen> as the final <electron acceptor>. For complete <oxidation> of <glucose>, the net material balance is
$$
\mathrm{C}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}\mathrm{O}_6+6\mathrm{O}_2\longrightarrow6\mathrm{CO}_2+6\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}.
$$
This records the overall conversion of <glucose>, <molecular oxygen>, <carbon dioxide> and <water>; it is not one <enzyme>-catalyzed step, and it omits the separate <ATP> accounting. For an original <stoichiometry> check, each side contains six <carbon>, twelve <hydrogen> and eighteen <oxygen> <chemical atoms>. If exactly $1\,\mathrm{mmol}$ of <glucose> undergoes this complete conversion, the equation requires $6\,\mathrm{mmol}$ of <molecular oxygen> and yields $6\,\mathrm{mmol}$ each of <carbon dioxide> and net <water>. This is an equation-based calculation, not an observed seed measurement.

The <molecular oxygen> is reduced to <water>, rather than being the source of the <carbon dioxide>'s <carbon>. A formal <redox electron balance>, using acidic half-reaction notation, makes the roles explicit:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{C}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}\mathrm{O}_6+6\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}&\longrightarrow6\mathrm{CO}_2+24\mathrm{H}^{+}+24e^{-},\\
6\mathrm{O}_2+24\mathrm{H}^{+}+24e^{-}&\longrightarrow12\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Adding and cancelling gives the net equation above. Both half-reactions conserve <chemical atoms> and <electric charge>. The $24$ <electrons> balance a net transfer; they are carried through cellular intermediates, rather than released as a pool of free <electrons>. The final respiratory <reduction> can be written $\mathrm{O}_2+4\mathrm{H}^{+}+4e^{-}\longrightarrow2\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}$. These equations are bookkeeping, not a tracing experiment that assigns every product <oxygen> <chemical atom> to its original <molecule>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.4, Electron Transport Chain, Figure 7.12: oxygen reduction to water]{ref}

= Respiration and ATP coupling
{parent=Aerobic respiration}
{title2=$\mathrm{ADP}+P_i\longrightarrow\mathrm{ATP}$}

<Glycolysis> converts <glucose> into two <pyruvate> <molecules>, with a net two <ATP> and reduced <NADH>; it does not directly consume <molecular oxygen>. <Pyruvate> processing and the <citric acid cycle>, also called the <TCA cycle>, release <carbon dioxide> and supply reduced carriers. Thus releasing <carbon dioxide> and consuming <molecular oxygen> occur at different stages.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.2, Outcomes of Glycolysis; §7.3, Breakdown of Pyruvate and Citric Acid Cycle]{ref}

\Image[cellular-respiration-carbon-redox-energy-flow.png]
{title=<Cellular respiration>: separate <carbon>, <electron> and <energy> accounts}
{description=Original pathway schematic. Blue arrows trace the fuel pathway and <carbon dioxide> release; purple arrows indicate reduced-carrier delivery and terminal <molecular oxygen> <reduction>; orange dashed arrows denote <energy coupling>. Carrier chemistry and intermediate steps are abbreviated. Arrow widths encode no rates or yields.}

An <electron transport chain> reoxidizes carriers such as <NADH>. Coupling some of its <redox> steps to <proton> transport establishes an <electrochemical gradient>; <ATP synthase> can use that gradient to form <ATP> from <adenosine diphosphate> and <inorganic phosphate>. This is <oxidative phosphorylation>. A separate route, <substrate-level phosphorylation>, transfers a <phosphate> group from a reacting <substrate> to <adenosine diphosphate>. In typical <eukaryotic> <biological cells>, the respiratory chain occupies the inner membrane of a <mitochondrion>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.1, Substrate Phosphorylation; §7.4, Electron Transport Chain and Chemiosmosis, Figures 7.12–7.14]{ref}

The useful achievement is controlled transfer: fuel <oxidation> can replenish an <ATP> supply that supports <active transport> and synthesis. <ATP> is a reusable chemical carrier, not newly created <energy>; <ATP> <hydrolysis> and the coupled cellular process have their own net <free energy> balance. Some fuel <energy> is dissipated as <heat>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.1, ATP in Living Systems and Energy from ATP; §7.4, ATP Yield]{ref}

There is no universal yield of $36$ or $38$ <ATP> per <glucose>. Actual <ATP> recovery depends on carrier transfer routes, coupling and diversion of intermediates to other <metabolism>. In particular, an <oxygen> uptake rate cannot be converted into an <ATP> production rate by assuming one fixed multiplier.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.4, ATP Yield: shuttle differences and use of intermediates]{ref}

= Anaerobic respiration
{parent=Cellular respiration}
{wiki}

<Anaerobic respiration> uses a respiratory <electron transport chain> with a final <electron acceptor> other than <molecular oxygen>. External acceptors can include <sulfate>, depending on the <organism>. “External” distinguishes a supplied terminal acceptor from the fuel-derived organic intermediates used in <fermentation>; it does not mean that the <reduction> must take place outside the <biological cell>. In <fermentation>, regeneration of carriers such as <NADH> to their oxidized form permits <glycolysis> to continue without a respiratory <electron transport chain>. Both routes can operate without <molecular oxygen>, but they are different mechanisms.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §7.5, opening distinction, Anaerobic Cellular Respiration and Figure 7.16]{ref}

= Respirometer
{parent=Cellular respiration}
{wiki}

A <respirometer> measures gas changes associated with <cellular respiration>. In the absorber-based <pea seed respirometry> apparatus, the changing gas inventory is inferred from a liquid boundary's displacement. A <respirometer> is therefore a physical measurement coupled to a biological process, not a direct <ATP> counter.

= Respirometry
{parent=Respirometer}
{wiki}

= Pea seed respirometry
{parent=Respirometer}

The University of Oklahoma K20 Center's handout describes three sealed vial-and-pipette <respirometers> in each <temperature> bath: soaked, germinating <peas>; nongerminating <peas> plus inert plastic beads; and beads alone. Displaced <water> establishes equal occupied <volumes>. Absorbent cotton holds <potassium hydroxide>, which removes <carbon dioxide>; nonabsorbent cotton separates it from the <peas>. The source equilibrates the vials before submersion and again before the initial reading. Colored <water> makes the boundary visible in each calibrated pipette. Readers then record positions every five minutes for twenty minutes.<Cell Respiration by the K20 Center>[Cell Respiration, K20 Center handout adapted from AP Biology Lab Manual, pp. 3–4, procedure 1–16: preparation, equilibration and readings]{ref}

\Image[pea-respirometer-absorber-and-controls.png]
{title=<Pea seed respirometry>: absorber, gas readout and matched controls}
{description=Original schematic of the university handout's vial-and-pipette apparatus. <Carbon dioxide> is removed from the gas; <molecular oxygen> consumption draws the <water> boundary toward the vial. The three sample groups share a bath and matched occupied <volumes>. Geometry, particle counts and boundary position are illustrative; no measured readings are plotted.}

Bath <temperature>, vial position and seal integrity matter. The beads-only <respirometer> detects common <temperature> or atmospheric <pressure> drift; its change is subtracted from each pea reading. Nongerminating <peas> provide a biological comparison, not an assumption of zero <cellular respiration>. A larger corrected uptake slope for germinating <peas> would support greater <molecular oxygen> consumption by that sample under the tested conditions.<Cell Respiration by the K20 Center>[Cell Respiration, pp. 1, 3–4 and 6: living dormant seeds, position/leaks, corrected differences and slope]{ref}

Equal displaced <volumes> help match the gas spaces; they do not establish equal living <mass> or equal developmental state. Comparing uptake per <seed> and uptake per dry <mass> asks different questions. Leaks, incomplete <carbon dioxide> absorption, gas dissolution and changing water-vapour contributions can distort the <respirometer> inference. A blank correction cannot repair a sample-specific leak. These are consequences of the <respirometer gas balance>, rather than measurements reported in the handout.

= Respirometer gas balance
{parent=Respirometer}

For an original idealized <mathematical model>, let $n_{\mathrm O_2}(t)$ be the <amount of substance> of <molecular oxygen> consumed since the initial reading and $n_{\mathrm{CO}_2}(t)$ the <carbon dioxide> produced. Suppose a fraction $f$ of that <carbon dioxide> is removed from the gas, all other gas inventories remain fixed, and <temperature> $T$ and gas <pressure> $P$ stay constant. The <ideal gas law> gives the positive gas contraction
$$
D(t)=V(0)-V(t)=\frac{RT}{P}\left[n_{\mathrm O_2}(t)-(1-f)n_{\mathrm{CO}_2}(t)\right],
$$
where $R$ is the <molar gas constant> and $V$ the gas <volume>. With complete absorption, $f=1$, the contraction reflects <molecular oxygen> uptake regardless of the amount of <carbon dioxide> made. For a uniform pipette bore of <area> $A$, an inward boundary displacement $x$ gives $D=Ax$. A graduated pipette can instead supply the displaced <volume> directly.

Let $D_s$ denote sample contraction and $D_b$ the matched blank contraction. Define $D_{\mathrm{corr}}=D_s-D_b$ with both taken relative to their own initial readings. Under the preceding assumptions,
$$
n_{\mathrm O_2}(t)\simeq\frac{P D_{\mathrm{corr}}(t)}{RT},\qquad
\overline{r}_{\mathrm O_2}=\frac{P}{RT}\frac{D_{\mathrm{corr}}(t_2)-D_{\mathrm{corr}}(t_1)}{t_2-t_1}.
$$
The <slope> of corrected <volume> against <time> estimates uptake over the chosen interval. The approximate sign acknowledges that a liquid boundary also responds to hydrostatic and capillary <pressure>, while the gas can exchange <water> vapour. A fixed-<volume> apparatus would instead show a <pressure> decrease; its ideal conversion is $n_{\mathrm O_2}\simeq V\bigl(P(0)-P(t)\bigr)/(RT)$ after complete absorption and appropriate blank correction.

The <ideal gas law> also exposes drift without any biology: for fixed gas inventory, $V\propto T/P$. A one-percent rise in absolute <temperature> at fixed <pressure> produces a one-percent expansion. Matching the initial gas spaces makes such common changes easier to subtract. Subtracting unmatched blanks can leave a physical signal that looks biological.

= Respiratory quotient
{parent=Respirometer gas balance}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{RQ}=n_{\mathrm{CO}_2}/n_{\mathrm O_2}$}

The <respiratory quotient> compares <carbon dioxide> production with <molecular oxygen> consumption over the same interval. Complete <glucose> <oxidation> in the net equation above gives $\mathrm{RQ}=6/6=1$. In the ideal <respirometer gas balance> with no absorber, $f=0$, that conversion gives $D=0$: substantial <cellular respiration> can occur without any net gas contraction. A zero contraction therefore does not by itself demonstrate zero <metabolism>.

Other fuels, incomplete conversion and concurrent synthesis can alter the ratio. Absorber-based <respirometry> isolates <molecular oxygen> uptake by deliberately removing the other gas signal; it cannot independently determine the <respiratory quotient> or identify <glucose> as the consumed fuel. Separate <carbon dioxide> measurement would supply the missing account.

= Secondary metabolic byproduct
{parent=Metabolism}

A compound released through specialized functions such as signaling, defense or stress adaptation rather than the core reactions needed for energy and biomass. <Dimethyl sulfide> and <chloromethane> are examples discussed as atmospheric biosignatures. Their specialized origins may improve specificity while lowering the global emission flux.

= Primary metabolic byproduct
{parent=Metabolism}

A product released by reactions needed for energy acquisition, growth or biomass construction. <Methane> from energy-yielding microbial reactions and <molecular oxygen> from oxygenic <photosynthesis> are examples. Abiotic chemistry can make some of the same molecules, so production mechanism and environmental abundance matter.

= Evolution
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Evolution> is descent with inherited modification. Within a population, changes in <allele frequencies> provide a precise genetic description; over longer histories, branching descent connects populations through <common descent>. An individual growing larger is <biological development>, whereas an inherited variant becoming more frequent across generations is <evolution>. See https://evolution.berkeley.edu/evolution-101/an-introduction-to-evolution/[UC Museum of Paleontology: the definition of evolution].

<Mutation> supplies new <alleles>, <genetic recombination> reshuffles inherited material, <natural selection> favors variants with greater reproductive contribution in a specified environment, and <genetic drift> changes frequencies through chance. <Gene flow> moves variants between populations. Consequently, an increasing <allele frequency> alone does not identify <natural selection>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/19-2-population-genetics[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: population genetics].

Evidence includes ordered <fossils>, shared inherited structures and similarities in <DNA>; <phylogenetics> uses such observations to infer relationships. A <phylogenetic tree> is an inferred historical account, with uncertainty about particular branches. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/18-1-understanding-evolution[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: evidence of evolution]. Evidence also comes from experiments: Blount, Borland and Lenski reported an <Escherichia coli> lineage able to use <citrate> with <oxygen> present by 31,500 generations, and tested historical dependence by replaying <evolution> from stored ancestors. See https://www.blount-lab.org/uploads/2/3/0/3/23037222/2008_pnas_blount_et_al.pdf[Blount, Borland and Lenski, 2008: original experimental report]. Such observations constrain explanations; no single frequency equation captures every evolutionary process.

= Mosaic evolution
{parent=Evolution}

<evolution> in which different anatomical or developmental components change on different schedules. A <fossil> can combine derived states in one system with retained ancestral states in another.

= Evolutionary reversal
{parent=Evolution}

A change returning a character to a state resembling an earlier ancestral state. Similar terminal appearances do not by themselves identify the developmental route of the change.

= Character loss
{parent=Evolution}

Loss of an inherited anatomical or molecular feature. Its inference requires distinguishing genuine absence from incomplete observation and reconstructing the ancestral state.

= Red Queen hypothesis
{parent=Evolution}
{c}

A family of hypotheses concerning continuing adaptation in response to other evolving organisms. In explanations of sex, changing host-parasite associations can favour production of less familiar host genotypes; the advantage is conditional.

= Clonal selection
{parent=Evolution}

Differential expansion or persistence of lineages under a specified environment. Increased survival can confer an advantage even without a higher instantaneous division rate.

= Major evolutionary transition
{parent=Evolution}

A transition in which previously reproducing entities become integrated into a new higher-level reproductive unit. <cooperation> and control of lower-level conflict are complementary issues.

= Somatic evolution
{parent=Evolution}

Change among cell lineages within an organism through <mutation>, drift and selection. Success of a cell lineage can conflict with the <reproductive success> of the organism.

= Gene loss
{parent=Evolution}

= Biological fitness
{parent=Evolution}

Reproductive contribution measured relative to a specified population, environment and timescale. Survival or task performance is a component or proxy, not automatically total <reproductive success>.

= Mimicry
{parent=Evolution}

Resemblance between organisms that influences a receiver’s <animal behaviour> and thereby changes fitness. Similarity alone does not establish the receiver-mediated evolutionary process.

= Mimicry ring
{parent=Mimicry}

A set of co-occurring organisms sharing a mimetic signal. Membership should be tested against the relevant receiver’s <perception> and behaviour rather than human resemblance alone.

= Batesian mimicry
{parent=Mimicry}
{c}

Resemblance of a relatively undefended organism to a defended model that reduces attack through the receiver’s response to the model. Protection depends on model abundance and <predator> experience.

= Müllerian mimicry
{parent=Mimicry}
{c}

Shared warning signals among defended organisms, potentially reducing each participant’s cost of educating <predators> through <predator learning>. Benefits depend on encounter rates, defence and generalization.

= Evolutionary rescue
{parent=Evolution}

Recovery of a declining <biological population> through inherited adaptive change. Immediate survival enabled by <phenotypic plasticity> can provide time for rescue, but is not itself evidence that inherited rescue occurred.

= Baldwin effect
{parent=Evolution}
{c}

An evolutionary role for acquired adjustments, including <learning>, in allowing persistence and changing the <natural selection> experienced by inherited variants. It does not require direct inheritance of learned contents.

= Fitness landscape
{parent=Evolution}

Assignment of a fitness or experimental performance measure to genotypes, together with a specified relation between neighbouring genotypes. Context and <epistasis> affect accessible improvement paths.

= Gene–culture coevolution
{parent=Evolution}

Evolutionary feedback between inherited genetic differences and culturally transmitted practices. Dairying and <lactase persistence> illustrate why the timing of the practice and allele rise should be tested separately.

= Macroevolution
{parent=Evolution}

Patterns and processes at and above the species level, including lineage origination, diversification and extinction. Its timescale does not make it independent of population processes.

= Diversification rate
{parent=Macroevolution}

A rate describing lineage accumulation, often expressed as speciation rate minus extinction rate under a branching model.

= Adaptive radiation
{parent=Macroevolution}

Diversification of a lineage into ecologically differentiated descendants. Colonization, ecological opportunity and reproductive barriers are distinct contributing processes.

= Microevolution
{parent=Evolution}

Evolutionary change within populations, including changes in allele frequencies and inherited quantitative traits.

= Coevolution
{parent=Evolution}

Reciprocal heritable evolutionary responses in interacting lineages, each altering selection on the other. Functional matching, co-occurrence and immediate plastic responses are insufficient by themselves to demonstrate coevolution.

= Antagonistic coevolution
{parent=Coevolution}

Reciprocal evolutionary change driven by opposed fitness effects, including some host–<parasite> and sexual interactions. Correlated traits or temporal change alone do not establish reciprocal selection.

= Evolutionary biologist
{parent=Evolution}

= Nicholas G. Crawford
{parent=List of evolutionary biologists}

= Work by Nicholas G. Crawford
{parent=Nicholas G. Crawford}
{c}

= Frédéric Delsuc
{parent=List of evolutionary biologists}
{c}

An author of https://www.nature.com/articles/nature04336[the primary study linked here].

= Work by Frédéric Delsuc
{parent=Frédéric Delsuc}
{c}

= Domestication
{parent=Evolution}

A historical process involving sustained relationships with humans and inherited population change. Individual tameness, management and species transport alone are not sufficient definitions.

= Biogeography
{parent=Evolution}

Study of the distribution of organisms through space and time and the historical processes producing it. Present distribution alone does not identify an evolutionary birthplace.

= Sympatry
{parent=Biogeography}

Geographic coexistence at a stated scale. <fossils> from one deposit may still represent different times or habitats.

= Species translocation
{parent=Biogeography}

Human-mediated movement of organisms between places. It includes movements within a historical native range as well as introductions beyond it.

= Phylogeography
{parent=Biogeography}

Study of the geographic distribution of genealogical lineages. Modern distributions can reflect old structure, recent introductions and lineage loss.

= Vicariance
{parent=Biogeography}

Division of an ancestral distribution by a newly formed barrier. It must be distinguished from dispersal across an existing barrier using chronological as well as geographic evidence.

= Biological homology
{parent=Evolution}

Correspondence of structures or other characters through shared evolutionary ancestry. Shared function alone does not establish it; comparative position, <biological development> and <phylogenetics> help distinguish it from <convergent evolution>.

= Genetic assimilation
{parent=Evolution}

Evolution of expression of a formerly induced <phenotype> in the absence of the original inducing condition. It is a particular outcome of <genetic accommodation>, not direct copying of acquired bodily modifications into <DNA>.

= Genetic accommodation
{parent=Evolution}

Evolutionary modification of an environmentally responsive <phenotypic trait> or its regulation. It can change a response threshold, magnitude or specificity and need not reduce <phenotypic plasticity>.

= Plasticity-led evolution
{parent=Genetic accommodation}

A sequence in which an environmental change induces a <phenotype> through <phenotypic plasticity>, followed by inherited refinement through <natural selection>. Evidence requires separating initial induction from subsequent inherited change.

= Convergent evolution
{parent=Evolution}

Independent evolution of similar outcomes in different lineages. Similar <phenotypes> can arise through the same <gene>, different <genes> in a shared pathway, or distinct mechanisms; independence of the evolutionary events must first be established.

= Experimental evolution
{parent=Evolution}

Observation of evolutionary change in controlled, replicated <biological populations>. Known ancestors, archived samples, <DNA sequencing> and reconstruction of candidate <mutations> help connect genetic changes to measured <genotype fitness>.

= Exaptation
{parent=Evolution}

Recruitment of an existing character for a function different from its previous role. It is a claim about functional history; <gene co-option> can supply a developmental mechanism for such recruitment.

= Adaptation
{parent=Evolution}

An inherited feature shaped by <natural selection> for its contribution to <genotype fitness>, or the evolutionary process producing such improvement. An immediately helpful response within a lifetime need not itself constitute evolutionary change.

= Speciation
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

<Speciation> is the formation of distinct <species> through <evolution>. Detecting a difference between <biological populations> is a starting observation; its interpretation depends on the stated <species concept> and the evidence for lasting separation. <Reproductive isolation> is one important route to limiting <gene flow> in <sexual reproduction>.

= Hybrid speciation
{parent=Speciation}

Origin of a reproductively distinct lineage through hybridization. A surviving hybrid individual alone is not a new species.

= Reinforcement
{parent=Speciation}

Evolution of increased premating isolation because interpopulation mating has fitness costs. Ecological divergence without such selection is not sufficient evidence.

= Secondary contact
{parent=Speciation}

Renewed contact between populations following a period of separation.

= Sympatric speciation
{parent=Speciation}

Origin of distinct species without initial geographic separation at the relevant scale. Current sympatry alone does not establish this historical route.

= Allopatric speciation
{parent=Speciation}

Evolution of distinct species during geographic separation. Persistence of isolation after renewed contact is a further test.

= Ecological speciation
{parent=Speciation}

Evolution of <reproductive isolation> as a consequence of divergent ecological <natural selection>. Ecological differences or distinct <reaction norms> alone are insufficient unless they reduce <gene flow>.

= Sensory drive
{parent=Ecological speciation}

Divergence of sensory systems and signals associated with the physical or ecological environment, potentially generating mating barriers.

= Magic trait
{parent=Ecological speciation}

A trait affected by divergent ecological selection that also contributes to assortative mating. Distinguish one pleiotropic trait from tightly linked separate loci.

= Reproductive isolation
{parent=Speciation}
{wiki}

<Reproductive isolation> consists of barriers that reduce <gene flow> through <sexual reproduction>. Distinguish <premating isolation> from barriers after mating. Barriers before <fertilization> also include incompatible <gametes>; barriers after <fertilization> can reduce offspring survival or fertility. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[§18.2, “Reproductive Isolation”, opening paragraphs, gametic barriers and hybrid inviability/sterility]{ref}

An assay should state which step it measures. Observing fewer cross-group matings does not measure offspring fertility; observing <fertilization> does not demonstrate that the offspring will reproduce. These distinctions make a partial barrier informative without declaring completed <speciation>.

= Cytoplasmic incompatibility
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

Reproductive incompatibility associated with an inherited cytoplasmic factor, including some bacterial symbionts. Reciprocal crosses, microbial manipulations and host-background controls identify its cause.

= Reproductive character displacement
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

Greater divergence of mating signals or preferences in <sympatry> than outside contact. The pattern can follow <reinforcement>, but also ecological differences, sorting or interference without reduced hybrid fitness.

= Haldane's rule
{parent=Reproductive isolation}
{c}

When one sex of first-generation <hybrids> is absent, rare or sterile, it tends to be the <heterogametic sex>. This predicts a sex bias within crosses, rather than a universal difference in isolation between birds and mammals.<Sex ratio and unisexual sterility in hybrid animals by J. B. S. Haldane>[Journal of Genetics 12, pp. 101–109]{ref}

= Dobzhansky-Muller incompatibility
{parent=Reproductive isolation}
{c}

A harmful interaction between <alleles> that evolved in separate lineages and are compatible with their own <genetic backgrounds>. For example, substitutions yielding $Ab$ and $aB$ can each be tolerated, while the combination $AB$ is harmful in a <hybrid>. This is a form of <epistasis>.

= Premating isolation
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

<Premating isolation> reduces mating between <biological populations>. The <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> assay measures this stage using observed pairings.

= Postmating reproductive isolation
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

<Postmating reproductive isolation> acts after mating. Specify whether the test concerns <fertilization>, offspring survival, or the offspring's own <reproduction>; those outcomes are not interchangeable.

= Hybrid breakdown
{parent=Postmating reproductive isolation}

Reduced survival or fertility appearing in later-generation or backcross descendants of <hybrids>, even when first-generation offspring are fertile. <genetic recombination> can expose harmful combinations previously masked in the first generation.

= Postzygotic isolation
{parent=Postmating reproductive isolation}

A barrier to <gene flow> acting after <fertilization>, through reduced survival or fertility of <hybrids>. Test both sexes and later generations: survival of first-generation offspring does not establish their fertility.

= Hybrid sterility
{parent=Postzygotic isolation}

Reduced or absent fertility of hybrid offspring. It must be distinguished from failure to mate or reduced viability before reproductive maturity.

= Dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

The 1989 experiment used eight <Drosophila pseudoobscura> <biological populations> from one Bryce Canyon population: four maintained on starch medium, four on maltose, in cages at $25\,{}^\circ\mathrm{C}$. Testing began about a year after establishment. <Reproductive Isolation as a Consequence of Adaptive Divergence in Drosophila pseudoobscura by Diane M. B. Dodd>[p. 1308, population origin, media and cage conditions]{ref}

Before testing, flies spent one generation on cornmeal–molasses–agar. Virgins aged 3–6 days entered multiple-choice chambers: twelve females from each population, followed by males from both populations introduced nearly simultaneously without anesthesia. Observers scored matings for 60–90 minutes under bright indirect lighting. One group's right wing tips were clipped for identification, with clipping reversed between replicates. <Reproductive Isolation as a Consequence of Adaptive Divergence in Drosophila pseudoobscura by Diane M. B. Dodd>[p. 1308, mating-preference procedure and wing-marking control]{ref}

All sixteen cross-diet comparisons had positive <mating assortment contrasts>; eleven departed significantly from <random mating>. Same-diet comparisons did not show significant departures. Common-medium rearing addressed immediate diet conditioning; same-diet controls addressed separation and establishment <population bottlenecks>. Mate choice was not the selection target; the <biological populations> were maintained separately. The result supports partial <premating isolation> accompanying dietary divergence, not completed <speciation>. <Reproductive Isolation as a Consequence of Adaptive Divergence in Drosophila pseudoobscura by Diane M. B. Dodd>[pp. 1309–1310, Tables 1–2 and discussion of conditioning and physical isolation]{ref}

\Image[dietary-divergence-mating-assay.png]
{title=Design of the <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> assay}
{description=Original design schematic. Arrows indicate procedure, not a measured <reproductive isolation> rate. The four mating categories are possible outcomes; no invented counts are shown.}

The <causal inference> remains limited. A negative same-diet <hypothesis test> does not prove zero <reproductive isolation>. One generation under common conditions does not exclude every persistent environmental influence. The chamber readout alone cannot identify a particular <gene>, establish offspring fertility, or predict <gene flow> after sustained contact in nature. Further experiments would need to target those questions separately.

= Mating assortment contrast
{parent=Reproductive isolation}
{title2=$I=(N_{\mathrm{same}}-N_{\mathrm{cross}})/N$}

For observed pairings between two labels $A,B$, let $N_{\mathrm{same}}=N_{AA}+N_{BB}$ and $N_{\mathrm{cross}}=N_{AB}+N_{BA}$, where the first label denotes the female's group and the second the male's. A <mating assortment contrast> is
$$
I=\frac{N_{\mathrm{same}}-N_{\mathrm{cross}}}{N},\qquad N=N_{\mathrm{same}}+N_{\mathrm{cross}}>0.
$$
It lies between $-1$ and $1$. Positive $I$ describes an excess of same-label pairings over cross-label pairings; whether that is evidence of <assortative mating> depends on the null <mathematical model> and the assay. The four counts form a <contingency table>.

Here is an original scope check. Suppose independently paired females and males have <probabilities> $p_f,p_m$ of carrying label $A$. Then
$$
q_{\mathrm{same}}=p_fp_m+(1-p_f)(1-p_m),\qquad \mathbb E[I]=2q_{\mathrm{same}}-1.
$$
With $p_f=p_m=1/2$, the <expected value> is zero. With $p_f=p_m=4/5$, it is $9/25=0.36$, despite pairing being independent of label. This counterexample concerns unequal pool composition; it does not replace the group introductions and <contingency table> tests in the <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> experiment. Even with balanced pools, finite observed samples fluctuate: a positive $I$ alone is not a <hypothesis test>, much less a universal <species> boundary.

= Common descent
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

= Horizontal gene transfer
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

<Horizontal gene transfer> moves genetic material between lineages through routes other than ordinary inheritance from parent to offspring. <Endosymbiotic gene transfer> is a specialized case involving an internal partner or its descendant <organelle>.

= Gene flow
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

= Introgression
{parent=Gene flow}

Transfer of inherited material between diverged lineages through <hybrid> reproduction and subsequent crosses. Shared adaptive <alleles> may therefore reflect transfer rather than repeated independent <mutations>.

= Adaptive introgression
{parent=Introgression}

Transfer of inherited variation between lineages through <hybridization> and subsequent backcrossing, followed by a selective benefit in the recipient. Shared ancestry at a region and evidence of its benefit are separate requirements.

= Natural selection
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

<Natural selection> involves systematic differences in expected <reproductive success> associated with variation in <phenotype>. For a continuing evolutionary response, the relevant variation must be inherited: offspring must tend to preserve the differences being favored. Actual offspring counts also fluctuate by chance, producing <genetic drift>. Survival matters insofar as it affects contribution to future generations. <Genotype fitness> is therefore contextual, depending on environment and the life cycle being counted, rather than simply measuring strength or longevity. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/19-1-population-evolution[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: population evolution].

In chapter IV of his 1859 book, Darwin explained <natural selection> through preservation of favorable variations and greater chances of survival and reproduction. See https://darwin-online.org.uk/converted/published/1859_Origin_F373/1859_Origin_F373.html[Darwin, On the Origin of Species, first edition, chapter IV]. The beautiful consequence is cumulative change without foresight: inherited differences are filtered repeatedly by present conditions. <Natural selection> neither generates a needed <mutation> on demand nor guarantees progress toward a predetermined goal.

= Artificial selection
{parent=Natural selection}

Selection imposed through human choices about which organisms contribute to later generations. Strong consistent criteria and a controlled environment can produce rapid short-term response while exposing correlated costs.

= Balancing selection
{parent=Natural selection}

Selection maintaining more than one inherited variant. Heterozygote advantage and appropriate negative frequency dependence are different mechanisms that can produce this outcome.

= Viability selection
{parent=Natural selection}

= Multilevel selection
{parent=Natural selection}

Analysis separating selection within groups from selection associated with differences among groups. Group benefit alone does not show that between-group selection overcomes within-group advantage.

= Frequency-dependent selection
{parent=Natural selection}

Dependence of relative fitness on the frequencies of variants in the population. Its sign and strength must be specified for the ecological context.

= Negative frequency-dependent selection
{parent=Frequency-dependent selection}

Selection in which relative advantage decreases as a variant becomes common, potentially maintaining variation under the stated ecological assumptions.

= Positive frequency-dependent selection
{parent=Frequency-dependent selection}

<natural selection> in which a variant’s relative fitness increases with its frequency under a specified ecological setting. Shared warning patterns can benefit from prior <predator learning>.

= Inclusive fitness
{parent=Natural selection}

A framework combining an actor’s effects on its own reproduction with effects on others, weighted by appropriate <genetic relatedness> and reproductive values. Coefficients and marginal effects depend on the model and population structure.

= Kin selection
{parent=Inclusive fitness}

Selection associated with the indirect reproductive consequences of actions affecting genetically associated recipients. It can favour cooperation without removing all <evolutionary conflicts> among relatives.

= Local competition
{parent=Kin selection}

Competition among a restricted set of neighbours or relatives. Benefits to related recipients can be offset when those recipients compete for the same limited breeding opportunities.

= Evolutionary conflict
{parent=Natural selection}

A divergence between the outcomes favoured by selection on different individuals, replicating lineages or genetic elements. Conflict can exist without overt fighting.

= Intragenomic conflict
{parent=Evolutionary conflict}

Conflict among genetic elements whose transmission or expression favours different outcomes despite residence in one organism. Organismal performance alone is not the relevant fitness currency for every element.

= Meiotic drive
{parent=Intragenomic conflict}

Transmission distortion favouring an element over the Mendelian expectation. In a strict usage this acts during <meiosis>; broader usage also includes biased survival of <gametes>.

= Centromere drive
{parent=Meiotic drive}

Biased transmission associated with centromere differences during asymmetric <meiosis>, where only some meiotic products contribute to the next generation.

= Drive suppressor
{parent=Meiotic drive}

A modifier reducing a driver’s transmission advantage or harmful consequences. Selection depends on linkage, background and the costs of suppression.

= Segregation distortion
{parent=Meiotic drive}

Deviation in inherited <allele> proportions from the Mendelian expectation. Embryonic viability selection can also distort offspring counts and must be separated from gametic transmission bias.

= Selfish genetic element
{parent=Intragenomic conflict}

A genetic element that can spread through a transmission or replication advantage even when it imposes costs on the organism carrying it.

= Sexual conflict
{parent=Evolutionary conflict}

Differences in the outcomes favoured by selection on males and females, for example over mating, fertilization or <parental care>. Shared benefits from reproduction do not imply identical optima.

= Sex peptide
{parent=Sexual conflict}

A seminal protein studied in Drosophila melanogaster whose transfer affects female physiology and behaviour. Its evolutionary costs and benefits depend on mating regime and measured time horizon.

= Interlocus sexual conflict
{parent=Sexual conflict}

Conflict mediated by interacting traits encoded at different loci in the sexes, including manipulative traits and resistance.

= Intralocus sexual conflict
{parent=Sexual conflict}

Different sex-specific optima for a trait influenced by shared <genetic variation>. Sex-limited expression can reduce the conflict without requiring all genetic effects to become independent.

= Sexual antagonistic coevolution
{parent=Sexual conflict}

Reciprocal evolutionary change in traits of the sexes arising through opposed reproductive effects. Correlated morphologies alone need not prove historical reciprocal selection.

= Sibling rivalry
{parent=Evolutionary conflict}

Competition among offspring over resources, care or other opportunities. It can interact with <parent-offspring conflict> by redistributing a fixed supply and altering total parental provisioning.

= Brood reduction
{parent=Sibling rivalry}

A decline in the number of surviving offspring within a brood, often associated with limited provisioning. The occurrence of brood reduction alone does not establish an adaptive parental or offspring strategy.

= Siblicide
{parent=Sibling rivalry}

Killing of a sibling, whose ecological and evolutionary explanations depend on resource limitation, asymmetry and the consequences for survivors and parents.

= Sibling negotiation
{parent=Sibling rivalry}

Contingent communication among siblings about their willingness to contest a resource, potentially reducing unnecessary competitive costs.

= Parent-offspring conflict
{parent=Evolutionary conflict}

Differences between parental and offspring strategies favoured by selection, including allocation and duration of care. Costs imposed on other offspring have different weights in the parties’ fitness accounts.

= Evolutionary policing
{parent=Evolutionary conflict}

Behaviour or control that reduces the success of selfish actions by other group members. Selection on policing depends on its costs, effects on productivity and genetic or direct benefits to the policing party.

= Worker policing
