{parent=Evolutionary policing}

Suppression of worker reproduction by other workers, including discriminatory removal of worker-laid eggs. Genetic incentives and effects on colony productivity can both matter.

= Partner sanctions
{parent=Evolutionary policing}

Conditional reduction of benefits to a partner that supplies a poor service, potentially changing the returns to exploitation. An immediate physiological response and its evolutionary explanation should be distinguished.

= Positive selection
{parent=Natural selection}

A process by which a heritable change is favoured through an increase in lineage reproductive success under defined conditions. In <tumours>, <mutation> opportunity and selection must be analysed separately.

= Purifying selection
{parent=Natural selection}

Removal of harmful inherited variants through their lower <genotype fitness>. Reduced efficacy in small <biological populations> can permit harmful changes to spread more readily by <genetic drift>.

= Background selection
{parent=Purifying selection}

Reduction of linked genetic diversity caused by removal of deleterious alleles and their associated backgrounds. It can mimic some genomic diversity patterns attributed to a recent selective sweep.

= Sexual antagonism
{parent=Natural selection}

Opposing effects of the same inherited variant on reproductive performance in the two sexes. Net evolutionary outcomes depend on inheritance, sex-specific expression and the strength of the opposing effects.

= Sexual selection
{parent=Natural selection}

Differences in reproductive success arising through access to mates or fertilizations. Conspicuous signals can diverge rapidly under <sexual selection> without equivalent divergence in the functions required for <meiosis>.

= Mate choice
{parent=Sexual selection}

Behaviour influencing which potential partner contributes to reproduction. The opportunity to choose and the costs of searching constrain its expression.

= Mate preference
{parent=Sexual selection}

Differential responsiveness to potential mates or mating cues. Preference must be distinguished from the realized mating pattern produced by encounter, competition and acceptance.

= Haploid selection recurrence
{parent=Natural selection}
{title2=$p'=\frac{wp}{wp+(1-p)}$}

Consider an explicitly idealized <haploid> population with two inherited types $A,B$, one <allele> per individual at the tracked <genetic locus>, and discrete, nonoverlapping generations. Assume faithful type transmission, no <mutation> or <gene flow>, a constant environment, and reproductive contributions independent of type frequency. Let the <expected values> of offspring surviving to the next census be $m_A,m_B>0$, with relative <genotype fitness> $w=m_A/m_B$.

For $A$ frequency $p$, expected offspring contributions are proportional to $pm_A$ and $(1-p)m_B$. Normalizing gives the deterministic large-population <mathematical model>
$$
p'=\frac{pm_A}{pm_A+(1-p)m_B}=\frac{wp}{wp+(1-p)}.
$$
The denominator is mean relative <genotype fitness>. For $w=1.2$ and $p_0=0.2$, $p_1=0.24/1.04=3/13\approx0.23077$. Equivalently, 200 $A$ and 800 $B$ parents with $m_A=1.2,m_B=1$ contribute expected counts 240 and 800. Fractional per-parent <expected values> are averages, not literal fractional offspring. The ratio of these expectations is a deterministic approximation, not generally the expectation of a random ratio.

The odds multiply particularly simply:
$$
\frac{p_{t+1}}{1-p_{t+1}}=w\frac{p_t}{1-p_t},\qquad p_t=\frac{w^tp_0}{1-p_0+w^tp_0}.
$$
Thus $p_{10}\approx0.60753$: repeated modest advantage can substantially change composition. For $w>1$ and $0<p<1$, $p'-p=(w-1)p(1-p)/(1+(w-1)p)>0$. The deterministic boundary $p=0$ remains unchanged: <natural selection> needs existing variation.

\Image[natural-selection-frequency.png]
{title=Deterministic selection and stochastic sampling in a finite population}
{description=Both panels start at $p_0=0.2$. The left compares the deterministic <haploid selection recurrence> with eight simulated finite-population paths at $w=1.2$; the right shows eight neutral <genetic drift> paths at $w=1$. Each simulation samples $N=50$ offspring independently per generation. These are illustrative <mathematical models>, not experimental measurements.}

A distinct stochastic extension fixes $N$ offspring and samples their types independently using a <binomial distribution>: $K\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(N,q)$, where $q=wp/(wp+1-p)$, then $p'=K/N$. Its conditional <expected value> is $q$ and conditional <variance> is $q(1-q)/N$. With $w=1$, the conditional <expected value> remains $p$, yet realized frequencies wander through <genetic drift>. With $w>1$, <genetic drift> can still lower $p$ or lose $A$ entirely. Over several generations, the deterministic curve need not equal the mean stochastic trajectory, because the recurrence is nonlinear.

= Reproductive success
{parent=Natural selection}
{wiki}

= Phylogenetics
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

= Phylogenetic character
{parent=Phylogenetics}

A heritable feature represented by distinct states in a comparative analysis. State definitions, homology assumptions, uncertainty and correlations among characters affect the reconstruction.

= Cladogram
{parent=Phylogenetics}

A branching diagram representing a hypothesis of relationships. Branch geometry need not encode elapsed time or amount of anatomical change.

= Outgroup
{parent=Phylogenetics}

A lineage outside the focal group used to inform rooting and <character polarity>. An extant <outgroup> is not an unchanged ancestor.

= Phylogenetic nomenclature
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Naming <clades> by explicit ancestry-based definitions. A definition fixes the rule connecting a name to ancestry; an inferred <phylogenetic tree> determines which organisms satisfy that rule.

= Phylogenetic specifier
{parent=Phylogenetic nomenclature}

A named organism, species or other specified reference used to anchor a <clade> definition. Internal specifiers are included and external specifiers excluded.

= Branch-based clade definition
{parent=Phylogenetic nomenclature}

= Stem-based clade definition
{synonym}

= Maximum-clade definition
{synonym}

A definition selecting the largest <clade> containing specified internal reference taxa but excluding specified external reference taxa. With one of each, it includes organisms more closely related to the internal than to the external specifier.

= Node-based clade definition
{parent=Phylogenetic nomenclature}

= Minimum-clade definition
{synonym}

A definition selecting the smallest <clade> containing specified internal reference taxa: their <last common ancestor> and every descendant. The specifiers need not be living.

= Phylogenomics
{parent=Phylogenetics}

= Total-evidence phylogenetics
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Inference combining evidence types such as morphological characters and molecular sequences, with explicit assumptions about homology, missing data and evolution.

= Molecular phylogeny
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Inference of evolutionary relationships from molecular data. Structural correspondence is most persuasive when evaluated alongside alignments, sequence profiles and taxonomic distributions.

= Phylogenetic nonindependence
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Correlation among species observations caused by shared evolutionary history. Comparative tests must distinguish replicated evolutionary changes from repeated sampling of the same ancestral trait.

= Turtle problem
{parent=Phylogenetics}

The historical difficulty of placing <turtles> among reptiles, including conflict between a roofed temporal <skull> and evidence for <diapsid> ancestry. It exposes the difference between character similarity and inferred descent.

= Polyphyly
{parent=Phylogenetics}

= Polyphyletic group
{synonym}

= Polyphyletic
{synonym}

Assembly of separate lineages without including their relevant connecting ancestry, often using independently acquired similar features. Selected tips alone do not always distinguish this from <paraphyly>; the ancestry included and the interpretation of characters matter.

= Paraphyly
{parent=Phylogenetics}

= Paraphyletic group
{synonym}

= Paraphyletic
{synonym}

Inclusion of an ancestor while excluding some descendants. Traditional reptiles with <birds> excluded provide an example; the status depends on the stated group definition.

= Monophyly
{parent=Phylogenetics}

= Monophyletic group
{synonym}

= Monophyletic
{synonym}

In the cladistic sense, inclusion of an ancestor and all its descendants. Such a group is a <clade>.

= Synapomorphy
{parent=Phylogenetics}

A shared derived character supporting a particular branch of a <phylogenetic tree>. Its level depends on inferred character polarity; a trait shared ancestrally across a wider group need not diagnose a smaller <clade>.

= Homoplasy
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Similarity of character states not explained simply by inheritance of that state from the relevant common ancestor. Independent origins and evolutionary reversals can make a single feature misleading.

= Last common ancestor
{parent=Phylogenetics}

The most recent ancestor shared by the stated descendant lineages. In population history it is important to distinguish a shared ancestral population from the common ancestor of copies at a particular <genetic locus>.

= Stem group
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Extinct lineages more closely related to the specified <crown group> than to its living sister group, but outside that crown. Its membership depends on the crown and sister group specified.

= Crown group
{parent=Phylogenetics}

The <clade> comprising the <last common ancestor> of a stated set of living lineages and all its descendants. An older fossil closely related to that group may instead belong to its <stem group>.

= Maximum parsimony
{parent=Phylogenetics}

An inference criterion selecting histories or trees minimizing the stated cost of character changes. A minimum-change history is not automatically the most probable history under a stochastic evolutionary model.

= Ancestral state reconstruction
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Inference of characters at ancestral nodes from observed relatives and an evolutionary model. Historical hypotheses should carry uncertainty in trees, rates, character coding and root states into subsequent tests.

= Character polarity
{parent=Ancestral state reconstruction}

The inferred ancestral or derived status of a character state relative to a particular comparison. A state can be derived at one node and ancestral at another.

= Stochastic character mapping
{parent=Ancestral state reconstruction}

Sampling evolutionary character histories conditional on observations, a tree and transition assumptions. Histories can include multiple unobserved events along a branch, rather than only changes implied by endpoints.

= Bayesian divergence-time inference
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{c}

Joint inference of evolutionary times, rates and related parameters using <Bayesian inference>. Sequence distances identify products of rates and durations; absolute ages require additional time information and assumptions.

= Bayesian phylogenetic inference
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{c}

Inference of a <posterior distribution> over <phylogenetic trees> and model parameters by combining a sequence <likelihood function> with a <prior distribution>. Integrating nuisance parameters is distinct from maximizing the likelihood.

= Phylogenetic pruning algorithm
{parent=Phylogenetics}

A recursive calculation of the <likelihood function> on a <phylogenetic tree>, summing over unknown ancestral states while reusing partial calculations from descendant subtrees.<Evolutionary trees from DNA sequences: a maximum likelihood approach by Joseph Felsenstein>[Ancestral-state summation and likelihood recursion]{ref}

= Nucleotide substitution model
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{title2=$Q$}

A probabilistic model of sequence changes along a <phylogenetic tree>. A <continuous-time Markov chain> rate matrix gives branch transition probabilities through a <matrix exponential>; assumptions about composition and rate variation affect inference.

= Incomplete lineage sorting
{parent=Phylogenetics}

Persistence of ancestral variation through successive lineage splits, allowing the ancestry of one genomic region to disagree with the population branching history. Such disagreement is not by itself evidence of <introgression>.

= Molecular clock
{parent=Phylogenetics}

A <molecular clock> relates accumulated <nucleotide substitutions> to elapsed evolutionary time through an assumed rate. Two lineages evolving at the same constant rate $r$ for time $T$ have total expected substitution count $2rT$ per site before accounting for repeated changes obscured in endpoint sequences. Rate variation among sites or branches requires a more general clock model.

= Fossil calibration
{parent=Molecular clock}

Use of dated fossil evidence to constrain evolutionary times. Geological age, phylogenetic placement, stem versus crown assignment and uncertainty all affect the constraint.

= Soft calibration bound
{parent=Fossil calibration}

A calibration constraint allowing a small nonzero probability outside its principal range. It represents uncertainty but still needs biologically defensible tail probabilities.

= Hard calibration bound
{parent=Fossil calibration}

A calibration constraint assigning zero probability to ages beyond a specified limit. Sequence data cannot overcome an excluded age under that prior.

= Relaxed molecular clock
{parent=Molecular clock}

A model allowing evolutionary rates to vary among branches. The distribution and dependence of rates affect inferred times and must be distinguished from absolute-time calibration.

= Strict molecular clock
{parent=Molecular clock}

A model assigning the same evolutionary rate to the relevant branches. Expected branch length is rate multiplied by duration; scaling times and inverse-scaling the rate preserves the sequence likelihood.

= Clade
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{wiki}

A <clade> comprises an ancestor and all its descendants. Membership is about <common descent>, not a taxonomic rank or a degree of cellular complexity. In a <two-domain system>, the nuclear lineage of <eukaryotes> nests within <archaea>; a group containing only the <prokaryotic> members excludes descendants and is therefore not that complete <clade>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/20-2-determining-evolutionary-relationships[OpenStax Biology 2e, 20.2: clades and shared ancestry].

= Total group
{parent=Clade}

A specified <crown group> together with its <stem group>. <fossil> occurrences must be assigned at the correct level before they are used for <fossil calibration>.

= Phylogenetic tree
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{wiki}

A <phylogenetic tree> describes ancestral relationships. In a <probabilistic graphical model> on a rooted <tree>, <latent variables> at ancestral vertices are inferred from observations at leaves using <belief propagation>.

= Phylogenetic epoch
{parent=Phylogenetic tree}
{title2=$E_k$}

A <phylogenetic epoch> is an interval between successive branching or merging events during which the number of extant ancestral branches is constant. Its expected duration with $k$ branches is denoted $E_k$.

= Species tree
{parent=Phylogenetic tree}

A representation of the branching history of species lineages. Individual <gene trees> can differ, so a well-supported tree for one gene is not automatically a complete species history.

= Gene tree
{parent=Phylogenetic tree}

A <phylogenetic tree> describing the ancestry of sampled gene copies or a genomic region. It need not match a <species tree>, because lineage sorting, duplication or <introgression> can produce a different history.

= Felsenstein pruning algorithm
{c}
{parent=Phylogenetic tree}

The <Felsenstein pruning algorithm> computes a site <likelihood function> by <sum-product belief propagation> from leaves to root. For a finite-state <Markov kernel> $K$, the subtree <likelihood function> obeys $L_v(a)=\prod_{w\text{ child of }v}\sum_bK(a,b)L_w(b)$, with observed-state indicators at leaves. Summing against the root <probability distribution> gives the site <likelihood function>. An outward pass gives edge <posterior probabilities> and expected transition counts for the <expectation-maximization algorithm>.

= Calcium in biology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Calcium has roles in cell signalling and skeletal structure. Dietary <calcium intake> and <serum calcium> are distinct quantities: physiological regulation means a change in one need not correspond to an equivalent change in the other.

= Serum calcium
{parent=Calcium in biology}

Serum calcium is the concentration of calcium in blood serum. It can be an <exposure> in an <observational study> or <Mendelian randomization>, but genetically changing it need not reproduce the effect of changing dietary <calcium intake>.

= Hypercalcaemia
{parent=Serum calcium}
{wiki}

= Hypercalcemia
{synonym}

Hypercalcaemia is an abnormally elevated <serum calcium> concentration. A diagnosis in medical records depends on testing and diagnostic practice, which can introduce <selection bias> into an <observational study>.

= Calcium intake
{parent=Calcium in biology}

Calcium intake is the amount of calcium consumed, including food and supplements. It is an <exposure> that can be changed by an intervention, and should not be identified automatically with <serum calcium> in a <causal inference> analysis.

= Reproduction
{parent=Biology}

= Oviposition
{parent=Reproduction}

Egg laying, including choice of timing and substrate. Plant choice by an adult can determine the conditions experienced by its offspring.

= Asexual reproduction
{parent=Reproduction}

<Asexual reproduction> produces offspring without fusion of <gametes>. An operational <species concept> for such <organisms> needs evidence other than interbreeding.

= Budding
{parent=Reproduction}
{wiki}

<Budding> produces a new individual from an outgrowth of its parent. In <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>, the daughter <biological cell> grows while attached to the mother and later separates; see the Burgess, Powers and Mell account cited there. <Budding> describes <reproduction>, not <fermentation>.

= Sexual reproduction
{parent=Reproduction}

= Outcrossing
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

Mating between different individuals; its consequences depend on population structure and the compatibility of their alleles.

= Self-fertilization
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

= Selfing
{synonym}

Sexual reproduction in which male and female gametes derive from the same individual.

= Autogamy
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

A sexual nuclear cycle involving fusion of pronuclei derived within one individual. In some Paramecium it renews the nuclear apparatus without outcrossing and produces pronounced homozygosity.

= Reproductive assurance
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

Maintenance of reproductive output when access to compatible partners or pollen is unreliable. Autonomous self-fertilization can provide it in suitable self-compatible plants, but bisexuality alone does not guarantee that outcome.

= Monoecy
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

Occurrence of separate male and female reproductive structures on one individual. It does not mean that each <flower> is bisexual.

= Spore
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

A reproductive or dispersal cell capable of initiating growth without fusion with another cell. In the ordinary land-plant sexual cycle, <haploid> <spores> formed by <meiosis> initiate <gametophytes>; they are distinct from fusing <gametes>.

= Heterospory
{parent=Spore}

= Heterosporous
{synonym}

Production of distinct <microspores> and <megaspores> that give rise to male and female <gametophytes>. Retention of developmental stages within spore walls changes protection and provisioning without itself removing the need for water-dependent sperm movement in heterosporous ferns.

= Megaspore
{parent=Heterospory}

The spore type developing into a female <gametophyte>.

= Microspore
{parent=Heterospory}

The spore type developing into a male <gametophyte>. Its production is distinct from the later production of <sperm>.

= Homospory
{parent=Spore}

= Homosporous
{synonym}

Production of one principal spore type, as in many <ferns>. The resulting <gametophytes> may produce both kinds of <gametes>, with their actual sexual development depending on the species and conditions.

= Sporopollenin
{parent=Spore}

A resistant wall material in <spores> and <pollen>. It helps protect dispersal stages without eliminating the environmental requirements of later development.

= Spore dispersal
{parent=Spore}

Movement of <spores> away from their source. Resistant walls and elevated release can improve dispersal without guaranteeing germination or successful <fertilization> in the next generation.

= Alternation of generations
{parent=Sexual reproduction}
{title2=$n\leftrightarrow2n$}

Alternation between a multicellular <haploid> <gametophyte> and a multicellular <diploid> <sporophyte>. <meiosis> produces <spores>, whereas <fertilization> restores the diploid condition; the generations grow by <mitosis>.

= Sporophyte
{parent=Alternation of generations}

The generation developing from a <zygote> and producing <spores> through <meiosis>. In the usual land-plant cycle it is <diploid>. Its nutritional independence and stature differ markedly between <bryophytes> and <vascular plants>.

= Sporangium
{parent=Sporophyte}

= Sporangia
{synonym}

A structure in which <spores> are produced. One unbranched bryophyte <sporophyte> bears a single sporangium; branching sporophytes can distribute several sporangia on one body.

= Microsporangium
{parent=Sporangium}

= Microsporangia
{synonym}

A spore-producing structure associated with the male developmental lineage in <heterospory>.

= Gamete
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

A gamete is a reproductive <biological cell> that can fuse with another <gamete> during <fertilization>. In the ordinary <diploid> inheritance model, a <haploid> <gamete> carries one copy at each <genetic locus>.

= Ovum
{parent=Gamete}

An <ovum> is a female <gamete>.

= Sperm
{parent=Gamete}
{wiki}

A male <gamete>.

= Fertilization
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

<Fertilization> is fusion of <gametes>. Fusion of two <haploid> <gametes> restores the <diploid> state in the inheritance model used for <Mendelian segregation>.

= Double fertilization
{parent=Fertilization}
{wiki}

In the common <angiosperm> arrangement, one <sperm> fuses with the <ovum> to form a <diploid> <zygote>; another fuses with the central <biological cell>'s two polar <cell nuclei>, initiating <endosperm> with three <chromosome> sets. The <zygote> develops into a <plant embryo>, and the <ovule> develops into a <seed>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/32.2 Pollination and Fertilization>[Biology 2e, §32.2 Double Fertilization, Figure 32.18]{ref}

For an original counting example, stipulate a <plant> with $2n=14$ <chromosomes> and the common two-polar-nucleus arrangement. Each <haploid> <sperm> and <ovum> contributes $n=7$. The <zygote> therefore has $7+7=14$ <chromosomes>, while the initiating <endosperm> <cell nucleus> has $7+7+7=21$. The maternal <seed coat> remains derived from $2n=14$ <plant tissue>; the whole <seed> has no single uniform <chromosome> count. This is bookkeeping for a stipulated example, not identification of a particular <species>. It follows by counting the <chromosome> sets entering each fusion, rather than assigning one count to everything enclosed by a <seed coat>.

= Zygote
{parent=Fertilization}
{wiki}

The <biological cell> formed by fusion of <gametes>.

= Pollination
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

In <angiosperms>, <pollination> transfers <pollen> from an <anther> to a <stigma (botany)>. <Fertilization> is a later fusion of <gametes>; <seed> development follows. <Pollination> may use animals, wind or water, so an insect visit is neither required for every <flower> nor sufficient evidence of <fertilization>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/32.2 Pollination and Fertilization>[Biology 2e, §32.2 opening definition, Pollination by Wind and Pollination by Water]{ref}

= Self-incompatibility
{parent=Pollination}

A recognition system preventing some self or genetically incompatible pollinations from completing fertilization. Its presence and mechanism must be tested rather than inferred from bisexual floral structure.

= Cross-pollination
{parent=Pollination}

Transfer of <pollen> between different individuals of the same <species>; compare <self-pollination>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/32.2 Pollination and Fertilization>[Biology 2e, §32.2 opening paragraphs: self-pollination and cross-pollination]{ref}

A <flower> can contain both reproductive groups and still receive <pollen> from another individual. Conversely, transfer between two <flowers> on one <plant> does not by itself imply <cross-pollination>. Following the donor and recipient individuals makes <pollination> useful for studying <genetics>: organ position alone cannot identify whose <alleles> meet at <fertilization>.

= Self-pollination
{parent=Pollination}
{wiki}

Transfer of <pollen> within one <plant>, including between its <flowers>.

= Pollen
{parent=Pollination}

An <angiosperm> <pollen> grain is a small male <gametophyte>, not itself a <seed> or a single <gamete>. Its tube-forming <biological cell> supports delivery of <sperm>.<Observing the growth of pollen tubes (Practical Biology)>[Teaching notes: pollen grain and tube as male gametophyte]{ref}

= Pollen analysis
{parent=Pollen}

Use of identified pollen in sediments or other material to reconstruct vegetation and environmental change. Production, transport and preservation affect the relationship between counts and vegetation.

= Pollen tube
{parent=Pollen}
{wiki}

A growing extension from a <pollen> grain. In a compatible <flower>, the <pollen tube> provides a route for <sperm> to the <angiosperm female gametophyte>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/32.2 Pollination and Fertilization>[Biology 2e, §32.2 Double Fertilization, Figure 32.18]{ref}

= Pollen tube germination assay
{parent=Pollen tube}

A <pollen tube germination assay> makes tube emergence visible outside a <flower>. The published Practical Biology experiment, <Observing the growth of pollen tubes (Practical Biology)>, Technical note 3, lists florist <lilies> among successful materials without specifying a <species>. Record the actual identification; success with one sample cannot establish a universal growth rate.<Observing the growth of pollen tubes (Practical Biology)>[Technical note 3 and Teaching notes: material dependence and contorted versus straight tubes]{ref}

Pearson's <Core practical 4: Investigate the effect of sucrose concentration on pollen tube growth by Pearson Education Ltd>, Teacher sheet, pp. 1–2, describes an inspectable experiment. Mix equal volumes of <sucrose> solution and mineral-<salt> medium on clean cavity slides. Dust in <pollen> from mature <anthers> with a mounted needle, record the addition time, and incubate without coverslips in covered dishes containing wet filter paper. The moist chamber reduces drying; the uncovered drops avoid oxygen limitation. Brief observations with an <optical microscope> at $100\times$, using an <eyepiece graticule> calibrated against a <stage micrometer>, reveal tube emergence and length. Return slides to the chambers and switch off the lamp between observations to reduce heating and drying. For comparisons of <sucrose> <concentration>, use the same <flower> across treatments to control maturity, then repeat with other <flowers>.<Core practical 4: Investigate the effect of sucrose concentration on pollen tube growth by Pearson Education Ltd>[Teacher sheet, pp. 1–2, steps 3–8 and answer 1; Technician sheet, p. 1, equipment and plant materials]{ref}

\Image[pollen-tube-assay-and-fertilization-evidence.png]
{title=What a <pollen tube germination assay> observes, and what remains to reach <fertilization>}
{description=Original apparatus and evidence schematic. A drop contains <pollen> with and without emerged <pollen tubes>; these are cartoons, not measured tube lengths or experimental success proportions. The separate lower route shows the additional stages inside a compatible <flower>. No time or length scale is implied.}

An additional proposed <experimental design> check is a medium-only slide: it tests whether debris is being mistaken for <pollen tubes>. Compare matched <pollen> samples at different final <sucrose> <concentrations>, keeping <temperature>, elapsed time and observation rules fixed. A sample known to produce <pollen tubes> in a previously successful medium would test whether an apparent failure comes from handling or the tested condition. These controls are questions to test, not guaranteed outcomes. A negative result cannot distinguish dead <pollen> from an unsuitable medium without further comparisons.

Two original calculations clarify the readout. First, take $0.40\,\mathrm{mL}$ of $0.80\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <sucrose> solution and $0.40\,\mathrm{mL}$ of <sucrose>-free medium. Assuming additive <volumes>, conservation of the <amount of substance> gives
$$
c_{\mathrm{final}}=\frac{c_{\mathrm{stock}}V_{\mathrm{stock}}}{V_{\mathrm{stock}}+V_{\mathrm{medium}}}=0.40\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}.
$$
This <dilution> also halves the medium's original component <concentrations>. Label the final <molar concentration>, not the starting bottle.

Second, stipulate a toy field containing $40$ <pollen> grains, of which $12$ have emerged <pollen tubes>. Its emergence fraction is $12/40=0.30$. If those twelve <pollen tubes> have total measured length $960\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, their <arithmetic mean> length is $80\,\mathrm{\mu m}$. Assigning length zero to the other grains instead gives $960/40=24\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ per grain. These invented numbers show why emergence fraction and mean length among emerged tubes answer different questions. Specify the denominator and the emergence criterion before counting, and avoid selecting only conspicuous long <pollen tubes>.

Tube emergence in a drop demonstrates growth under that medium's conditions. It observes no <stigma (botany)> interaction, passage through a <style (botany)>, delivery into an <ovule>, <gamete> fusion, or <seed> development. Thus a positive <pollen tube germination assay> does not demonstrate successful <fertilization> in a living <flower>. Its beauty is making one otherwise hidden part of <sexual reproduction> directly observable.<Observing the growth of pollen tubes (Practical Biology)>[Teaching notes: steps between pollen germination and seed formation; prepared style slides as a separate observation]{ref}

= Oogenesis
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

<Oogenesis> is development of female <gametes>; the unequal divisions of human <meiosis> produce <polar bodies> alongside the developing <ovum>.

= Nurse cell
{parent=Oogenesis}

A <germline>-associated <cell> supplying macromolecules to an <oocyte> in systems such as <Drosophila>. Maternal <RNA> synthesis and its eventual localization are distinct processes.

= Follicle cell
{parent=Oogenesis}

A somatic cell surrounding an <oocyte>. In <Drosophila melanogaster>, ovarian follicle cells interpret oocyte signals and contribute to eggshell pattern and later embryonic positional information.

= Polar body
{parent=Oogenesis}

A <polar body> is a small product of an unequal division during <oogenesis>.

= Oocyte
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

An <oocyte> is a female germ <biological cell> developing toward an <ovum>.

\Include[genetics]{parent=Biology}

= Gametophyte
{parent=Sexual reproduction}
{wiki}

The <plant> life-cycle generation that produces <gametes>. In an <angiosperm>, male <gametophytes> are <pollen> grains and the female <gametophyte> is the <embryo sac>.

= Prothallus
{parent=Gametophyte}

A name commonly used for a fern <gametophyte>. A small photosynthetic free-living body is a useful example, but not a description of every fern gametophyte.

= Archegonium
{parent=Gametophyte}

= Archegonia
{synonym}

An egg-bearing reproductive structure in relevant land-plant lineages. The young <sporophyte> can be retained on the maternal <gametophyte> after <fertilization>.

= Antheridium
{parent=Gametophyte}

= Antheridia
{synonym}

A male gamete-producing structure in relevant plant lineages. In <bryophytes> and many <ferns>, it produces motile <sperm> whose movement to an egg requires a suitable water connection.

= Pathogen
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= RNA splicing
{c}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Processing of a precursor <RNA> that removes an <intron> and joins the flanking <exons>. The <ribozyme> experiments show one self-splicing mechanism; <RNA splicing> need not always be autonomous.

= Splicing intermediate
{parent=RNA splicing}

A molecular species produced during a partially completed splicing reaction. Time-dependent accumulation can help distinguish an assembly defect from a later catalytic block.

= Exon definition
{parent=RNA splicing}

Coordinated recognition of the boundaries of an <exon>, helping specify splice-site pairing. Disrupting communication can repress <exon> use without directly occupying one essential site.

= Spliceosome
{parent=RNA splicing}

The dynamic RNA–protein machinery carrying out spliceosomal <intron> removal. Regulatory interference can occur during early assembly or later catalytic transitions.

= SPF45
{parent=RNA splicing}
{c}

A spliceosomal factor involved in substrate-specific 3′ splice-site recognition and progression through the second catalytic step. Interaction with <Sex-lethal> provides a mechanism of late splicing control.<Splicing regulation at the second catalytic step by Sex-lethal involves 3′ splice site recognition by SPF45 by María José Lallena and others>[Autoregulatory substrate experiments]{ref}

= U1 snRNP
{parent=RNA splicing}
{c}

A small nuclear ribonucleoprotein involved in 5′ splice-site recognition. Its association and productive communication with other sites are distinct steps.

= U2AF
{parent=RNA splicing}
{c}

An auxiliary splicing factor system involved in recognition of the <polypyrimidine tract> and 3′ splice-site region. Competition with its recruitment can alter use of alternative acceptors.

= Exon junction complex
{parent=RNA splicing}

A multiprotein assembly associated with spliced <RNA> that can communicate processing history to later <RNA> regulation. A downstream complex can contribute to termination surveillance; it is not required for every form of <nonsense-mediated decay>.

= Polypyrimidine tract
{parent=RNA splicing}

A pyrimidine-rich precursor-<RNA> segment involved in recognition near many spliceosomal $3'$ <splice sites>.

= RNA splicing branch point
{parent=RNA splicing}
{c}

The precursor-<RNA> branch-site position whose reactive group initiates the first transesterification in spliceosomal <RNA splicing>.

= Splice site
{parent=RNA splicing}

A precursor-<RNA> position used as a boundary in <RNA splicing>. Selecting different donor or acceptor sites changes which sequences remain in the mature product.

= Splice junction
{parent=RNA splicing}

The junction between segments joined during <RNA splicing>. A read crossing an <exon>–<exon> junction can distinguish a processed transcript from the corresponding unspliced precursor.

= Alternative splicing
{parent=RNA splicing}

Production of different mature <RNA> sequences from the same precursor by changing which <splice sites> or <exons> are used. A cassette <exon> can be included in one <transcript isoform> and skipped in another.

= Intron retention
{parent=Alternative splicing}

Persistence of an intronic sequence in a transcript that would otherwise have been removed by <RNA splicing>. It can change coding potential, localization or transcript survival.

= Percent spliced in
{parent=Alternative splicing}
{title2=$\psi$}

The inclusion fraction of an alternative <exon> or event. With effective included and skipped molecule abundances $I$ and $S$, $\psi=I/(I+S)$; expressing this fraction as a percentage gives $100\psi$. Raw junction counts can require opportunity or length correction before substitution.

= Splicing code
{parent=Alternative splicing}

A predictive set of rules connecting <RNA> sequence features and cellular regulators to <alternative splicing> outcomes. A useful code predicts the extent of <exon> inclusion in a specified <cell type>, rather than merely listing enriched motifs.

= Intron
{parent=RNA splicing}
{wiki}

A segment represented in a precursor <RNA> that is removed by <RNA splicing>; the corresponding segment of a <gene> is also called an <intron>. An <intron> can interrupt <ribosomal RNA> as well as <messenger RNA>.

= Self-splicing intron
{parent=Intron}

An intron capable of catalysing its removal through RNA-mediated reactions, although proteins can assist folding and activity in cells.

= Group II intron
{parent=Self-splicing intron}

A class of self-splicing introns with mechanistic and structural relationships to spliceosomal splicing. Some have mobile forms; this does not imply that an ordinary spliceosomal intron retains an autonomous mobile system.

= Exon shuffling
{parent=Intron}

Evolutionary reassortment of exon-associated coding segments. Introns can provide boundaries permitting changes in protein-domain arrangement, but the role of a particular intron requires historical evidence.

= Group I catalytic intron
{parent=Intron}
{wiki}

= Group I intron
{synonym}

A class of <introns> with a conserved <RNA> catalytic core and a two-step <RNA splicing> mechanism initiated by external <guanosine>. Some <group I introns> self-splice under suitable conditions; others require <protein> assistance. The <ribozyme> body explains the Tetrahymena example.

= Exon
{parent=RNA splicing}
{wiki}

A segment retained in the mature <RNA> after <RNA splicing>, or the corresponding segment of a <gene>. An <exon> need not encode a <protein>: the <ribosomal RNA> <exons> in the Tetrahymena <ribozyme> experiment are an example.

= Microexon
{parent=Exon}

A very short <exon>. Specialized alignment and junction validation can be needed to detect its inclusion; the exact length cutoff should be stated for a given analysis.

= Guanosine
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <nucleoside> comprising <guanine> attached to <ribose>. Its free $3'$ <hydroxyl group> initiates <group I intron> <RNA splicing>; <guanosine> is incorporated into the excised <intron>, rather than acting as a regenerated <catalyst>.

= Gel electrophoresis
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Separation of charged <molecules> through a gel in an <electric field>. Denaturing conditions suppress folded structures, helping compare <RNA> lengths; matching migration alone does not establish an identical <nucleotide sequence> or identical <covalent bonds>. In the <ribozyme> experiments, labelled <RNA> was detected after separation by exposing photographic film.

In <DNA> <agarose gel electrophoresis>, the backbone <phosphate> groups carry negative <electric charge> under the usual mildly alkaline <buffer solution> conditions. <DNA> therefore moves toward the positive <anode>, away from the negative <cathode>.<Agarose Gel Electrophoresis by Addgene>[Introduction; Loading Samples and Running an Agarose Gel, step 6: DNA migration toward the positive electrode]{ref}

The <electric field> points the other way: the <electric force> $\mathbf F=q\mathbf E$ reverses direction when $q<0$. The apparatus is driven by an external source of <electric potential difference>; the positive <anode> convention here belongs to that operating mode. Direction follows <electric charge>, so it is not a rule that all <molecules> move toward the same <electrode>.

The useful result is a spatial comparison: a mixture becomes separated <gel bands>, which can be compared with a <DNA ladder> or recovered for subsequent analysis. <Agarose gel electrophoresis> of linear double-stranded <DNA>, <gel electrophoresis> of denatured <RNA>, and <SDS-PAGE> of <proteins> use different conditions to make migration informative about chain size. An <electric field> supplies motion; the gel supplies a porous obstacle course.

\Image[gel-electrophoresis-polarity-and-agarose-sieving.png]
{title=<DNA> migrates toward the positive <anode>, opposite to the <electric field>; the <agarose> mesh separates linear <DNA> fragments. Conceptual apparatus and mesh, not measured trajectories or a drawing to scale}

= Polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

Electrophoretic separation in a polyacrylamide matrix. Native conditions can preserve molecular complexes, whereas denaturing conditions address different separation goals.

= Electrophoretic mobility shift assay
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

A binding assay that detects a change in electrophoretic mobility when a ligand associates with a labelled target, such as an <RNA>–<protein> complex. A concentration series and competition controls help distinguish specific binding from aggregation or non-specific retention.

= Gel band
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

A <gel band> is a localized population of stained or otherwise detected <molecules> with sufficiently similar migration in one sample lane. One visible <gel band> need not contain only one <nucleotide sequence>. Two different linear <DNA> fragments of 375 <base pairs> can occupy the same unresolved <gel band>; equal migration supplies size evidence under suitable conditions, not a sequence determination.

<Fluorescence> from a bound stain can support an estimate of <DNA> quantity, but suitable staining and detector response matter.<Five Main Nucleic Acid Gel Electrophoresis Steps by Thermo Fisher Scientific>[Step 4, Sample visualization, Fluorescent dyes: relation between fluorescence and bound nucleic acid]{ref}

For a quantitative model with equal staining response per unit <mass>, background-subtracted, integrated <fluorescence> $I$ satisfies $I=km$. This requires an unsaturated detector and a common proportionality constant $k$; visual peak brightness alone does not supply $I$.

The <amount of substance> is $n=m/M$, where $M$ is <molar mass>. For double-stranded <DNA> with approximately equal average <mass> per <base pair>, $M$ is proportional to length $L$, so $n$ is proportional to $I/L$. In a constructed example, equal integrated signals from 250-<base-pair> and 1000-<base-pair> <gel bands> imply approximately equal <masses> but four times as many short <DNA> <molecules>. Conversely, equal numbers of those <DNA> <molecules> would give about four times the signal for the longer fragments in this model. Neither conclusion follows from darkness in a schematic drawing.

= Agarose gel electrophoresis
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

<Agarose gel electrophoresis> uses a porous <agarose> <polymer> network. For linear double-stranded <DNA> within a suitable separation range, shorter fragments generally move farther in a fixed run. Raising the <agarose> concentration changes the mesh and the range of sizes that can be resolved.<Five Main Nucleic Acid Gel Electrophoresis Steps by Thermo Fisher Scientific>[Step 1, Selection and gel preparation; Recommended percentage of agarose gels for separation of DNA fragments]{ref} A compact circular <DNA> <molecule> need not migrate like linear <DNA> of the same length: geometry affects passage through the mesh.<Five Considerations for the Nucleic Acid Gel Electrophoresis Process by Thermo Fisher Scientific>[Effects of conformation, Figure 1: nicked circular, linear and supercoiled forms of the same DNA]{ref}

A concrete laboratory example is Addgene's documented <DNA> separation protocol. Its standard 1% gel contains 1 g of <agarose> in 100 mL of <TAE buffer>. Dissolved <agarose> sets in a casting tray around a comb; removing the comb leaves sample wells. The gel is covered with the same <buffer solution> in a box connected to a voltage source. Loading buffer contains <glycerol> to increase sample <density> and a tracking dye to show run progress. A <DNA ladder> occupies a separate lane beside the samples. With wells near the negative <cathode>, <DNA> migrates toward the positive <anode>. After separation, bound stain makes the <DNA> visible by <fluorescence> under a <UV transilluminator>. The <DNA ladder> supplies the size reference; the tracking dye monitors migration but does not identify sample <DNA>.<Agarose Gel Electrophoresis by Addgene>[Equipment; Pouring a Standard 1% Agarose Gel, steps 1–7; Loading Samples and Running an Agarose Gel, steps 1–9; Analyzing Your Gel]{ref}

In the manufacturer's controlled comparison, the same <DNA ladder> runs in 1%, 2%, and 3% <agarose> gels with the same run time and other stated conditions. The changed <gel band> spacing shows why a calibration belongs to its particular gel conditions, rather than establishing a universal distance law.<Five Main Nucleic Acid Gel Electrophoresis Steps by Thermo Fisher Scientific>[Step 1, Figure 3 and caption: same DNA ladder at three agarose percentages under the same conditions, including run time]{ref}

In interpreting an unknown sample, a blank lane tests for detectable background or carryover in that blank, while the <DNA ladder> checks migration and detection of its known fragments. An empty sample lane cannot by itself distinguish absent <DNA> from unsuccessful loading or insufficient detection. These controls answer different questions; neither makes matching migration a proof of matching <nucleotide sequence>.

= DNA ladder
{c}
{parent=Agarose gel electrophoresis}

A <DNA ladder> contains <DNA> fragments of known lengths in <base pairs>. Its <gel bands> supply size references. Match the sample's strandedness and shape, and use the specified lengths for that <DNA ladder>. A size reference need not provide a quantitative <mass> standard.<Five Main Nucleic Acid Gel Electrophoresis Steps by Thermo Fisher Scientific>[Step 2, Nucleic acid ladder selection guidelines: fragment structure, conformation, intended use and manufacturer-specific composition]{ref}

= Gel electrophoresis size calibration
{parent=Agarose gel electrophoresis}
{title2=$d=a-b\log_{10}(L/L_0)$}

A <gel electrophoresis size calibration> relates migration distance to known fragment lengths on the same gel. Here is an explicitly chosen model for linear double-stranded <DNA>, not measured data or a universal law. Let $L$ be the numerical length in <base pairs> and $d$ the numerical migration distance in millimetres from the wells:
$$
d=55-12\log_{10}(L/100).
$$
The <logarithm> has a dimensionless argument: $L/100$ is the length divided by 100 <base pairs>. The chosen <DNA ladder> fragments $L=100,250,500,1000$ give $d=55.00,50.22,46.61,43.00$, respectively. The <gel bands> therefore appear in the reverse order of length along the direction of migration.

\Image[gel-electrophoresis-ladder-calibration.png]
{title=Constructed <DNA ladder>, unknown, mixture and blank lanes with a <logarithm> calibration. All positions follow the chosen model; the unknown is 375 <base pairs> at 48.11 mm, and darkness does not represent <DNA> quantity}

To invert the calibration, rearrange the <logarithm>:
$$
L=100\,10^{(55-d)/12}.
$$
Our chosen unknown $L=375$ gives $d=48.1116248\ldots$, between the 250-<base-pair> and 500-<base-pair> references. Inserting that distance into the inverse recovers 375; retaining only $d=48.11$ gives about $375.12$ <base pairs>. The extra digits reflect rounding in this model, not the resolving power of a real gel.

For <linear interpolation> between those two reference <gel bands>, use the <logarithm> of length. Here the fraction of the distance from the 500-<base-pair> reference toward the 250-<base-pair> reference is $t=\log_{10}(500/375)/\log_{10}2\approx0.4150$, giving $L=500(250/500)^t=375$. Direct <linear interpolation> of lengths instead gives approximately $396.24$ <base pairs>. This difference explains why the choice of calibration coordinates matters. A real calibration must be checked over its usable range; folded <RNA> or circular <DNA> does not automatically share the linear double-stranded <DNA> calibration.

= UV transilluminator
{c}
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

A <UV transilluminator> supplies <ultraviolet> illumination for viewing stained <DNA> by <fluorescence> after <gel electrophoresis>.

= SDS-PAGE
{c}
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}
{wiki}

= Sodium dodecyl sulfate polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis
{synonym}

An analytical form of <gel electrophoresis> using an anionic <surfactant> and a <polymer> gel to separate <proteins> predominantly by chain size under denaturing conditions. <Western blot separation> often uses <SDS-PAGE>; the resulting apparent <mass> and subsequent <antibody> binding provide complementary, incomplete evidence about identity.

= Starch gel electrophoresis
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

<Starch gel electrophoresis> uses a starch-based gel to separate material in an <electric field>. The <IgG chain reduction experiment> uses it to compare <polypeptide chains>.

= Denaturing polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}

<Gel electrophoresis> through polyacrylamide under conditions that suppress <nucleic acid> folding; suitable sequencing conditions resolve adjacent <nucleotide> lengths.

= ELISA
{c}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay
{synonym}

\Include[physiology]{parent=Biology}

= Medicine
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Infectious disease
{parent=Medicine}

Disease caused by an infectious agent. Infection, detectable exposure and clinically damaging disease are distinct outcomes.

= Immune-related adverse event
{parent=Medicine}

Damage to normal tissues associated with therapeutic immune activation, including inflammatory complications of <immune checkpoint blockade>.

= Lymphodepletion
{parent=Medicine}

Reduction of a patient's lymphocyte populations, sometimes used to prepare for adoptive cell therapy. Its effects and toxicities are part of the full treatment regimen.

= Progression-free survival
{parent=Medicine}

Time from a defined starting point until disease progression or death under a specified clinical-trial definition. It differs from overall survival and response rate.

= Extravasation
{parent=Medicine}

Exit of circulating <tumour> cells through a vascular boundary into tissue, one step in a multistage metastatic process.

= Intravasation
{parent=Medicine}

Entry of <tumour> cells from tissue into a blood or lymphatic vessel, distinct from later survival or growth at a distant site.

= Premetastatic niche
{parent=Medicine}

A tissue environment altered before arrival of malignant cells in a way that can favour subsequent metastatic establishment. Conditioning must be distinguished from effects of <tumour> cells already present.

= Tumour microenvironment
{parent=Medicine}

The non-malignant cells, extracellular material, signals and physical conditions surrounding and interacting with a <tumour>. Its components can support or restrain progression depending on their state and context.

= Metastasis
{parent=Medicine}

= Metastases
{synonym}

The establishment of malignant growth at a site distant from the original <tumour>, requiring successful dissemination, survival and expansion. Circulating <tumour> cells alone do not establish that a <metastasis> has formed.

= Experimental metastasis assay
{parent=Metastasis}

An assay introducing <tumour> <biological cells> at a selected point of dissemination, for example by vascular injection. Its interpretation must state which preceding barriers were bypassed.

= Metastatic cascade
{parent=Metastasis}

A sequence of conditional transitions from a primary <tumour> to a distant growing lesion. A bypass assay tests only selected transitions, and failure at one stage need not identify the slowest step in every setting.

= Tumour dormancy
{parent=Metastasis}

Persistence without substantial net expansion under specified conditions. Cellular quiescence and a balance between proliferation and death are different possible mechanisms.

= Circulating tumour cell
{parent=Metastasis}

A <cancer> <biological cell> detected in circulation. Detection measures one part of dissemination rather than total metastatic success.

= Disseminated tumour cell
{parent=Metastasis}

A <cancer> <biological cell> found away from the primary site. Its presence does not prove that it will generate a clinically significant distant colony.

= Metastatic colonization
{parent=Metastasis}

Establishment and sustained expansion at a distant site. A disseminated <biological cell> can survive or remain dormant without completing this stage.

= Carcinoma
{parent=Medicine}

A malignant <tumour> of epithelial origin. Its cells can change phenotype without ceasing to belong to their original malignant lineage.

= Tumour
{parent=Medicine}

An abnormal tissue mass. The term includes benign as well as malignant growths, so <tumour> and <cancer> are not always interchangeable.

= Oncology
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

= Cancer
{parent=Oncology}
{wiki}

= Chronic lymphocytic leukaemia
{parent=Cancer}

A mature B-cell malignancy characterized by accumulation of a clonal population. Its initiation, indolent expansion and progression are distinct biological endpoints.

= Tumour latency
{parent=Cancer}

The interval between a defined starting event and detection of <tumour> development under a specified assay. Detection threshold and competing causes of death affect interpretation.

= Tumour maintenance
{parent=Cancer}

Continued survival or growth of established malignant disease. A <gene> required before initiation need not remain necessary after additional alterations accumulate.

= Autochthonous tumour model
{parent=Cancer}

A model in which <tumours> arise within the relevant host tissue rather than being introduced as an established graft. Defined engineered initiation still differs from a full human exposure history.

= Humanized mouse
{parent=Cancer}

A mouse modified to contain selected human biological components, such as immune <biological cells>. Humanization is partial and does not automatically reproduce all human immune interactions.

= Syngeneic tumour model
{parent=Cancer}

A tumour-graft model using a genetically compatible host and donor, enabling investigation in an immune-competent setting. It generally uses mouse rather than human <tumour> <biological cells>.

= Genetically engineered mouse model
{parent=Cancer}

A mouse model in which defined genetic changes are introduced to test disease mechanisms. Tissue, timing, expression level and <genetic background> constrain its relationship to human <cancer>.

= Immune evasion
{parent=Cancer}

Avoidance or suppression of immune-mediated elimination. Normal immune-regulatory mechanisms can be repurposed in a <tumour> context.

= Tumour clonality
{parent=Cancer}

The organization of a <tumour> into related lineages sharing inherited changes. A clonal alteration need not be a causal driver because passengers can hitchhike during expansion.

= Tumour purity
{parent=Cancer}

The proportion of a sampled <tumour> preparation contributed by malignant <biological cells>. It affects interpretation of copy number, variant fractions and expression measurements.

= Cancer dependency
{parent=Cancer}

A requirement for a particular activity in maintenance of a <cancer> under specified conditions. A dependency is not automatically an initiating driver or a selective therapeutic target.

= Oncogene addiction
{parent=Cancer dependency}

Strong dependence of an established <tumour> on an oncogenic activity. Dependence can change as the <tumour> acquires cooperating or bypass alterations.

= Chronic myeloid leukaemia
{parent=Cancer}

A myeloid malignancy classically associated with the BCR-ABL fusion. Dependence on a defined kinase driver provides a mechanistic basis for targeted inhibition.

= Cancer invasion
{parent=Cancer}

<Cancer invasion> is penetration of cancer cells into surrounding tissue across the normal boundaries of their compartment. It is distinct from <cell proliferation> and from completion of distant metastatic colonization.

= Collective cancer invasion
{parent=Cancer invasion}

= Collective invasion
{synonym}

Invasion by connected or coordinated groups of <cancer> <biological cells> rather than only isolated migrants. Leader and follower <biological cells> can perform different tasks.<Collective invasion in breast cancer requires a conserved basal epithelial program by Kevin J. Cheung and others>[Functional leader-cell experiments]{ref}

= Invadopodium
{parent=Cancer invasion}

= Invadopodia
{synonym}

An actin-rich cellular protrusion associated with localized <extracellular matrix> degradation during invasion. Formation, matrix digestion and whole-cell invasion are related but distinct experimental endpoints.

= Pharmacology
{parent=Medicine}

= Off-target drug effect
{parent=Pharmacology}

A drug effect mediated through an interaction other than the intended target. A residual phenotype after verified protection of the intended target is a useful candidate, but requires independent causal validation.

= Drug hypersensitivity
{parent=Pharmacology}

An increased response to a drug relative to a specified control. Cellular hypersensitivity does not uniquely imply higher molecular affinity for the intended target.

= Disc diffusion assay
{parent=Pharmacology}

= Halo assay
{synonym}

A growth-inhibition assay in which a compound diffuses from a disc into a lawn of <biological cells>. Halo size combines susceptibility with <molecular diffusion> and growth conditions and is not directly a molecular <binding affinity>.

= Pharmacokinetics
{parent=Pharmacology}

= Target engagement
{parent=Pharmacology}

Evidence that an intervention interacts with its intended molecular target in the biological setting being tested. A downstream phenotype alone is insufficient.

= Therapeutic window
{parent=Pharmacology}

A range of exposures giving useful benefit while keeping adverse effects acceptable. Biochemical target inhibition alone does not establish this range.

= Ipilimumab
{parent=Pharmacology}

A therapeutic antibody directed against <CTLA-4>, used as an immune checkpoint inhibitor in defined clinical contexts.

= Chemoresistance
{parent=Pharmacology}

Reduced susceptibility of cells or disease to a chemotherapy regimen. Survival can reflect pre-existing resistant states, induced changes or genetic evolution.

= Ulixertinib
{parent=Pharmacology}

A small-molecule inhibitor of <ERK> used experimentally to test pathway requirements. Developmental interpretation requires stage, dose, target-engagement and viability controls.

= Pharmacologist
{parent=Pharmacology}

= List of pharmacologists
{parent=Pharmacologist}

= Colin W. Taylor
{c}
{parent=List of pharmacologists}

Homepage: https://www.phar.cam.ac.uk/research/taylor

= Work by Colin W. Taylor
{parent=Colin W. Taylor}

= Stephen C. Tovey
{c}
{parent=List of pharmacologists}

Publication record: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20980441/

= Work by Stephen C. Tovey
{parent=Stephen C. Tovey}

= Infection
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

An <infection> involves invasion and multiplication of an infectious agent in a <host>. <Bacteria> are one possible cause; an agent capable of causing <disease> is a <pathogen>. The National Cancer Institute defines infection through invasion and growth in the body. https://www.cancer.gov/publications/dictionaries/cancer-terms/def/infection[NCI Dictionary: infection]{ref}.

In a <SIR model>, acquiring <infection> moves a member of the <susceptible compartment> into the <infectious compartment>. That immediate transition is a model assumption: the <SEIR model> instead includes an exposed stage before infectiousness. Specify these states when translating a biological process into <compartmental models (epidemiology)>.

= Immunity
{disambiguate=medical}
{parent=Medicine}

= Immunity
{synonym}

In infection modelling, <immunity> is protection against infection or disease, with its strength and duration represented by the chosen <host> states. A <SIR model> has a permanently protected recovered state, whereas a <SIS model> has no lasting post-recovery protection.

= Complement system
{parent=Immunity (medical)}
{wiki}

The <complement system> comprises immune <proteins>; its components provide an interaction partner for <Fc fragments>.

= Isolation
{disambiguate=health care}
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

<Isolation (health care)> separates recognized infected or ill individuals from susceptible contacts to limit transmission. <Quarantine> instead concerns exposed individuals who may or may not be infected.

= Psychoactive drug
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

A psychoactive drug is a substance that changes mental activity, mood or perception. In population <causal inference>, its per-user adverse-event risk and its total population harm are distinct: the latter also depends on how many people use it, for how long, and on substitution between substances.

= Mephedrone
{parent=Psychoactive drug}
{wiki}

Mephedrone is 4-methylmethcathinone, a synthetic stimulant <psychoactive drug>. The chemical identity is described in https://www.euda.europa.eu/publications/drug-profiles/synthetic-cathinones_en[the EUDA synthetic-cathinone profile]. In <causal inference>, an association between increased use and fewer deaths involving another drug does not itself establish substitution: changes in use, population composition and ascertainment must be distinguished.

= MDMA
{c}
{parent=Psychoactive drug}
{wiki}

= Ecstasy
{synonym}

MDMA is 3,4-methylenedioxymethamphetamine, a <psychoactive drug> commonly called ecstasy. A product's street label does not establish its measured chemical contents. Epidemiological comparisons should distinguish single-substance outcomes from outcomes attributed to several drugs.

= Cocaine
{parent=Psychoactive drug}
{wiki}

Cocaine is a stimulant <psychoactive drug>. Comparisons of its population harm require exposure denominators and consistent definitions of drug-related outcomes, rather than death counts alone.

= Informed consent
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

Informed consent is voluntary agreement after receiving and understanding relevant information about an intervention or participation in research. In a <clinical trial>, the participant is informed about the study procedures, potential benefits and risks, alternatives and the option to decline or withdraw. Consent precedes <randomization>.

= Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

= HSCT
{synonym}
{c}

= Haematopoietic stem cell transplantation
{synonym}

= Neurology
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

= Neuroimaging
{parent=Neurology}
{wiki}

= Magnetoencephalography
{parent=Neuroimaging}
{title2=MEG}
{wiki}

= Electroencephalography
{parent=Neuroimaging}
{title2=EEG}
{wiki}

= Migraine
{parent=Neurology}
{wiki}

Migraine is a neurological disorder that can be an outcome in a <clinical trial> or an <observational study>. Its statistical association with an <exposure> does not alone establish a <causal effect>.

= Disease
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

= Hepatic steatosis
{parent=Disease}

= American foulbrood
{parent=Disease}
{c}

A bacterial <disease> of honeybee brood caused by <Paenibacillus larvae>. Colony-level <disease> outcomes are distinct from an individual worker’s immune activity.

= Rasopathy
{parent=Disease}
{c}

A developmental syndrome associated with dysregulation of <Ras>-controlled signaling. A broad phosphoprotein pattern alone does not establish diagnosis or identify a causal mutation.

= Diabetes
{parent=Disease}
{wiki}

= Type 1 diabetes
{parent=Diabetes}
{wiki}

= Type 2 diabetes
{parent=Diabetes}
{wiki}

= Insulin resistance
{parent=Diabetes}
{wiki}

A reduced biological response to <insulin>; it is a property of response, not simply a low <insulin> <concentration>. The <glucose clamp technique> helps separate these quantities.

= Prion disease
{parent=Disease}

A prion disease is associated with self-propagating abnormal folding of a <protein>. Its epidemiological interpretation requires distinguishing exposure, infection, clinical onset and diagnostic detection.

= Variant Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease
{parent=Prion disease}

= vCJD
{c}
{synonym}

A human <prion disease> associated with exposure to the <BSE> agent. Exposure totals and clinical-onset totals measure different stages of the disease process; <incubation periods> and ascertainment affect their relationship.

= Scrapie
{parent=Prion disease}

A <prion disease> of sheep and goats. A <scrapie> test result and a <BSE> diagnosis are distinct outcomes, even when the clinical appearances overlap.

= Bovine spongiform encephalopathy
{parent=Prion disease}

= BSE
{c}
{synonym}

A <prion disease> of cattle. A positive surveillance test is an observed diagnostic outcome, whose frequency need not equal true infection <prevalence> unless test performance and sampling are accounted for.

= Myelodysplastic syndrome
{parent=Disease}
{wiki}

= MDS
{synonym}
{c}

= Chronic disease
{parent=Disease}

A chronic disease persists over an extended period. A <continuous-time multi-state model> can represent its progression through severity states, but long duration alone does not imply irreversible progression or justify ignoring state misclassification.

= Immunology
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

= Immune-competent host
{parent=Immunology}

= Immune response
{parent=Immunology}

Cellular and molecular changes following recognition of a challenge. Increased activation can reflect increased exposure or damage, so its magnitude is not interchangeable with protective capacity.

= Innate immunity
{parent=Immunology}

= Adaptive immunity
{parent=Immunology}

= Inflammation
{parent=Immunology}

= Immune challenge
{parent=Immunology}

Experimental manipulation intended to stimulate an <immune response>. A nonreplicating inflammatory stimulus separates some host responses from effects of a live <pathogen>, but need not reproduce natural <infection>.

= Disease tolerance
{parent=Immunology}

Reduction of health or fitness damage for a given <pathogen> burden. Low symptoms alone cannot distinguish tolerance from successful <disease resistance>.

= Disease resistance
{parent=Immunology}

Reduction of <pathogen> burden through prevention or control of <infection>. It differs from <disease tolerance>, which reduces damage at a given burden.

= Psychoneuroimmunology
{parent=Immunology}

Study of interactions among <animal behaviour>, the <nervous system> and <immunity>. Bidirectional mechanisms require interventions or temporal evidence beyond a cross-sectional association.

= Social immunity
{parent=Immunology}

Collective protection against <pathogens> arising through interactions among individuals, including <hygienic behaviour>. A colony-level reduction in <infection> need not be increased cellular <immunity> in every worker.

= MHC class II
{parent=Immunology}
{c}

An antigen-presentation system displaying peptides to appropriate CD4 T cells. Its usual source and processing route differ from the intracellular-protein sampling pathway of MHC class I.

= MHC class I
{parent=Immunology}
{c}

A major histocompatibility complex presentation system displaying intracellularly derived peptides to appropriate <T cells>. Presentation and direct CAR recognition have different requirements.

= T-cell exhaustion
{parent=Immunology}
{c}

An altered T-cell functional and regulatory state associated with persistent stimulation. Blocking one inhibitory receptor need not reverse every component of the state.

= HLA
{parent=Immunology}
{c}

Human leukocyte antigen, the human MHC system. Particular <HLA> molecules restrict which peptides a conventional T-cell receptor can recognise.

= Cancer vaccine
{parent=Immunology}

An intervention intended to generate or strengthen immune recognition of tumour-associated targets. Successful priming does not guarantee entry or function within a <tumour>.

= Adoptive T-cell therapy
{parent=Immunology}

Transfer of prepared <T cells> to a patient to supply or amplify a therapeutic immune response, including expanded tumour-derived or genetically engineered populations.

= Immune checkpoint blockade
{parent=Immunology}

Treatment that interferes with inhibitory immune regulatory interactions, such as <PD-1>/<PD-L1> or CTLA-4-associated restraint. It requires a usable antitumour recognition and effector system.

= Antigen presentation
{parent=Immunology}

Display of antigen-derived material for immune recognition, including peptide display by MHC molecules to <T-cell receptors>. Processing, expression and recognition are distinct requirements.

= Cross-presentation
{parent=Antigen presentation}

Presentation of externally acquired antigen on MHC class I. Dendritic cells can thereby prime antiviral CD8 responses without themselves being productively infected.

= MHC restriction
{parent=Antigen presentation}
{c}

Dependence of a T-cell response on both a recognized peptide and a compatible presenting MHC molecule. Infected matched and mismatched targets distinguish it from recognition of viral infection alone.

= Tapasin
{parent=Antigen presentation}

A component of the MHC-class-I peptide-loading machinery. It helps connect peptide supply and selection to stable class-I display.

= TAP
{parent=Antigen presentation}
{c}

Transporter associated with antigen processing, supplying peptides to the endoplasmic reticulum for MHC-class-I loading. Peptide-pulsing experiments can bypass this transport step.

{parent=Immunology}
