= Biology laboratory technique
{parent=Biology}

= Cell ablation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Selective destruction of <cells> to test their necessity or the response of surrounding tissues. Compensation and collateral injury complicate interpretation; ablation alone is not a record of ancestry.

= Molecular marker
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

A measured molecular feature used to identify a <cell state>, tissue or experimental condition. Marker loss can reflect altered expression without loss of the <cells> themselves.

= Protein staining
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

= Coomassie staining
{parent=Protein staining}
{c}

= Densitometry
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

= Southern blot
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{c}

Detection of selected <DNA> fragments after separation and transfer to a <cell membrane>, using a complementary probe. A virus-specific probe distinguishes viral from abundant host <DNA> in a mixed sample.

= In situ hybridization
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Localization of a nucleic acid sequence through hybridization of a labelled complementary probe. Controls for probe specificity, background and tissue preservation are required.

= Pulse-chase experiment
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Labeling a cohort briefly and following its fate during an unlabeled chase; useful for separating transport kinetics from steady-state localization.

= Orthotopic transplantation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Placement of a graft in the corresponding anatomical organ or site. It preserves more relevant local context than some ectopic grafts but still bypasses natural <tumour> initiation.

= Tn5 transposase
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{c}

A bacterial DNA-transposition enzyme adapted for adapter insertion in <ATAC-seq>. Accessibility and sequence-dependent insertion bias both influence recovered fragments.

= Nascent RNA measurement
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Measurement of newly synthesized <RNA> to distinguish early transcriptional changes from accumulated <RNA> abundance. Short-interval perturbations help prioritize direct regulatory responses.

= Reporter assay
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

An assay in which a measurable output is linked to a chosen regulatory sequence or activity. Reporter <RNA> abundance, <biological translation> and <protein> stability can affect the same endpoint differently.

= Oligonucleotide probe
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

= Fluorescence in situ hybridization
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

= Biosensor
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

A measurement system that converts a biological or biochemical interaction into an observable signal.

= Redox biosensor
{parent=Biosensor}

A sensor designed to report an oxidation-reduction-sensitive variable. Localization, calibration, dynamic range and sensitivity to other conditions determine what its signal can establish.

= Chromatin immunoprecipitation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Enrichment of chromatin associated with a selected <protein>, followed by measurement of the recovered <DNA>. Association with a fragment need not mean direct base-specific binding; sequential enrichment can test co-association while retaining those resolution limits.

= Chromatin immunoprecipitation sequencing
{parent=Chromatin immunoprecipitation}

= ChIP-seq
{synonym}
{c}

Sequencing recovered <DNA> to map <chromatin> associated with a <protein> or modification. <antibody> specificity, fragment resolution and normalization constrain interpretation.

= Bradford assay
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{c}

A dye-binding estimate of <protein> concentration using a calibration series and absorbance near $595\,\mathrm{nm}$. Protein-dependent dye response, buffer interference and blank treatment limit how directly a <bovine serum albumin> standard estimates another <protein>.

= Intravital microscopy
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Microscopy of cells and tissues within a living organism, allowing direct observation of behaviour in a physiological setting subject to preparation and imaging constraints.

= Xenograft
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

A graft transferred between species. Tumour xenografts test behaviour in the recipient setting, which may lack immune interactions present in the original host.

= Patient-derived xenograft
{parent=Xenograft}

= PDX
{synonym}
{c}

A graft derived from a patient <tumour> and propagated in another species. Engraftment and serial passage can select lineages and alter the representation of the original sample.

= Immunoprecipitation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

= Single-molecule fluorescence in situ hybridization
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Detection of individual <RNA> molecules by multiple sequence-complementary fluorescent probes. Probe specificity, molecule detection and compartment boundaries require validation before interpreting subcellular enrichment.

= Sequential fluorescence in situ hybridization
{parent=Single-molecule fluorescence in situ hybridization}

= seqFISH
{synonym}
{c}

= seqFISH+
{parent=Sequential fluorescence in situ hybridization}
{c}

= Multiplexed error-robust fluorescence in situ hybridization
{parent=Single-molecule fluorescence in situ hybridization}

= MERFISH
{synonym}
{c}

= Ribosome profiling
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Sequencing of <RNA> fragments protected by <ribosomes> to measure their positions on transcripts. Occupancy measures a combination of entry, passage and dwell time; more footprints can reflect slower elongation rather than more completed <protein>.

= Initiation-site profiling
{parent=Ribosome profiling}

Methods that preferentially capture <ribosomes> near <biological translation> starts, often using short inhibitor treatments. Candidate peaks require calibration and independent validation.

= Three-nucleotide periodicity
{parent=Ribosome profiling}

Frame-related enrichment at a three-base interval in correctly positioned translating-ribosome reads. It supports <biological translation> but is difficult to establish for very short or low-count <reading frames>.

= Ribosome-protected fragment
{parent=Ribosome profiling}

An <RNA> fragment protected from digestion by a <ribosome> in the assayed preparation. Length, genomic position and frame periodicity help distinguish productive <biological translation> from other protected <RNA>.

= Translation complex profiling
{parent=Ribosome profiling}

= TCP-seq
{synonym}
{c}

Profiling of preserved ribosomal and small-subunit-associated complexes to distinguish stages including scanning and recycling. Captured distributions remain subject to crosslinking and fragment-selection biases.

= Metabolic RNA labelling
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Incorporation of a traceable precursor into newly synthesized <RNA>. A pulse and chase separates labelled-cohort survival from continuing synthesis, provided incorporation, chase efficiency, toxicity and reutilization are controlled.

= RNA structure probing
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{c}

Experimental inference of <RNA secondary structure> or accessibility from chemical or enzymatic reactivity. Changes in reactivity can also reflect <protein> shielding, so comparisons and orthogonal controls are necessary.

= RNA immunoprecipitation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{c}

Recovery of an <RNA>-associated complex using an <antibody> against a chosen <protein>. The associated <RNA> need not contact that <protein> directly, especially when partners remain intact during isolation.

= RIP-seq
{parent=RNA immunoprecipitation}
{c}

= Minigene splicing reporter
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

An engineered transcript containing a selected <RNA splicing> region in a controlled expression context. Mutating a candidate <cis-regulatory element> tests local function, but an isolated construct can omit distant regulation or native <chromatin> context.

= Crosslinking and immunoprecipitation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Mapping close <RNA>–<protein> contacts by crosslinking, <immunoprecipitation> of a selected <protein> and analysis of attached <RNA> fragments. Input abundance, <antibody> specificity and crosslinking bias constrain the interpretation of an apparent binding peak.

= CLIP-seq
{parent=Crosslinking and immunoprecipitation}
{c}

= HITS-CLIP
{synonym}
{c}

= PAR-CLIP
{parent=CLIP-seq}
{c}

= eCLIP
{parent=CLIP-seq}
{c}

= iCLIP
{parent=CLIP-seq}
{c}

= Cell transplantation
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}

Transfer of identified cells into a recipient environment to test survival, developmental potential and interactions. Appropriate graft controls distinguish environmental influence from injury.

= Pulse-chase analysis
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{wiki}

<Pulse-chase analysis> marks a cohort during a short pulse, then follows it while unlabeled precursor reduces subsequent labeling during the chase. Label specificity and effective suppression of new incorporation constrain interpretation.

= Label-retaining cell
{parent=Pulse-chase analysis}

A cell retaining a dilution-sensitive label after a chase. Retention can reflect slow division, <terminal differentiation> or assay properties; it is not by itself a functional stem-cell test.

= Autoradiography
{parent=Biology laboratory technique}
{wiki}

<Autoradiography> localizes a radioactive label through its effect on a recording medium. In <electron microscopy>, developed photographic grains are compared with specimen structures; their positions have finite localization precision.

= Virus
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Viral infection
{parent=Virus}

= Viral latency
{parent=Viral infection}

Persistence of a <virus> in a restricted programme distinct from productive particle formation. Latency does not mean complete molecular inactivity.

= Viral capsid
{parent=Virus}

= Capsid
{synonym}

A <protein> shell enclosing a viral <genome>. Protection from <nuclease> is consistent with enclosure but does not alone demonstrate infectivity.

= Squirrelpox
{parent=Virus}

A poxviral disease important in some <red squirrel> declines. Differential susceptibility and field exposure both matter when attributing population change. <Parapoxvirus causes a deleterious disease in red squirrels associated with UK population declines by Daniel M. Tompkins and others>[Proceedings of the Royal Society B 269, pp. 529–533; contrasting experimental susceptibility of red and grey squirrels]{ref}

= Provirus
{parent=Virus}

A <provirus> is a viral DNA form integrated into a cellular genome. Its location and regulatory sequences can influence both viral expression and neighbouring cellular genes.

= Retroviral life cycle
{parent=Virus}

The <retroviral life cycle> includes entry, RNA-to-DNA copying by reverse transcriptase, a DNA intermediate and gene expression followed by particle production. Integration as a provirus can make the genetic change persistent across cell divisions.

= Rous sarcoma virus
{c}
{parent=Virus}

<Rous sarcoma virus> is a transforming avian retrovirus used to dissect the relationship between viral replication, src-dependent transformation and cellular growth-control genes.

= Retrovirus
{parent=Virus}

A <retrovirus> replicates through reverse transcription of its RNA into DNA, followed by a DNA stage that can integrate into the host genome. Viral gene expression can then use cellular transcription machinery.

= Dengue virus
{parent=Virus}
{title2=DENV}

<Dengue virus> exposure can be investigated through <antibodies> in serum, but antibody detection must be distinguished from identification of current infection. Cross-reactivity with antibodies to other related viruses can limit interpretation.<Serologic Tests for Dengue Virus by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention>[IgG Tests]{ref}

= Sendai virus
{c}
{parent=Virus}
{wiki}

= Bacteriophage
{parent=Virus}
{wiki}

A <bacteriophage> is a <virus> that infects <bacteria>. The <Luria-Delbrück experiment> uses a <bacteriophage> challenge to reveal resistant <biological colonies>.

= Phi X 174
{c}
{parent=Bacteriophage}

= Entomology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Entomology> studies <insects>.

= Entomologist
{parent=Entomology}

An <entomologist> studies <entomology>.

= List of entomologists
{parent=Entomologist}

= Mir Subhan Mulla
{c}
{parent=List of entomologists}

= Mir S. Mulla
{c}
{synonym}

= M. S. Mulla
{c}
{synonym}

= Mir Mulla
{c}
{synonym}

Identity and discipline: https://entomology.ucr.edu/news/2023/02/09/entomologists-remember-distinguished-professor-emeritus-dr-mir-s-mulla

= Work by Mir Subhan Mulla
{parent=Mir Subhan Mulla}

= Microinjection
{parent=Biology}

<Microinjection> introduces material through a fine needle into a <biological cell> or one of its compartments.

= Anatomy
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Ligament
{parent=Anatomy}

A connective-tissue structure joining skeletal components and constraining motion at a <joint>.

= Gill
{parent=Anatomy}

A respiratory organ exposing a vascular exchange surface to water.

= Appendicular skeleton
{parent=Anatomy}

The skeleton of appendages and their supporting girdles.

= Axial skeleton
{parent=Anatomy}

The <skull> and vertebral-axis skeleton, contrasted with the girdles and appendages of the <appendicular skeleton>.

= Urostyle
{parent=Axial skeleton}

The posterior rod of the adult <frog> skeleton formed from caudal vertebral and associated developmental components. It replaces a freely jointed adult tail and participates in force transmission.

= Sacrum
{parent=Axial skeleton}

The vertebral region transmitting forces between the vertebral column and pelvic apparatus.

= Sacroiliac joint
{parent=Sacrum}

The articulation between the sacral apparatus and <ilium>. Its geometry affects transmission of <hindlimb> forces and pelvic motion.

= Meckel's cartilage
{parent=Anatomy}

An embryonic cartilaginous component of the lower-jaw region. Its persistence or removal informs models of the evolutionary jaw–ear transition.

= Epipubic bone
{parent=Anatomy}

A bone projecting anteriorly from the pelvis in several mammalian lineages. Its presence in stem <eutherians> must be distinguished from the crown placental condition.

= Nasal turbinate
{parent=Anatomy}

A nasal structure whose functions can include conditioning respiratory air or supporting olfactory tissue. Anatomical identification and function require care in <fossils>.

= Semicircular canal
{parent=Anatomy}

An inner-ear structure involved in sensing angular motion. Its dimensions are influenced by body size, movement and fluid mechanics.

= Endolymph
{parent=Anatomy}

Fluid within the membranous inner ear. Temperature-dependent fluid properties motivate biomechanical proxies for extinct-animal physiology.

= Dermal skeleton
{parent=Anatomy}

Skeletal tissues formed in a dermal association. The name distinguishes their developmental location from the internal cartilaginous skeleton; it does not imply that every component is external to the body.

= Gastralium
{parent=Dermal skeleton}

= Gastralia
{synonym}

A ventral dermal skeletal element relevant to comparisons of early <turtle> body plans.

= Fin
{parent=Anatomy}

= Median fin
{parent=Fin}

An unpaired <fin> on the dorsal or ventral midline, including dorsal, anal and caudal appendages.

= Paired fin
{parent=Fin}

A member of a bilateral appendage pair. Pectoral and <pelvic fins> have distinct positions and need not have originated simultaneously.

= Median-fin co-option hypothesis
{parent=Paired fin}

The proposal that developmental mechanisms first operating in <median fins> were redeployed in paired appendages. Shared mechanisms do not by themselves specify the anatomical precursor or migration route.

= Lateral fin-fold hypothesis
{parent=Paired fin}

The proposal that discrete <paired fins> evolved through differentiation and regional restriction of an earlier continuous ventrolateral appendage field.

= Gill-arch hypothesis of paired fin origins
{parent=Paired fin}

= Archipterygium hypothesis
{synonym}

The proposal that paired appendages evolved through modification of branchial structures. Evidence for a pharyngeal contribution to the shoulder is distinguishable from evidence that the whole <fin> arose by conversion of a <gill> arch.

= Pelvic fin
{parent=Paired fin}

A paired more posterior <fin> associated with the <pelvic girdle>.

= Pectoral fin
{parent=Paired fin}

A paired anterior <fin> associated with the <pectoral girdle>.

= Fin ray
{parent=Fin}

= Eye
{parent=Anatomy}

= Anapsid
{parent=Anatomy}

A temporal <skull> condition lacking the characteristic temporal openings. It can be ancestral or secondarily acquired, so its occurrence does not establish one exclusive lineage.

= Temporal emargination
{parent=Anatomy}

A marginal excavation of the temporal <skull> region. It is not automatically homologous to a fully bounded <temporal fenestra>.

= Temporal fenestra
{parent=Anatomy}

An opening behind the orbit in the <skull>. Homology depends on position and surrounding bones; a notch opening to a <skull> margin is a <temporal emargination>.

= Feather
{parent=Anatomy}

An integumentary structure whose distribution among living and <fossil> <dinosaurs> informs <bird> origins and functional change. Filamentous coverings and specialized flight <feathers> should not be treated as one invariant form.

= Odontode
{parent=Anatomy}

A small mineralized skeletal unit relevant to comparisons between dermal coverings and oral dentitions. Tissue composition and position must be treated as distinct characters.

= Paired appendage
{parent=Anatomy}

A pectoral or pelvic appendage belonging to a paired <vertebrate> system. Comparative organization connects <fin> and <limb> histories while allowing extensive specialization.

= Vertebrate limb
{parent=Paired appendage}

= Limb
{synonym}

An appendage with an inherited digit-bearing organization, which can be modified or lost in descendant lineages.

= Hindlimb
{parent=Vertebrate limb}

= Foot
{parent=Hindlimb}

= Knee
{parent=Hindlimb}

= Hip
{parent=Hindlimb}

= Anatomical digit
{parent=Paired appendage}

A distal <finger> or toe and its corresponding skeletal organization. Positional assignment, morphology and developmental identity must be distinguished when comparing reduced hands.

= Finger
{parent=Anatomical digit}

A <anatomical digit> of the <forelimb>, distinguished from a toe of the <hindlimb>.

= Thumb
{parent=Finger}

= Digit identity
{parent=Anatomical digit}

The morphological and developmental character identity assigned to a <anatomical digit>. It is not necessarily equivalent to its ordinal position among the surviving <anatomical digits> of a reduced <limb>.

= Digit identity specification
{parent=Digit identity}

Developmental assignment of position-associated anatomical and molecular character to a digit. A change in digit number or growth is not automatically a homeotic change of identity.

= Polydactyly
{parent=Anatomical digit}

A condition with additional <anatomical digits> relative to the relevant standard pattern. In early digit-bearing <vertebrates>, counts above five show that the modern pentadactyl ground plan cannot simply be projected onto the earliest <limbs>.

= Endoskeleton
{parent=Anatomy}

Internal supporting skeletal structures. In paired appendages, the identities and articulations of elements provide stronger comparative information than the mere presence of mineralized tissue.

= Anatomical joint
{parent=Endoskeleton}

= Pelvic girdle
{parent=Endoskeleton}

= Pectoral girdle
{parent=Endoskeleton}

= Suspensory locomotion
{parent=Anatomy}

Movement while hanging below a support. It is not synonymous with an upright trunk or with all forms of arboreal climbing.

= Wrist
{parent=Anatomy}

The joint region linking the forearm and hand. Similar gross posture can involve different ranges of motion and load-bearing anatomy.

= Claw
{parent=Anatomy}

A pointed keratinous terminal structure on a digit, used in functions including purchase, digging and prey handling.

= Tongue
{parent=Anatomy}

A muscular oral structure involved in functions including food handling and acquisition.

= Flipper
{parent=Anatomy}

A flattened locomotor appendage specialized for movement in water. Similar functions have arisen in distinct vertebrate lineages.

= Nostril
{parent=Anatomy}

An external opening of the nasal airway. Position relative to the rest of the skull is distinct from the number of openings.

= Vertebral column
{parent=Anatomy}

The serial axial skeletal structure composed of vertebrae. Regional proportions and joint surfaces constrain locomotor reconstructions.

= Pelvis
{parent=Anatomy}

The skeletal structure connecting the hindlimb apparatus to the axial skeleton in the ancestral tetrapod arrangement. Reduction of this connection is informative in whale evolution.

= Skull
{parent=Anatomy}

The bony framework of the head. Comparisons must distinguish deformation after death from biological differences.

= Basipterygoid process
{parent=Skull}

A skull-base projection that contacts the palatal apparatus. Its size and articulation contribute to constraints on <cranial kinesis>.

= Cranial kinesis
{parent=Skull}

Movement between parts of the <skull> beyond ordinary lower-jaw rotation. <joint> surfaces, soft tissues, <muscles> and constraints elsewhere in the <skull> jointly determine realized movement.

= Palate
{parent=Skull}

The roof of the mouth and its supporting <anatomy>. In avian comparisons, contacts among the <vomer>, <palatine bone>, <pterygoid bone> and braincase are important for <cranial kinesis>.

= Neognathous palate
{parent=Palate}

An avian palatal arrangement with more mobile pterygoid–palatine articulation and reduced bracing compared with the <palaeognathous palate>. Mobility varies among species and cannot be read from the group name alone.

= Palaeognathous palate
{parent=Palate}

An avian palatal arrangement with extensive bracing among the <vomer>, <pterygoid bone>, <palatine bone> and <skull> base. It is associated with <Palaeognathae>; the name does not establish evolutionary primitiveness.

= Orbit (anatomy)
{parent=Skull}

The <eye> socket, distinct from a <temporal fenestra> behind it.

= Braincase
{parent=Skull}

The region of the skull enclosing and protecting the brain.

= Jaw
{parent=Anatomy}

An oral apparatus involved in acquiring and processing food. Its bony articulations and associated muscles can change function during evolution.

= Mastication
{parent=Anatomy}

Mechanical processing of food by repeated jaw and tooth movements. It changes food particle size and surface area before subsequent digestion.

= Orthogrady
{parent=Anatomy}

An upright orientation of the trunk. It is not synonymous with suspension, knuckle-walking or habitual terrestrial <bipedalism>.

= Bipedalism
{parent=Anatomy}

Locomotion using two limbs for support and propulsion. Occasional bipedal movement must be distinguished from habitual or obligate bipedal locomotion.

= Knuckle-walking
{parent=Anatomy}

Locomotion with weight supported on the dorsal surfaces of flexed fingers. Similar external support posture need not imply identical wrist mechanics or a unique evolutionary origin.

= Hair
{parent=Anatomy}

A keratinous epidermal appendage of <mammals>. Its poor preservation in many fossils limits direct reconstruction of its early history.

= Auditory bulla
{parent=Anatomy}

A bony enclosure associated with the middle-ear region. Its construction can supply diagnostic phylogenetic characters.

= Involucrum
{parent=Auditory bulla}

The thickened medial portion of the cetacean <auditory bulla>, important in recognizing early whales before a fully aquatic skeleton evolved.

= Astragalus
{parent=Anatomy}

An ankle bone also called the talus. The detailed double-pulley construction in early whales helps test their artiodactyl ancestry.

= Ectotympanic
{parent=Anatomy}

A bony component supporting the mammalian tympanic membrane, evolutionarily related to the <angular bone>.

= Incus
{parent=Anatomy}

A mammalian middle-ear ossicle homologous to the <quadrate>.

= Malleus
{parent=Anatomy}

A mammalian middle-ear ossicle homologous to the <articular>.

= Angular bone
{parent=Anatomy}

An ancestral lower-jaw bone contributing to the evolutionary origin of the mammalian <ectotympanic>.

= Squamosal
{parent=Anatomy}

A skull bone participating in the mammalian <dentary>–<squamosal> jaw articulation.

= Quadrate
{parent=Anatomy}

A skull bone forming the upper component of the ancestral jaw joint, homologous to the mammalian <incus>.

= Articular
{parent=Anatomy}

A bone forming the ancestral lower component of the <articular>–<quadrate> jaw joint, homologous to the mammalian <malleus>.

= Dentary
{parent=Anatomy}

The principal tooth-bearing lower-jaw bone of <mammals>; enlargement and changes in articulation are informative in early mammalian evolution.

= Secondary palate
{parent=Anatomy}

A partition separating nasal and oral passages. A bony secondary palate forms part of the mammalian feeding and respiratory apparatus.

= Tooth
{parent=Anatomy}

= Teeth
{synonym}

A mineralized oral structure involved in food acquisition or processing. <dental occlusion>, replacement pattern and tissue distribution can be more informative than gross shape alone.

= Dental crown
{parent=Tooth}

= Odontoblast
{parent=Tooth}

A specialized cell producing <dentine>. Its tissue contribution differs from that of an <ameloblast>.

= Dentine
{parent=Tooth}

A mineralized dental tissue formed by <odontoblasts>. It differs developmentally and structurally from the outer <enamel> coating of a typical <human> <tooth> crown.

= Enamel
{parent=Tooth}

A highly mineralized epithelial product formed by <ameloblasts>, distinct from <dentine>. <fossil> tissue distributions and enamel-matrix <gene expression> support an ancestry in dermal coverings deeper than its familiar deployment on <teeth>.<New genomic and fossil data illuminate the origin of enamel by Qingming Qu and others>[Dermal distribution and inferred expansion into the mouth]{ref}

= Enamel rod
{parent=Enamel}

An organized structural unit of mammalian crown <enamel>. Its architecture is a derived tissue organization, distinct from the deeper origin of the <enamel> tissue itself.

= Dentition
{parent=Tooth}

The arrangement and complement of teeth, including their classes, positions and replacement pattern.

= Tooth enamel
{parent=Tooth}

A highly mineralized dental tissue. Its distribution and wear relative to other tissues help maintain functional tooth surfaces.

= Incisor
{parent=Tooth}

An anterior tooth class specialized in diverse ways for cropping, cutting or gnawing.

= Molar
{parent=Tooth}

A posterior tooth class important in many forms of food processing. Molar form varies with lineage, position and wear.

= Premolar
{parent=Tooth}

A tooth class lying in front of the molars in the typical heterodont mammalian arrangement.

= Tribospheny
{parent=Tooth}

= Tribosphenic molar
{synonym}

An occlusal construction combining shearing and crushing, with the lower molar talonid basin interacting with an upper cusp. Similar functional geometry does not alone settle whether all examples share one evolutionary origin.

= Dental mesowear
{parent=Tooth}

Assessment of macroscopic wear-related cusp shape and relief. It integrates a different temporal scale of food processing from <dental microwear>.

= Dental microwear
{parent=Tooth}

Small wear features on tooth surfaces used to infer food processing near the end of the wear record. Diet, external grit, preparation and post-mortem damage must be distinguished.

= Hypselodonty
{parent=Tooth}

Continued growth of a tooth, distinguished from a tall but finite reserve crown in <hypsodonty>.

= Hypsodonty
{parent=Tooth}

A high-crowned tooth construction supplying a reserve against wear. It differs from <hypselodonty>, and is not by itself proof of a grass diet.

= Selenodonty
{parent=Tooth}

A dental construction with crescentic ridges, characteristic of many <ruminants>.

= Lophodonty
{parent=Tooth}

A dental construction in which cusps are connected into ridges. Ridge arrangement and wear influence the cutting and grinding performance of the surface.

= Carnassial
{parent=Tooth}

A specialized shearing tooth. In <Carnivora> the principal opposing pair is the upper fourth premolar and lower first molar; other predatory lineages can specialize different teeth.

= Diphyodonty
{parent=Tooth}

A replacement pattern with two successive generations for the replaced tooth classes. It should not be taken to mean every tooth in the jaw is replaced exactly once.

= Dental occlusion
{parent=Tooth}

The relationship and contact of opposing <teeth> during jaw closure and chewing. Precise correspondence of opposing surfaces permits specialized cutting and grinding.

= Insect cuticle
{parent=Anatomy}

A protective extracellular surface layer. Embryonic cuticle patterns in insects can report developmental identities but require complementary molecular readouts.

= Histology
{parent=Anatomy}
{wiki}

= Tissue
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Histology}
{wiki}

= Biological tissue
{synonym}

= Connective tissue
{parent=Tissue (biology)}

= Muscle
{parent=Tissue (biology)}

= Myomere
{parent=Muscle}

One of a repeated series of axial <muscle> units, useful in comparing early <chordate> organization.

= Fibrosis
{parent=Tissue (biology)}

<fibrosis> is persistent accumulation and remodeling of extracellular material in tissue. In mechanobiology, altered matrix architecture and resistance can change the forces experienced by resident cells.

= Epithelium
{parent=Tissue (biology)}

A tissue forming a lining or covering. Its normal organization and differentiation state matter when comparing epithelial tumors with adjacent tissue.

= Endothelium
{parent=Epithelium}

The cellular lining of blood and lymphatic vessels. Its polarized surfaces and junctions participate in vascular <lumen formation>.

= Stratified epithelium
{parent=Epithelium}

An <epithelium> organized into multiple cell layers. Cell production, differentiation and loss must be balanced across the layers.

= Squamous epithelium
{parent=Epithelium}

An <epithelium> characterized by flattened surface cells. Stratified examples contain renewing basal populations and differentiating suprabasal layers.

= Suprabasal differentiation
{parent=Squamous epithelium}

Progression away from the basal compartment toward specialized surface layers. Differentiation, delamination and subsequent cell loss need not occur simultaneously.

= Cell extrusion
{parent=Epithelium}

Removal of a cell from an epithelial sheet through coordinated behaviour of that cell and its neighbours. Extrusion and apoptosis are distinct events and need not occur in the same order.

= Ameloblast
{parent=Epithelium}

An epithelial cell producing <enamel> during <tooth> development. Loss of the enamel-producing cellular layer after eruption limits replacement of worn crown <enamel>.

= Bone
{parent=Tissue (biology)}

A mineralised connective tissue forming major components of the vertebrate skeleton.

= Parietal bone
{parent=Bone}

= Frontal bone
{parent=Bone}

= Ossification
{parent=Bone}

Formation of <bone>. Dermal, perichondral and endochondral modes should be distinguished from mineral deposition in <cartilage>.

= Frontoparietal bone
{parent=Bone}

A skull-roof <bone> formed by integration of frontal and parietal components, characteristic of <frogs> and some other <vertebrates>.

= Rib
{parent=Bone}

A skeletal element associated with the vertebral axis. Reduction of free <ribs> is part of the characteristic <frog> body organization.

= Maxilla
{parent=Bone}

= Premaxilla
{parent=Bone}

= Perichondral bone
{parent=Bone}

<bone> deposited around a cartilaginous skeletal element, distinct from internal <endochondral bone>.

= Endochondral bone
{parent=Bone}

<bone> deposited during replacement of a cartilaginous skeletal template. It should be distinguished from <calcified cartilage> and a superficial <perichondral bone> layer.

= Dermal bone
{parent=Bone}

<bone> forming within dermal or related <connective tissues> rather than replacing a cartilaginous template. Large skull-roof <bones> and small surface <odontodes> are distinct anatomical organizations.

= Macromeric dermal skeleton
{parent=Dermal bone}

A dermal skeleton organized into relatively large plates or <bones>, contrasting with a covering dominated by small separate scales or <odontodes>.

= Fibulare
{parent=Bone}

A proximal ankle element paired with the <tibiale>.

= Tibiale
{parent=Bone}

A proximal ankle element that is elongated in the derived <frog> <hindlimb>.

= Tibiofibula
{parent=Bone}

The compound lower-leg element formed by union of the <tibia> and <fibula> in adult <frogs>.

= Radioulna
{parent=Bone}

The compound forearm element formed by union of the <radius bone> and <ulna> in adult <frogs>.

= Ilium
{parent=Bone}

The dorsal component of the <pelvic girdle>. Its anterior elongation is an important feature of the frog-lineage locomotor apparatus.

= Vertebra
{parent=Bone}

= Axis vertebra
{parent=Vertebra}

The second <vertebra>; its specialization varies across <vertebrate> lineages.

= Atlas vertebra
{parent=Vertebra}

The first <vertebra>, associated with the articulation of the head and axial column.

= Presacral vertebra
{parent=Vertebra}

A <vertebra> anterior to the <sacrum>. Counts depend on identifying the sacral articulation, not just numbering visible centra.

= Quadrate bone
{parent=Bone}

A <bone> of the <jaw> suspension in many nonmammalian <vertebrates>; its mobility contributes to avian <cranial kinesis>.

= Palatine bone
{parent=Bone}

A <bone> contributing to the <palate>.

= Pterygoid bone
{parent=Bone}

A palatal <bone> participating in the connection between the <palate> and posterior <skull> apparatus.

= Vomer
{parent=Bone}

A median palatal <bone> whose form and contacts contribute to comparisons of avian <cranial kinesis>.

= Fibrolamellar bone
{parent=Bone}

Bone tissue associated with rapid deposition. Rapid growth alone does not uniquely establish sustained <endothermy>.

= Phalanx
{parent=Bone}

= Phalanges
{synonym}

= Metacarpal
{parent=Bone}

= Fibula
{parent=Bone}

One of the principal lower-leg bones, paired with the <tibia>.

= Tibia
{parent=Bone}

One of the principal lower-leg bones, paired with the <fibula>.

= Femur
{parent=Bone}

The principal proximal bone of the lower <limb> and the corresponding pelvic appendage in relevant comparisons.

= Ulna
{parent=Bone}

One of the principal forearm bones, paired with the <radius bone>.

= Radius bone
{parent=Bone}

One of the two principal forearm bones; movement relative to the <ulna> contributes to forearm rotation.

= Humerus
{parent=Bone}

The single proximal element of the upper <limb> and the corresponding pectoral appendage in relevant <fossil> comparisons.

= Cartilage
{parent=Tissue (biology)}

A connective tissue with specialised extracellular matrix, providing support in structures including much of the developing vertebrate skeleton.

= Calcified cartilage
{parent=Cartilage}

<cartilage> with mineral deposited in its matrix. Mineralization does not convert its histological identity automatically into <bone>.

= Extracellular matrix
{parent=Tissue (biology)}
{wiki}

= Basement membrane
{parent=Extracellular matrix}
{wiki}

A <basement membrane> is a specialized extracellular layer associated with the basal surface of an epithelium and other tissue interfaces. Breaching it is a mechanically and biologically distinct step from moving across a culture surface.

= Matrix stiffness
{parent=Extracellular matrix}

<Matrix stiffness> describes resistance of an <extracellular matrix> to deformation under a specified loading geometry and timescale. It differs from pore size, ligand density and fibre alignment, although changing a matrix formulation can alter all of them.

= Antibiotic
{parent=Biology}

An <antibiotic> inhibits susceptible <microorganisms>.

= Antibiotic tolerance
{parent=Antibiotic}

Reduced killing under antibiotic exposure without a corresponding increase in the concentration inhibiting growth. It differs from growth-based <antibiotic resistance>.

= Bacterial persistence
{parent=Antibiotic tolerance}

Survival of a subpopulation during bactericidal exposure while the broader population remains susceptible. Regrown survivors can recover the original susceptibility; persistence is not synonymous with every persistent infection.

= Persister cell
{parent=Bacterial persistence}

= Minimum inhibitory concentration
{parent=Antibiotic}

The lowest tested antimicrobial concentration preventing defined visible growth under specified conditions. It measures an organism-level response, not just the catalytic efficiency of an isolated resistance <enzyme>.

= Ceftazidime
{parent=Antibiotic}

A <beta-lactam> antibiotic with bulky substituents whose susceptibility to hydrolysis depends on the particular <beta-lactamase>.

= Penicillin G
{parent=Antibiotic}

A <beta-lactam> antibiotic used as a substrate in comparisons of <beta-lactamase> kinetics.

= Antibiotic resistance
{parent=Antibiotic}

<Antibiotic resistance> is the ability of a <microorganism> to grow or survive under specified <antibiotic> exposure that inhibits a susceptible comparison.

= Kanamycin
{parent=Antibiotic}

An <antibiotic> used as a selection agent in the <1973 plasmid construction experiment>.

= Tetracycline
{parent=Antibiotic}

An <antibiotic> used as a selection agent in the <1973 plasmid construction experiment>.
